The structure of gauge invariant Gaussian quantum operations on finite Fermion systems
Eric A. Carlen

Abstract
. Let ℋ 1 {\mathcal{H}}_{1} be a finite dimensional complex Hilbert space. Let ψ ↦ Z ⁡ ( ψ ) \psi\mapsto Z(\psi) be a canonical anti-commutation relations (CAR) field over ℋ 1 {\mathcal{H}}_{1} acting irreducibly on a Hilbert space 𝒦 {\mathord{\mathscr{K}}} . The ∗ * -algebra 𝒜 ℋ 1 {\mathscr{A}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}} generated by the Z ⁡ ( ψ ) Z(\psi) , ψ ∈ ℋ 1 \psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1} , is simply all operators on 𝒦 {\mathscr{K}} . However, the CAR field endows 𝒜 ℋ 1 {\mathscr{A}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}} with additional structure, and we are concerned with quantum operations acting in harmony with this structure. In particular, there is a gauge automorphism group generated by “second quantizing” ψ ↦ e i ​ t ​ ψ \psi\mapsto e^{it}\psi . The fixed point algebra of the gauge group, 𝒢 ℋ 1 {\mathscr{G}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}} , is a sub-algebra of 𝒜 ℋ 1 {\mathscr{A}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}} studied by Araki and Wyss. It contains the density matrices of an important class of states, the gauge invariant Gaussian states , 𝔖 G ​ I ​ G {\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG} . Our focus is on semigroups { e t ​ ℒ } t ≥ 0 \{e^{t{\mathscr{L}}}\}_{t\geq 0} of quantum operations on 𝒜 ℋ 1 {\mathscr{A}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}} that map 𝔖 G ​ I ​ G {\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG} into itself. Each e t ​ ℒ e^{t{\mathscr{L}}} is one-to-one, and our first main result is a structure theorem for such quantum operations on 𝒢 ℋ 1 {\mathscr{G}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}} that map 𝔖 G ​ I ​ G {\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG} into itself. We apply this to study semigroups of quantum operations on 𝒢 ℋ 1 {\mathscr{G}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}} that map 𝔖 G ​ I ​ G {\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG} into itself. Our second main result is a structure theorem showing that they are parameterized by pairs ( G , A ) (G,A) where G G is a contraction semigroup generator on ℋ 1 {\mathcal{H}}_{1} , and 0 ≤ A ≤ − G − G ∗ 0\leq A\leq-G-G^{*} . We then show that each of these semigroups has a natural extension to the full CAR algebra 𝒜 ℋ 1 {\mathscr{A}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}} . Further results are obtained under further assumptions on the pair ( G , A ) (G,A) .



Keywords: Quantum operations, Fermions, Gaussian states

Subject Classification Numbers: 46L57, 81V74, 47C15

1.
Introduction

Len Gross pioneered many developments in the study of quantum operations, that is, completely positive trace preserving (CPTP) maps on Fermion systems His work, particularly the papers
[
35
,
36
]
, has inspired much work of my own. This work of Len Gross was motived by problems in quantum field theory. More recently, developments in quantum information theory and quantum computing have led to new questions about quantum operations on Fermion systems, already in the case of finitely many degrees of freedom. In this paper we answer some such questions and discuss others that remain open.

Systems of finitely many Fermionic degrees of freedom are specified by a finite dimensional complex Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, called the
single particle space
, and a conjugate linear map
Z
from
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{K}}})
, the bounded operators another Hilbert space
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
, satisfying

(1.1)

Z(\psi)Z^{*}(\phi)+Z^{*}(\phi)Z(\psi)=\langle\psi,\phi\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}{\mathds{1}}

together with

(1.2)

Z(\psi)Z(\phi)+Z(\phi)Z(\psi)=0\quad{\rm and}\quad Z^{*}(\psi)Z^{*}(\phi)+Z^{*}(\phi)Z^{*}(\psi)=0\ .

The equations (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) are known as the
canonical anti-commutation relations
(CAR) and the algebra generated the operators
Z(\psi)
and
their adjoints is known as the CAR algebra over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, denoted by
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. A CAR field is also known as a
representation
of the abstract CAR algebra which
can be constructed in the tensor algebra over

{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
; see e.g.
[
6
]
.

It is well-known that if the dimension of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
is
N
and no non-trivial subspace of
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
is invariant under all of the operators in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
(so that
Z
acts irreducibly on

{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
), then
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
is
2^{N}
-dimensional and
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}={\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{K}}})
. Thus, every CPTP map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{K}}})
is a CPTP map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, and the well-developed general theory of completely positive maps (see
[
51
]
, or in the finite dimensional case,
[
14
]
) is immediately applicable.

However,
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{K}}})
,
viewed from the perspective of a particular CAR field that generates it has physically significant structure built out of the CAR field, and we shall be interested in CPTP maps that
preserve aspects of this structure.

We now introduce this structure, and start by explaining the relation between any two CAR fields over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. The first construction of a CAR field over a finite dimensional
Hilbert space was given by Jordan and Wigner in 1928
[
40
]
, shortly after the beginning of modern quantum mechanics. Fock later gave another construction
[
32
]
. Both of these constructions have been reviewed many times; Dereziński
[
27
]
provides a clear and recent exposition of this and more.

In finite
dimensions, the relation between any two constructions is quite simple.
A classical theorem on the structure of Clifford algebras can be re-formulated (see
[
27
]
) to show that when
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
has finite dimension
N
, and
Z
and
\widetilde{Z}
are
two CAR fields over

{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
acting on irreducibly on Hilbert spaces
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
and
\widetilde{{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}}
respectively, then both
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
and
\widetilde{{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}}
have dimension
2^{N}
, and the fields
(representations) are unitarily equivalent in the sense that there exists
a unitary
{\mathcal{U}}:{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}\to\widetilde{{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}}
such that for all
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

\widetilde{Z}(\psi)={\mathcal{U}}Z(\psi){\mathcal{U}}^{*}\ .

Moreover, because
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
is a finite dimensional Hilbert space, any
*
-automorphism
\alpha
of
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{K}}})
, and hence of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is inner and
implemented by a unitary conjugation
[
11
]
. That is, there exists a unitary
{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}^{\phantom{*}}:{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}\to{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
such that for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

\alpha(A):={\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}^{\phantom{*}}A{\mathcal{U}}^{*}_{\alpha}\ ,

and the unitary
{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}
is uniquely determined by
\alpha
up to a phase.

Throughout the following discussion, we assume that there is given some CAR field
Z
over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
acting irreducibly on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
.
If
U
is any unitary on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, define

(1.3)

\alpha_{U}(Z(\psi)):=Z(U\psi)

for all
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. One readily checks that
\alpha_{U}(Z)
is a CAR field, and hence
\alpha_{U}
extends to a
*
-automorphism of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
implemented by a unitary

{\mathcal{U}}_{U}
, determined up to a phase.

A concrete construction of
{\mathcal{U}}_{U}
determines a preferred phase. The first step is to introduce the
number operator
.
Let
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{N}\}
be any orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Define the number operator
{\mathcal{N}}
by

(1.4)

{\mathcal{N}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{N}Z^{*}(\psi_{j})Z(\psi_{j})\ .

Using the CAR, one readily computes the commutators

(1.5)

[{\mathcal{N}},Z_{j}]=Z_{j}\quad{\rm and\ hence}\quad[{\mathcal{N}},Z_{j}^{*}]=-Z_{j}^{*}\ .

Evidently
{\mathcal{N}}
is positive semi-definite, and if
\Phi
is an eigenvector of
{\mathcal{N}}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
, is follows from (
1.14
) that
{\mathcal{N}}Z(\psi_{j})\Phi=(\lambda-1)Z(\psi_{j})\Phi
,
and then as is well known, it follows from this that the spectrum of
{\mathcal{N}}
is
\{0,1,\dots,N\}
. The eigenspaces for the eigenvalues
0
and
N
are one dimensional. Choose unit vectors
\Omega_{0}
and
\Omega_{1}
in the
0
-eigenspace and the
N
-eigenspace respectively. Define two states on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
by

\omega_{0}(A)=\langle\Omega_{0},A\Omega_{0}\rangle\quad{\rm and}\quad\omega_{1}(A)=\langle\Omega_{1},A\Omega_{1}\rangle\ .

These are the
vacuum state
and the
anti-vacuum states
respectively. While the vectors
\Omega_{0}
and
\Omega_{1}
, called the
vacuum vector
and the
anti-vacuum vector
are determined only up to a phase,
\omega_{0}
and
\omega_{1}
are uniquely determined by the CAR field
Z
.

For
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{N})\in\{0,1\}^{N}
, define
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
by

(1.6)

\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}:=(Z^{*}(\psi_{1}))^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots(Z^{*}(\psi_{N}))^{\alpha_{N}}\Omega_{0}\ .

Then it is easy to see, using the CAR, that
\{\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}}
is an orthonormal basis of
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
. Each
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is an eigenvector of each
Z^{*}(\psi_{j})Z(\psi_{j})
with

Z^{*}(\psi_{j})Z(\psi_{j})\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\alpha_{j}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ .

Thus,
\{Z^{*}(\psi_{j})Z(\psi_{j})\}
is a complete set of mutually commuting observables consisting of orthogonal projections. Physically,
Z^{*}(\psi_{j})Z(\psi_{j})
is the observable whose eigenvalue
1
corresponds to an observation that “the
j^{{\rm th}}
Fermionic mode is occupied”, and whose eigenvalue
0
corresponds to an observation that “the
j^{{\rm th}}
Fermionic mode is unoccupied”. Moreover, with
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|=\sum_{\char 17\relax=1}^{N}\alpha_{j}
,
{\mathcal{N}}\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}=|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
. This yields a spectral decomposition of
{\mathcal{N}}
:
For
n\in\{0,\dots,N\}
, define the orthogonal projections
E_{n}
by

(1.7)

E_{n}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ |{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|=n}|\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle\langle\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\quad{\rm so\ that}\quad{\mathcal{N}}=\sum_{n=0}^{N}nE_{n}\ .

Thus, a measurement of
{\mathcal{N}}
in the pure state
\Psi
yields
n
with probability
\langle\Psi,E_{n}\Psi\rangle
, in which case after the measurement the state is a
superposition of states in which exactly
n
of the Fermionic modes are occupied. It is for this reason that
{\mathcal{N}}
is called the number operator.

Returning to the automorphisms of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
induced by unitary operators on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, let
U
be any such unitary. Then
\{U\psi_{1},\dots,U\psi_{N}\}
is another orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.
Define

(1.8)

\widetilde{\Phi}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}:=(Z^{*}(U\psi_{1}))^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots(Z^{*}(U\psi_{N}))^{\alpha_{N}}\Omega_{0}\

for each
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
. Then
\{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}}
is another orthonormal basis of
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
. Define a unitary
{\mathcal{U}}_{U}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
by

(1.9)

{\mathcal{U}}_{U}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}

for each
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, noting that
{\mathcal{U}}_{U}
is independent of the choice of phase that specifies
\Omega_{0}
. It is easy to check, using the CAR, that for all
\phi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{U}}_{U}Z(\phi){\mathcal{U}}_{U}^{*}=Z(U\phi)
, so that

{\mathcal{U}}_{U}
implements that automorphism
\alpha_{U}
specified in (
1.3
). Note that the phase is such that
{\mathcal{U}}_{U}\Omega_{0}=\Omega_{0}
.

If
\{U_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is any one parameter group of unitaries on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, then
\alpha_{U_{t}}
is a one parameter group of

*
-automorphisms of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. This lifting of unitary groups acting on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
to unitary groups acting on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
is known as
second quantization
,
[
20
,
59
]
.
The simplest example,
U_{t}\psi=e^{it}\psi
for all
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
is physically important. The corresponding group of automorphisms is called the

gauge group
, and denoted
\{\alpha_{{\rm G},t}\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
.

It follows directly from (
1.6
), (
1.8
) and (
1.9
) that with
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|:=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\alpha_{j}
,
defining the self-adjoint operator
{\mathcal{N}}
by

(1.10)

{\mathcal{U}}_{e^{it}}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=e^{it|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|}\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}=e^{it{\mathcal{N}}}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ ,

and hence for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{H}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(1.11)

\alpha_{{\rm G},t}(A)=e^{it{\mathcal{N}}}Ae^{-it{\mathcal{N}}}.

While the definition (
1.4
) of
{\mathcal{N}}
may have appeared to depend on the choice of the basis
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{N}\}
, it is now clear that this is not the case because for any choice of the basis,
e^{it{\mathcal{N}}}
implements the gauge automorphism group, and
e^{it{\mathcal{N}}}\Omega_{0}=\Omega_{0}
.

There is another useful way to express the generators of the unitary groups
\{{\mathcal{U}}_{e^{itH}}\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
produced by second quantization that builds directly on the construction of
{\mathcal{N}}
..
Let
X=\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\phi_{j}|
be any operator on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, written as a sum of rank one projections, which is always possible using, for
example, the singular value decomposition. Define the operator
\widehat{X}
on
{\mathcal{K}}
by
\widehat{X}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z^{*}(\psi_{j})Z(\phi_{j})
. The transform

(1.12)

X=\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\phi_{j}|\ \mapsto\ \widehat{X}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z^{*}(\psi_{j})Z(\phi_{j})\

was studied by Araki and Wyss
[
6
]
who showed that
that
\widehat{X}
does not depend on the particular representation of
X
as a sum or rank one operators, and that
\|\widehat{X}\|\leq\|X\|_{1}
where the
norm on the left is the operator norm on
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{K}}})
and the norm on the right is the trace norm. Moreover, by a simple computation
[
6
]
, for any two operators
X,Y
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}

(1.13)

[\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}]=\widehat{[X,Y]}\ .

For further results on the transform (
1.12
), see
[
5
,
45
]
.

If
H
is self-adjoint with spectral decomposition

{\displaystyle H=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|}
, then

\widehat{H}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}Z^{*}(\psi_{j})Z(\psi_{j})\ .

Because
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{N}\}
is orthonormal it follows that
\{Z^{*}(\psi_{1})Z(\psi_{1}),\dots,Z^{*}(\psi_{N})Z(\psi_{N})\}
is a set of
N
mutually commuting orthogonal projections.
Taking
H
to be the identity on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
yields the number operator
{\mathcal{N}}
; that is; by
{\mathcal{N}}=\widehat{{\mathds{1}}}
, recovering the formula (
1.4
).

Using the CAR, one readily computes the commutators

(1.14)

[{\mathcal{N}},Z(\psi)]=Z(\psi)\quad{\rm and\ hence}\quad[{\mathcal{N}},Z^{*}(\psi)]=-Z^{*}(\psi)

for all choices of
\psi
.

It follows from (
1.14
) that

(1.15)

e^{it{\mathcal{N}}}Z(\psi)e^{-it{\mathcal{N}}}=Z(e^{it}\psi)\quad{\rm and\ hence}\quad e^{it{\mathcal{N}}}Z^{*}(\psi)e^{-it{\mathcal{N}}}=Z^{*}(e^{-it}\psi)\ ,

and this is another way to prove (
1.11
).
Of course for any
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
{\mathcal{H}}+a{\mathds{1}}
would generate a unitary group implementing the gauge automorphism group, but the choice of
{\mathcal{N}}
as the generator is singled out by the property that the generator has
\Omega_{0}
in its null-space, independent of the choice of phase on
\Omega_{0}
.

For a
*
-automorphism of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, let
\widehat{\alpha}
be its dual, acting on the set of states on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{q}}
by

\widehat{\alpha}(\rho)=\rho\circ\alpha\ .

1.1 DEFINITION
.

A quantum operation
\Phi
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is
gauge covariant
in case for all
t

(1.16)

\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm G},t}\circ\Phi=\Phi\circ\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm G},t}\ ,

and is
gauge contravariant
in case for all
t

(1.17)

\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm G},t}\circ\Phi=\Phi\circ\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm G},-t}\ ,

The dual
\widehat{\alpha}
of every
*
-automorphism
\alpha
of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is a quantum operation. Therefore, we say that
\alpha
is
gauge covariant
if and only if

\widehat{\alpha}\circ\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm G},t}=\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm G},t}\circ\widehat{\alpha}
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and that

\alpha
is
gauge contravariant
if and only if

\widehat{\alpha}\circ\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm G},t}=\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm G},-t}\circ\widehat{\alpha}
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

A quantum operation
\Phi
is gauge covariant if and only if for all states
\rho
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(1.18)

[{\mathcal{N}},\Phi(\rho)]=\Phi([{\mathcal{N}},\rho])\ ,

and gauge contravariant if and only if for all states
\rho
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,
[{\mathcal{N}},\Phi(\rho)]=-\Phi([{\mathcal{N}},\rho])
.

This brings us to the second way of viewing the automorphism
\alpha_{U}
specified in (
1.3
) in terms of the unitary
U
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.
Write
U
in the form
U=e^{-iH}
where
H
is self-adjoint, which is always possible by the Spectral Theorem. Then one easily checks that for all
\psi\in{\mathord{\mathscr{H}}}_{1}
,

(1.19)

e^{i\widehat{H}}Z(\psi)e^{-i\widehat{H}}=Z(e^{-iH}\psi)=Z(U\psi)\ .

Therefore
{\mathcal{U}}_{U}:=e^{it\widehat{H}}
is a unitary on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
implementing
\alpha_{U}
, and since
\widehat{H}\Omega_{0}=\Omega_{0}
,
{\mathcal{U}}_{U}\Omega_{0}=\Omega_{0}
.

1.2 DEFINITION
.

An automorphism of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is
quasi-free
if for some self-adjoint operator
H
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(1.20)

\alpha(A)=e^{i\widehat{H}}Ae^{-i\widehat{H}}

Since
{\mathcal{N}}=\widehat{{\mathds{1}}}
, it follows from (
1.13
) that for all
H
,
[\widehat{H},{\mathcal{N}}]=0
, and hence
e^{i\widehat{H}}
commutes with
{\mathcal{N}}
. In particular,
every quasi-free automorphism of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is gauge covariant
.

Because
{\mathcal{N}}
is self-adjoint, the commutant of
{\mathcal{N}}
is a von Neumann sub-algebra of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that was first studied by Araki and Wyss
[
6
]
.

1.3 DEFINITION
.

The
Araki-Wyss
algebra
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is the commutant of the number operator
{\mathcal{N}}
.

Evidently, for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\alpha_{{\rm G},t}(A)=A
. For this reason the Araki-Wyss algebra is sometimes called the

gauge invariant CAR algebra
, abbreviated as the GICAR algebra.

1.4 DEFINITION
.

For any state
\rho
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, define a quadratic form
Q_{\rho}(\phi,\psi)
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{H}}}_{1}
by

(1.21)

Q_{\rho}(\phi,\psi)=\rho(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\phi))\ ,

and define a positive semi-definite operator
Q_{\rho}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
by
\langle\phi,Q_{\rho}\psi\rangle=Q(\phi,\psi)
.
The operator
Q_{\rho}
is called the
symbol
of
\rho
. If
\rho(Z(\psi))=0
for all
\psi
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, it is called the
covariance
of
\rho
, or in the physics literature, the
two-point function
of
\rho
.

Every state
\rho
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{H}}})
is determined by its moments

\rho(Z^{*}(\psi_{1})\cdots Z^{*}(\psi_{m})Z(\varphi_{1})\cdots Z(\varphi_{n}))\ ,

where
0\leq m,n\leq N
and
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{m},\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n}\}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{1}

because the general element of
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{H}}})
can be written as a linear combination of elements of this form.

1.5 DEFINITION
.

A state
\rho
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is a

gauge invariant Gaussian state
in case all of its moments are determined by its second moments (those for
m=n=1)
, and hence by
Q_{\rho}
, in the following way

(1.22)

\rho(Z^{*}(\psi_{1})\cdots Z^{*}(\psi_{m})Z(\phi_{n})\cdots Z(\phi_{1}))=\begin{cases}0&m\neq n\\
\det\left(\langle\phi_{j},Q_{\rho}\psi_{k}\rangle\right)&m=n\ .\end{cases}

The set of all gauge invariant Gaussian states is denoted
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality associated to the GNS inner product
\langle A,B\rangle_{GNS,\rho}:=\rho(A^{*}B)
,

|\langle\phi,Q_{\rho}\psi\rangle|^{2}\leq\rho(Z^{*}(\phi)Z(\phi))\rho(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\psi))\leq\|\phi\|^{2}\|\psi\|^{2}\ ,

and it follows that
0\leq Q_{\rho}\leq{\mathds{1}}
.
Monomials of the form
Z^{*}(\psi_{1})\cdots Z^{*}(\psi_{n})Z(\varphi_{1})\cdots Z(\varphi_{n})
, with equal numbers of
Z^{*}
and
Z
terms are invariant under gauge automorphisms
\alpha_{{\rm G},t}
by (
1.15
) and (
1.11
). It follows that each
\rho\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}

and each
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

(1.23)

\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm G},t}(\rho)=\rho

This is the gauge invariant property of states in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
, while their Gaussian nature lies in the fact that they are completely determined by their second moments.
They are also physically natural, and arose in the BCS theory of superconductivity
[
10
,
13
]
in a way that had noting to do with looking for a Fermionic analog of classical Gaussian probability measures. Likewise, in the mathematical physics literature they arose in the work of Shale and Stinespring
[
62
]
on symmetry properties of states on CAR algebras. They sought, and found, a Fermionic counterpart to a Bosonic construction of Segal
[
60
]
, which did explicitly involve Gaussian probability densities, but they do not explicitly refer to Gaussian states. For other early work on Gaussian states, see
[
9
,
25
,
54
]
.

The discussion so far has not addressed the question of whether
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
is non-empty, but this is easy to settle.
Define
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
by

(1.24)

{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}:=\{Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})\ :\ 0\leq Q\leq{\mathds{1}}\}\ .

It turns out the states in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
stand in one-to-one correspondence with the operators
Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
. This is simplest to explain in terms of density matrices of states.

Let
Z
be a CAR field over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
that acts irreducibly on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
, so that
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
is
2^{N}
dimensional. The
normalized trace
is the state
\tau
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
given by

(1.25)

\tau(A)=\frac{1}{2^{N}}{\rm Tr}[A]\ .

Every state
\rho
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is represented by a density matrix,
also denoted

\rho
, and taken
with respect to the normalized trace
so that for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

\rho(A)=\tau(A\rho)\ .

It is convenient to use the same notation for states and their density matrices and we shall generally suppress the distinction where it is unlikely to lead to confusion.

Let
\alpha
be any
*
-automorphism of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. Writing
\alpha
as a unitary conjugation, and letting
\rho
also denote the density matrix of
\rho
with respect to
\tau
, for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}

\widehat{\alpha}(\rho)(A)=\tau(\rho\alpha(A))=\tau(\rho{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}A{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}^{*})=\tau(({\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}^{*}\rho{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha})A)\ .

Therefore,
\widehat{\alpha}(\rho)
has the density matrix
{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}^{*}\rho{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}
.

Fix a CAR field over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
acting irreducibly on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
.
For
Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
, let
{\displaystyle Q=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mu_{j}|\psi_{j}\rangle\psi_{j}|}
be a
spectral decomposition of
Q
. Define the density matrix
\rho_{Q}
by

(1.26)

\rho_{Q}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}((1-\mu_{j})2Z(\psi_{j})Z^{*}(\psi_{j})+\mu_{j}2Z^{*}(\psi_{j})Z(\psi_{j}))\ ,

noting that all of the factors commute so that the order is immaterial.

Simple computations show that indeed the symbol of
\rho_{Q}
is
Q
, and that the moments of
\rho_{Q}
are given by (
1.22
). Since the moments determine the state, there can be at most one state with such moments, and we have just displayed one. Hence
Q\mapsto\rho_{Q}
is one-to-one from
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
onto
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
. We have already observed that
states in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
are invariant under gauge transformations. It follows that if
\rho\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
, then its density matrix, also denoted
\rho
, belongs to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, and moreover, is invariant under gauge transformations:

\alpha_{{\rm G},t}(\rho)=\rho
.

We have already encountered specific examples of states in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
.
Suppose that
Q=\mu{\mathds{1}}
for some
\mu\in[0,1]
. Then

\rho_{\mu{\mathds{1}}}=2^{N}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left((1-\mu)Z(\psi_{j})Z^{*}(\psi_{j})+\mu Z^{*}(\psi_{j})Z(\psi_{j})\right)\ .

By (
1.1
), the density matrix
\rho_{\frac{1}{2}{\mathds{1}}}={\mathds{1}}
, and hence the state
\rho_{\frac{1}{2}{\mathds{1}}}=\tau
.
It is easy to see that
\prod_{j=1}^{N}Z(\psi_{j})Z^{*}(\psi_{j})\Psi=0
unless
\Psi
is a multiple of
\Omega_{0}
, and that

\prod_{j=1}^{N}Z^{*}(\psi_{j})Z(\psi_{j})\Psi=0
unless
\Psi
is a multiple of
\Omega_{1}
. Therefore,
the density matrix
\rho_{0}=2^{N}|\Omega_{0}\rangle\langle\Omega_{0}|
, and hence the state
\rho_{0}=\omega_{0}
,
and the density matrix
\rho_{{\mathds{1}}}=2^{N}|\Omega_{1}\rangle\langle\Omega_{1}|
, and hence the state
\rho_{{\mathds{1}}}=\omega_{1}
.
In summary,
\omega_{0}
,
\omega_{1}
and
\tau
are all GIG states. Segal was the first to emphasize that
\tau
is a natural non-commutative analog of the normal Gaussian law of classical probability in his approach to non-commutative integration
[
58
,
61
]
.

There is another way to write (
1.26
) that reveals another aspect of the Gaussian character of
\rho_{Q}
. Given
Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}^{\circ}
, the interior of
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
, define

(1.27)

H=-\log\left(\frac{Q}{{\mathds{1}}-Q}\right)\ .

Then

(1.28)

\rho_{Q}=\frac{1}{\tau(e^{-\widehat{H}})}e^{-\widehat{H}}\ .

Indeed,
H=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\log\left(\frac{\mu_{j}}{1-\mu_{j}}\right)|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|
if
Q=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mu_{j}|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|
is a spectral decomposition of
Q
, simple computations show that (
1.28
) is the same as (
1.26
) for
Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}^{\circ}
. We summarize this discussion in a lemma.

1.6 LEMMA
.

Let
\{Z_{1},\dots Z_{N}\}
be some fixed set of operators satisfying the CAR on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
of dimension
2^{N}
. Let
H
be self-adjoint on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.
Then
H
and the symbol
Q
of the state
\rho:=\frac{1}{\tau(e^{-\widehat{H}})}e^{-\widehat{H}}
are related by (
1.27
).

Moreover, the map

\rho\mapsto Q_{\rho}
is one-to-one from the set of gauge invariant Gaussian states onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
.

1.7 DEFINITION
.

A quantum operation
\Phi
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is a
gauge invariant Gaussian quantum operation
(GIG quantum operation) in case it is gauge covariant and maps

{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}

into itself. An automorphism
\alpha
is a
gauge invariant Gaussian quantum automorphism
in case its dual
\widehat{\alpha}
, considered a quantum operation,
is a GIG quantum operation.

One obvious example of a GIG quantum operation is

(1.29)

\Upsilon(A)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{it{\mathcal{N}}}Ae^{-it{\mathcal{N}}}{\rm d}t=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{2T}\int_{-T}^{T}e^{it{\mathcal{N}}}Ae^{-it{\mathcal{N}}}{\rm d}t

which is the orthogonal projection onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}
in with respect to the inner product
\tau(A^{*}B)
, and as such, is the tracial conditional expectation onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.
(See Appendix
A
).
If
\rho_{Q}
is the density matrix of the state in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
with symbol
Q
, then evidently
\Upsilon(\rho_{Q})=\rho_{Q}
. Consequently if
\Phi
is any GIG quantum operation,

\Phi\circ\Upsilon
,
\Upsilon\circ\Phi
and
\Upsilon\circ\Phi\circ\Upsilon
are also GIG quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that have the same restriction to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
as
\Phi
.

The following is proved in
[
6
]
:

1.8 THEOREM

(Araki-Wyss Theorem)
.

The complex linear span of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
is all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.

1.9 COROLLARY
.

Let
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}^{{\rm s.a.}}
denote the real linear space of self-adjoint operators in Ayaki-Wyss algebra
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.
Any linear map
\Lambda
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself maps
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}^{{\rm s.a.}}
into itself. The real linear span of

{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
is
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}^{{\rm s.a.}}
, and any real linear map
\Gamma
on

{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}^{{\rm s.a.}}
is completely determined by its action on
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.8
, every
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
can be written as
A=\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}\rho_{Q_{j}}
where each
z_{j}=x_{j}+iy_{j}\in{\mathbb{C}}
and each
\rho_{Q_{j}}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
. If
\Lambda
is a linear map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, then

\Lambda(A)=\Lambda(\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}\rho_{Q_{j}})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}\Lambda(\rho_{Q_{j}})\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\ .

If
A
is self-adjoint, then also
A=A^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{z_{j}}\rho_{Q_{j}}
, and hence
A=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\rho_{Q_{j}}
. Thus
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}^{{\rm s.a.}}
is the real linear span of

{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
. Finally, let
\Gamma
is any real linear map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}^{{\rm s.a.}}
, and let
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}^{{\rm s.a.}}
so that
A
can be written as

A=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\rho_{Q_{j}}
wih each
x_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then

\Gamma(A)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\Gamma(\rho_{Q_{j}})\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}

∎

Let
\Phi
be any quantum operation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. By the first part of Corollary
1.9
,
\Phi
maps

{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
into itself, and since
\Phi
is positive, it is Hermitian, and hence maps
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}^{{\rm s.a.}}
into itself. By the second part of Corollary
1.9
, the restriction of

\Phi
as a real linear map to

{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}^{{\rm s.a.}}
is completely determined by the action of
\Phi
on
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
. But then so is the restriction of
\Phi
as a complex linear map on

{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
because every real linear map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}^{{\rm s.a.}}
has a unique complex linear extension to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.
In view of these considerations, Corollary
1.9
motivates the following definition:

1.10 DEFINITION
.

Let
\Gamma
be a real linear map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}^{{\rm s.a.}}
. Let
\rho_{Q}
denote (the density matrix of) the GIG state on

{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
with symbol
Q
. The
symbol map of
\Gamma
is the map

\gamma_{\Gamma}^{\phantom{I}}:{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}\to{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
given by

(1.30)

\langle\phi,\gamma_{\Gamma}^{\phantom{I}}(Q)\psi\rangle=\tau(\Gamma(\rho_{Q})(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\phi))\ .

1.11 LEMMA
.

Let
\Phi
be a quantum operation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. Then
\Phi\big|_{{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
, the restriction of
\Phi

to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,
is completely determined by its symbol map.

Furthermore, let
\Phi
be any quantum operation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps

{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself.
Then
\Phi
has at least one extension to a GIG quantum operation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, namely
\Phi\circ\Upsilon
.

Proof.

Because of the one-to-one correspondence between GIG states and their symbols, and because of Corolary
1.9
,

\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}
, fully specifies the action of
\Phi
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.
This proves the first statement.

Now consider a quantum operation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. Then
\Phi\circ\Upsilon
is a quantum operation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that whose restriction to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is
\Phi
. Since
\Upsilon
is gauge-covariant, and since each
\alpha_{{\rm G},t}
acts trivially on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,
\Phi\circ\Upsilon
is gauge covariant.
∎

The following questions arise:

\bullet
What is the structure of the set of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that map

{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself?

\bullet
What is the structure of the set of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that map

{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself?

\bullet
Given a quantum operation
\Phi
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{GIG}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, what is the structure of the set of all GIG quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
extend
\Phi
?

To answer the first question, it suffices by Lemma
1.11
to first determine the class of maps on
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
that arise as symbol maps of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that map

{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, and second, to then show how to construct
\Phi
, as a quantum operation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
from the data determining its symbol map.

Towards an answer to the second question, note that by Lemma
1.11
, one can always extend a quantum operation
\Phi
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself to a GIG quantum operation on

{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, by the extension provided there, namely
\Phi\circ\Upsilon
.

However,
\Psi
is not one-to-one; it has a large null-space (for
{\rm dim}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})>1
). Later on, we shall be primarily concerned with continuous semigroup

\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of quantum operations, each of which maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. The continuity requirement is that
\lim_{t\to 0}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}={\mathds{1}}
, and it has the consequence that each
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
is one-to-one.
If
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is such a group of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, then
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\circ\Upsilon\}_{t\geq 0}
is a family of GIG quantum operations such that for all
s,t\geq 0
,
({\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\circ\Upsilon)({\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\circ\Upsilon)={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{s+t}\circ\Upsilon
, but is not a continuous semigroup because
\lim_{t\to 0}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}\circ\Upsilon=\Upsilon\neq{\mathds{1}}
. Fortunately, we shall see that when
\Phi
is a one-to-one quantum operation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, it always has one-to-one extensions to all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.

There are a number of variations on these questions taking into the account the notions of gauge covariant and gauge contravariant quantum operations.
The first work in this direction is a classic result of Hugenholtz and Kadison
[
39
]
on the automorphisms of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that map
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. Before stating their result, we must introduce another fundamental automorphism of the CAR algebra, namely the
particle-hole automorphism
.

While many treatments of the CAR are phrased from the beginning in terms of “annihilation operators” and “creation operators”, which arise directly in the construction of
Fock
[
32
]
,
the canonical anti-commutation relations (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) are symmetric in
Z(\psi)
and
Z^{*}(\phi)
. However
Z
is conjugate linear, and hence

Z^{*}
is linear. As emphasized by Araki
[
5
]
, this near symmetry is fundamentally important, and can be completed
by introducing one more piece of structure on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
: a complex conjugation
C
.

A complex conjugation
C
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
is a conjugate linear map on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
with the
property that
C^{2}={\mathds{1}}
. Regarding
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
as a real Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{1,\Re}
with the inner product
\Re\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
,
C
is an orthogonal transformation
with spectrum
\{-1,1\}
, and
C\psi=\psi
if and only if
C(i\psi)=-i\psi
. Hence
\psi\mapsto i\psi
is invertible from the
1
-eigenspace of
C
to the
-1
-eigenspace of
C
, and
both eigenspaces are
N
dimensional. It follows that if
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{N}\}
is any orthonormal basis for the
1
-eigenspace of
C
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{1,\Re}
, then

\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{N}\}
is also an orthonormal basis of the complex Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. It will be convenient to denote
C\psi
by
\overline{\psi}
.
Vectors
\psi
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that
\overline{\psi}=\psi
are called
real
. Given a self-adjoint operator
H
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, define
\bar{H}
, its complex conjugate, by

\bar{H}\psi=\overline{H\bar{\psi}}\ .

Finally it is easy to see that if
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{N}\}
any real orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, then for all operators
X
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

X^{T}:=\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}\langle\varphi_{j},X\varphi_{k}\rangle|\varphi_{k}\rangle\langle\varphi_{j}|

is independent of the particular real orthonormal basis. The operator
X^{T}
is the
transpose
of
T
. By the invariance just mentioned, it depends only on the complex conjugation
C
.

Complex conjugations arise physically in connection with time-reversal
[
41
,
69
]
.
If
\bar{H}=H
, then
H
is called
real
. In elementary quantum mechanics one encounters real Hamiltonians of the form
H=-\Delta+V
acting on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
, and for

\psi\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})

we define
C\psi
by
C\psi(x)=\overline{\psi(x)}
, the complex conjugate of the number
\psi(x)
. Then
H
is real, and if
\psi(x,t)
solves

{\displaystyle i\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\psi(x,t)=H\psi(x,t)}
, then
\overline{\psi(x,t)}
satisfies
{\displaystyle i\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\overline{\psi(x,-t)}=H\overline{\psi(x,-t)}}
.
Thus, complex conjugation is associated with time reversal, though more intricate examples arise, for example in the presence of magnetic fields. We shall proceed by fixing some arbitrary complex conjugation on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, but in a physical Fermion system,

C
would be selected by considerations related to time reversal. In any case, henceforth we assume that
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
is equipped with a preferred complex conjugation
C
.

We now define the particle-hole automorphism.
Given a CAR field over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, define the
particle-hole automorphism
,
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}
, of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
by

(1.31)

\alpha_{{\rm ph}}(Z(\psi))=Z^{*}(\overline{\psi})

Since as is easily checked,
\psi\mapsto Z^{*}(\overline{\psi})
is a CAR field over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, there is a unitary
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\rm ph}}
such that for all
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

(1.32)

{\mathcal{U}}_{{\rm ph}}Z(\psi){\mathcal{U}}^{*}_{{\rm ph}}=Z^{*}(\overline{\psi})\ ,

and hence
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}
is indeed a
*
-automorphism.

It is easy to see that
\alpha^{2}_{{\rm ph}}={\mathds{1}}
, and consequently we may choose the phase so that
{\mathcal{U}}^{*}_{{\rm ph}}={\mathcal{U}}_{{\rm ph}}
. Therefore, if
\rho
is a state on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, the density matrix of

\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm ph}}(\rho)
is
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}
applied to the density matrix of
\rho
. For any
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

(1.33)

\alpha_{{\rm ph}}(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\psi))=Z(\bar{\psi})Z^{*}(\bar{\psi})\quad{\rm and}\quad\alpha_{{\rm ph}}(Z(\psi)Z^{*}(\psi))=Z^{*}(\bar{\psi})Z(\bar{\psi})

Thus
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}
transforms the projector for “state
\psi
is occupied” into the projector for “state
\bar{\psi}
is unoccupied”, and hence then name “particle hole automorphism”.
Because the particle-hole automorphism interchanges “occupied” and “unoccupied”, it swaps the vacuum and the anti vacuum states. Indeed, it
follows from the CAR that
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}({\mathcal{N}})=N{\mathds{1}}-{\mathcal{N}}
. Consequently,

\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm ph}}(\omega_{0})=\omega_{1}\quad{\rm and}\quad\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm ph}}(\omega_{1})=\omega_{0}\ .

There is a more general expression of this.

1.12 LEMMA
.

The symbol map of
\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm ph}}
is
Q\mapsto{\mathds{1}}-Q^{T}
where
Q^{T}
denotes the transpose of
Q
computed using any orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
consisting of real vectors.

Proof.

Applying (
1.33
) to the formula (
1.26
) for density matrices
\rho
of states in

{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
, one sees that for all
Q
in
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}(\rho_{Q})\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
, and that the symbol of
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}(\rho_{Q})
is

{\mathds{1}}-Q^{T}
.
∎

While
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}
maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, it is not gauge covariant. Indeed for any three vectors
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
and
\psi_{3}
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

\alpha_{{\rm ph}}(Z^{*}(\psi_{1})Z^{*}(\psi_{2}(Z(\psi_{3}))=Z(\overline{\psi_{1}})Z(\overline{\psi_{2}})Z^{*}(\overline{\psi_{3}})\ .

However, by (
1.15
)

\alpha_{{\rm G},t}(Z^{*}(\psi_{1})Z^{*}(\psi_{2}(Z(\psi_{3}))=e^{-it}Z^{*}(\psi_{1})Z^{*}(\psi_{2}(Z(\psi_{3})

while

\alpha_{{\rm G},t}(Z(\overline{\psi_{1}})Z(\overline{\psi_{2}})Z^{*}(\overline{\psi_{3}}))=e^{it}Z(\overline{\psi_{1}})Z(\overline{\psi_{2}})Z^{*}(\overline{\psi_{3}})\ .

Therefore,
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}
is not gauge covariant. In fact, a simple extension of this argument shows that
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}
is
gauge contravariant
.

A related operation will also figure in our results.
Second quantization “lifts” the complex conjugation
C
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
to a complex conjugation
{\mathcal{C}}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
: Let
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{N}\}
be any real orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Then as in (
1.6
), this induces an orthonormal basis
\{\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}}
through

(1.34)

\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}:=(Z^{*}(\varphi_{1}))^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots(Z^{*}(\varphi_{N}))^{\alpha_{N}}\Omega_{0}\

for all
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
. Define the conjugate-linear unitary
{\mathcal{C}}:{\mathcal{K}}\to{\mathcal{K}}
by

(1.35)

{\textstyle{\mathcal{C}}\left(\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}}z_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\right)=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}}\overline{z_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ .}

Evidently,
{\mathcal{C}}
is a complex conjugation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
and self-adjoint. It is easy to check that for all
\phi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

{\mathcal{C}}Z(\phi){\mathcal{C}}=Z(\bar{\phi})\ .

(Note that
\phi\mapsto Z(\bar{\phi})
is not a CAR field on
{\mathcal{H}}
because it is linear, not conjugate linear.)

It follows from (
1.26
) that if
\rho_{Q}
is the density matrix of the GIG state with symbol
Q
,

(1.36)

{\mathcal{C}}\rho_{Q}{\mathcal{C}}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}((1-\mu_{j})2Z(\overline{\psi_{j}})Z^{*}(\overline{\psi_{j}})+\mu_{j}2Z^{*}(\overline{\psi_{j}})Z(\overline{\psi_{j}}))\ ,

1.13 DEFINITION
.

The map
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is defined by

\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}(A):={\mathcal{C}}A{\mathcal{C}}\ .

This map is called the
transpose map
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.

1.14 LEMMA
.

The transpose map
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, and the symbol map of

\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
is
Q\mapsto\overline{Q}=Q^{T}
. However, the transpose map is not CP.

Proof.

The fact that
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself is evident from (
1.36
), from which it also follows
that for
Q=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mu_{j}|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|
, the symbol of
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}(\rho_{Q})
is
\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mu_{j}|\bar{\psi}_{j}\rangle\langle\bar{\psi}_{j}|
,
which is the transpose of
Q
in an real orthonormal basis, which is how the transpose has been defined for operator on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
equipped with a convex conjugation
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Moreover, for any self-adjoint
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}
, the matrix representing
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}(X)
with respect to the real basis
\{\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\>\ {\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}\}
is is the transpose of the matrix
representing
X
with respect to this basis. Therefore,
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
is not CP.
∎

Recall Definition
1.20
. The following fundamental characterization of quasi-free automorphisms was proved in
[
39
]
.

1.15 THEOREM

(Hugenholtz and Kadison)
.

Let
Z
be a CAR field over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
acting irreducibly on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
. Let
\alpha
be an automorphism of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
such
that

\widehat{\alpha}
maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. Then either
\widehat{\alpha}(\omega_{0})=\omega_{0}
or else
\widehat{\alpha}(\omega_{0})=\omega_{1}
.
In the first case,
\alpha
is a quasi-free automorphism
\alpha_{U}
of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
induced by some unitary
U
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. In the second case,
\alpha\circ\alpha_{{\rm ph}}

is a quasi-free automorphism
\alpha_{U}
of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
induced by some unitary
U
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.

Because every quasi-free automorphism is gauge covariant, and because
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}
is gauge contravariant, it follows that every automorphism
\alpha
whose dual maps

{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself is either gauge covariant, which is the case of
\widehat{\alpha}(\omega_{0})=\omega_{0}
, or
gauge contravariant, which is the case of
\widehat{\alpha}(\omega_{0})=\omega_{1}
.

In their 1975 paper
[
39
]
, Hugenholtz and Kadison also gave examples of quantum operations that mapped
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. These were constructed in a “functorial”
manner, generalizing the “second quantization functor”
U\mapsto\alpha_{U}
. They constructed a map from contractions
S
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
into quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that reduces to the functorial map

U\mapsto\widehat{\alpha}_{U}
when
U
is unitary. The ingredients of their construction, recalled in Section
3
, were a unitary dilation of
S
onto
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}

and the vacuum state. They showed that their quantum operations mapped
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. A few year later in 1979, Evans
[
26
]
generalized this construction
using the same unitary dilation of
S
, but any GIG state
\rho_{Q_{0}}
in place of
\omega_{0}
. As Evans showed, this construction results in a quantum operation that maps
\rho_{Q}
, the
GIG state with symbol
Q
to the GIG state with symbol

(1.37)

SQS^{*}+({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}Q_{0}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}\ .

As a symbol of a state in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
, the operator specified in (
1.37
) must belong to
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
. It follows that

(1.38)

0\leq({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}Q_{0}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}\leq{\mathds{1}}-SS^{*}\ ,

and that for an appropriate choice of
Q_{0}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,
R:=({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}Q_{0}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}
can be any operator on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
satisfying
0\leq R\leq{\mathds{1}}-SS^{*}
.

In summary, given any contraction
S
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and an operator
R
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
satisfying

(1.39)

0\leq R\leq{\mathds{1}}-SS^{*}\ ,

the construction of Evans-Hugenholtz-Kadison yields quantum operation
\Phi_{S,R}
with symbol map

\Phi_{S,R}(\rho_{Q})
that has the symbol

(1.40)

\gamma_{\Phi_{S,R}}(Q)=SQS^{*}+R\ .

Moreover, for every choice of
R
, the map
\Phi_{S,R}
is an “extension” of the second quantization map
U\mapsto\widehat{\alpha}_{U}
from unitaries on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
to
*
-automorphisms of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
in that

\Phi_{S,R}^{\dagger}(Z(\psi))=Z(S\psi)

for all
\psi\in{\mathord{\mathscr{H}}}_{1}
, which extends (
1.3
). When
S
is a unitary, (
1.39
) required
R=0
.

1.16 DEFINITION
.

A
compatible pair

(S,R)
for Evans-Hugenholtz-Kadison construction consists of a contraction
S
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and
R\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
such that (
1.39
)
is satisfied, in which case
\Phi_{S,R}
denotes the quantum operation produced by the Evans-Hugenholtz-Kadison construction.

The Evans-Hugenholtz-Kadison construction is reviewed in Section
3
, and it is shown there that for all compatible pairs
(S,R)
,
\Phi_{S,R}
is gauge-covariant.
From their construction, one immediately obtains a second family of examples by composing
\Phi_{S,R}
with
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}
. Then by Lemma
1.12
, for all
\rho_{Q}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
,

\Phi_{S,R}\circ\alpha_{{\rm ph}}(\rho_{Q})

has the symbol

(1.41)

S^{*}({\mathds{1}}-Q^{T})S+R\ .

The symbol maps resulting from the Evans-Hugenholtz-Kadison construction, namely

(1.42)

\gamma_{\Phi_{S,R}}^{\phantom{I}}(Q)=S^{*}QS+R\quad{\rm and}\quad\gamma_{\Phi_{S,R}\circ\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm ph}}}^{\phantom{I}}(Q)=S^{*}({\mathds{1}}-Q^{T})S+R

are
affine maps
. This is remarkable because the map
Q\mapsto\rho_{Q}
is highly non-linear; as it must be:
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
is far from convex when
N>1
.
Indeed, a non-trivial convex combination of two distinct classical Gaussian probability densities on the real line is never Gaussian. The situation in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}

is more subtle, and was fully clarified by Wolfe
[
70
]
who proved the following lemma:

1.17 LEMMA

(Wolfe’s Lemma)
.

Let
Q_{0},Q_{1}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
, and let
\rho_{Q_{0}}
and
\rho_{Q_{1}}
be the corresponding GIG states. Let
0<\lambda<1
.
Then
(1-\lambda)\rho{Q_{0}}+\lambda\rho_{Q_{1}}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}

if and only if for some vector
\eta\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

(1.43)

Q_{1}-Q_{0}=\pm|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|\ .

Wolfe used Lemma
1.17
to give a new proof of Theorem
1.15
, and an extension of it to “non gauge invariant Gaussian states”. This broader class of Gaussian states is also of interest; see the recent papers
[
12
,
55
,
56
]
, all in the finite dimensional context. Many of the questions discussed here in the gauge invariant case are also of interest in the more general setting, and we plan to return to these questions later.

a topic to which we shall return in a later report.
We now use Wolfe’s Lemma to construct quantum operations that map
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself but which have different symbol maps than those provided by the Evans-Hugenholtz-Kadison construction.

1.18 THEOREM
.

There exist quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that map
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself whose symbol maps are not affine.

Proof.

Let
\{E_{0},E_{1}\}
be a binary POVM on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
. That is,
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
are positive operators on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
such that
E_{0}+E_{1}={\mathds{1}}
. Let
Q_{0},Q_{1}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
satisfy
(
1.43
) for some non-zero vector
\eta
and some choice of the sign.

Define a CPTP map
\Phi
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
by by

(1.44)

\Phi(X)={\rm Tr}[E_{0}X]\rho_{Q_{0}}+{\rm Tr}[E_{1}X]\rho_{Q_{1}}\ .

It is elementary to write down a Krauss representation for
\Phi
and to confirm that it is a quantum operation. Physically, it corresponds to performing a binary
measurement with possible results
\{0,1\}
such that if the result is
0
, the final state is
\rho_{Q_{0}}
, and if the result is
1
, final state is
\rho_{Q_{1}}
.
For any input state
\rho
,
{\rm Tr}[E_{0}\rho]
and
{\rm Tr}[E_{1}\rho]
are non-negative and sum to
1
, so that by Wolfe’s Lemma,
\Phi(\rho)\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
.
Evidently the symbol map of
\Phi
,
\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}
, is given by

(1.45)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}(Q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}[E_{0}\rho_{Q}]Q_{0}+{\rm Tr}[E_{1}\rho_{Q}]Q_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q_{0}+{\rm Tr}[E_{1}\rho_{Q}](Q_{1}-Q_{0})=Q_{0}\pm{\rm Tr}[E_{0}\rho_{Q}]|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|\ .

Since
E_{0}
can be any element of
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
, and the complex span of
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
is all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})
, if
Q\mapsto{\rm Tr}[E_{0}\rho_{Q}]
were affine, then
Q\mapsto\rho_{Q}
would be affine, which is not the case.
∎

Despite the example of Theorem
1.15
, it would be many years before anyone took up the problem of determining the structure of
quantum operations, other than duals of automorphisms, that map
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. While the corresponding problem in the context of the canonical commutation relations (CCR)
has been extensively investigated, the first work on this problem in the context of the CAR that we are aware of is a 2008 paper
[
28
]
of
Dierckx, Fannes and Pogorzelska. Their Proposition 8 (see
[
28
, Section 5.2]
) asserts that a linear map
\Gamma
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into
itself if and only if its symbol map

\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}
has the form

(1.46)

\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}(Q)=R\pm S^{*}QS\quad{\rm or}\quad\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}(Q)=R\pm S^{*}({\mathds{1}}-Q^{T})S\ .

They further assert that in the case of the plus signs in (
1.46
) there exists a quantum operation
\Phi
with such a symbol map if and only if

0\leq R\leq S^{*}S
, while they do not address this issue in the case of minus signs. The second statement is correct: The Evans-Hugenholtz-Kadison construction gives the existence of such
a quantum operation
\Phi
under the conditions
0\leq R\leq S^{*}S
, and Fannes and Rocha
[
31
]
showed the necessity of this restriction on
R
and
S
.

However, the first statement cannot be correct, because as we have seen in the examples (
1.44
), symbol maps, even of quantum operations that map

{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, are not always affine, while all of the maps in (
1.46
) are affine.

The maps
\Phi
specified in (
1.44
), which provide the counterexamples, always have a non-trivial null space. It turns out, as we prove here,
that whenever a GIG quantum operation
\Phi
is one-to-one, then its symbol map

\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}
is indeed affine. Note that if
\Phi
is part of a quantum dynamical semigroup, so that it has the form
\Phi=e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}}
for some
Lindblad generator
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}
(see
[
34
,
44
]
) then
\Phi
will indeed be one-to-one. Indeed, the latter half of the present work is largely concerned with semigroups of GIG quantum operations, and
our results requiring the one-to-one property will be applicable in all cases.

The main theorem proved in Section
2
is Theorem
1.19
below. It refers to the GIG quantum operations provided by the EHK construction that is
reviewed in Section
3
. For present purposes, all one need to know is the for each compatible pair
(S,R)
,
\Phi_{S,R}
is a well-defined GIF quantum operation whose symbol map is

Q\mapsto R+SQS^{*}
. The theorem also refers to the particle hole automorphism
\alpha_{{\rm ph}}
and to the map
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
defined in (
1.13
).

1.19 THEOREM
.

Let
\Phi
be a one-to-one Hermitian map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. Then either either

(1.47)

\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}({\mathds{1}})\geq\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}(0)\quad{\rm or\ else}\quad\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}(0)\geq\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}({\mathds{1}})

but not both. In the first case
there is a contraction
S
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and an operator
R\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
such that

SS^{*}+R\leq{\mathds{1}}
and such that either for all
Q

(1.48)

\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}(Q)=SQS^{*}+R

and
\Phi\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}=\Phi_{S,R}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
so that
\Phi\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
is CP,
or else for all
Q
,

(1.49)

\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}(Q)=SQ^{T}S^{*}+R\ ,

and and
\Phi\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}=\Phi_{S,R}\circ\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
, so that
\Phi\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
is co-CP, but not CP.

In the second case,
there is a contraction
S
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and an operator
R\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
such that

SS^{*}+R\leq{\mathds{1}}
and such that either for all
Q

(1.50)

\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}(Q)=S({\mathds{1}}-Q^{T})QS^{*}+R

and
\Phi\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}=\Phi_{S,R}\circ\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm ph}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
so that
\Phi\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
is CP,
or else for all
Q
,

(1.51)

\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}(Q)=S({\mathds{1}}-Q)S^{*}+R\ ,

and
\Phi\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}=\Phi_{S,R}\circ\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm ph}}\circ\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
so that
\Phi\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
is co-CP, but not CP.

Theorem
1.19
is proved in Section
2
. Then in Section
3
, the Evans-Hugenholz-Kadison construction is recalled in full detail, and some new results are proved under additional conditions on the symbol map. For instance, when the compatible pair
(S,R)
specifying
\Phi_{(S,R)}
is of the form
(e^{-H},R)
with
H\geq 0
and
[H,R]
,
an explicit diagonalization of
\Phi_{(S,R)}
in terms of “Fermionic Hermit polynomials” is obtained; see Theorem
3.10
. The results up to this point provide a complete analysis the class of one-to-one quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that map
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, and provide extensions of all of them to
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.

The rest of the paper is devoted to continuous semigroups of quantum operations
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that map
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself.
The property
\lim_{t\to 0}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}={\mathds{1}}
implies that each
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
is one-to-one, and hence Theorem
1.19
is applicable.

The main result of
Section
4
is Theorem
4.3
which establishes a one-to-one correspondence between semigroups
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of quantum operations on

{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that map
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, and pairs
(G,A)
where
G
is a contraction semigroup generator on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and
A
is an operator on

{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying
0\leq A\leq-G-G^{*}
.

The main result of Section
5
,Theorem
5.1
, provides an extension of every
semigroup of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself to all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. This improves on a result of Evans
[
26
]
who proved that such an extension exists when
G
commutes with
A
(though his results is
expressed somewhat differently). Moreover, Theorem
5.1

gives an explicit expression for the generator in terms of the data

(G,A)
figuring in Theorem
4.3
.

Under the commutativity condition of Evans, one can say more. In particular, the case in which
G=G^{*}
and
G
commutes with

A
, giving rise to the
Fermionic Mehler semigroups
, is then investigated in complete detail, and an explicit diagoanalization of the generators is obtained in this case;
see Theorem
5.15
together with the earlier Theorem
3.10
.
These semigroups have a natural extension to all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
; see Theorem
5.15
.
A simple argument using the Trotter Product Formula shows that the set of GIG semigroup generators is a convex cone; see Theorem
5.18
. The same reasoning used in the proof of this allows us to compute
symbol maps of semigroups generated by
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}+{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}
when we know the symbol maps of the semigroups generated by
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}
and by
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}
; see Theorem
5.18
.
We then turn to the problem of embedding GIG quantum operations in a GIG semigroup; Theorem
5.21
gives a necessary condition. Section
6
, presents some simple results on invariant states for GIG quantum operations.

Finally, Appendix A contain many facts concerning conditional expectations that are used in this work, and for which we do not know of a convenient reference. There are also some new
new results concerning the Petz recovery maps and its dual, the Accardi-Cecchini coarse graining operator which are shown to be Gaussian operations when they are induced by a Gaussian state.

2.
Proof of the structure theorem

This section provides the proof of Theorem
1.19
. The proof is somewhat lengthly, and will be broken into several pieces which are results of independent interest.

Throughout this section, we fix a linear map
\Phi
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and write
\gamma
in place of
\gamma_{\Phi}
to denote its symbol map.
Let
\rho_{Q}
denote the GIG density matrix with symbol
Q
. By (
1.27
) and (
1.28
), the map
Q\mapsto\rho_{Q}
is infinitely differentiable on
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}^{\circ}
.
Then since
\Phi
is linear, and the map sending
\rho
to its symbol is linear,
\gamma
is infinitely differentiable on
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}^{\circ}
.
Let
D\gamma_{Q}
denote its derivative at
Q
. That is, for self-adjoint
H
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

(2.1)

D\gamma_{Q}(H)=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{t}(\gamma(Q+tH)-\gamma(Q)\ .

We begin with a lemma
due to
[
28
]
, and we follow their proof.

2.1 LEMMA
.

Let
Q_{0}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}^{\circ}
and
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, There is an
\eta\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that for all
t
such that
Q_{0}+t|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}^{\circ}
,

(2.2)

D\gamma_{Q_{0}+t|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|}(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)=D\gamma_{Q_{0}}(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)=\pm|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|\ ,

and consequently

(2.3)

\gamma(Q_{0}+t|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)=\gamma(Q_{0})\pm t|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|\

where the sign is independent of
t
.

Proof.

Define
Q_{t}:=Q_{0}+t|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
, and let
r>0
be such that
Q_{t}\in\{Q\ :\ 0<Q<{\mathds{1}}\}
for
|t|<r
. It follows from Wolfe’s Lemma that for all
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
,

\rho_{(1-\lambda)Q_{0}+\lambda Q_{t}}=(1-\lambda)\rho_{Q_{0}}+\lambda\rho_{Q_{t}}\ .

Since
\Phi
maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself,
\Phi(\rho_{(1-\lambda)Q_{0}+\lambda Q_{t}})
,
\Phi(\rho_{Q_{0}})
and
\Phi(\rho_{Q_{t}})
all belong to
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
,
and since
\Phi
is linear,

(2.4)

\Phi(\rho_{(1-\lambda)Q_{0}+\lambda Q_{t}})=(1-\lambda)\Phi(\rho_{Q_{0}})+\lambda\Phi(\rho_{Q_{t}})\ .

Therefore, by Wolfe’s Lemma,

(2.5)

Q_{\Phi(\rho_{Q_{t}})}-Q_{\Phi(\rho_{Q_{0}})}=\gamma(Q_{t})-\gamma(Q_{0})=\pm|\eta_{t}\rangle\langle\eta_{t}|

for some
\eta_{t}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Since the map
\rho\mapsto Q_{\rho}
is linear, (
2.4
) yields

\gamma(Q_{0}+\lambda t|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)=(1-\lambda)\gamma(Q_{0})+\lambda\gamma(Q_{t})\ .

We may rewrite this as

{\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{t})-\gamma(Q_{0})=\frac{1}{\lambda}(\gamma(Q_{0}+\lambda t|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|))-\gamma(Q_{0}))}
.
Taking the limit
\lambda\downarrow 0
yields

(2.6)

\gamma(Q_{t})-\gamma(Q_{0})=tD\gamma_{Q_{0}}(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)\ .

Combining (
2.5
) and (
2.6
)

(2.7)

tD\gamma_{Q_{0}}(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)=\pm|\eta_{t}\rangle\langle\eta_{t}|\ .

Therefore some
\eta\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,
|\eta_{t}\rangle\langle\eta_{t}|=t|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|
for all
0\leq t\leq 1
, and then
(
2.5
) becomes (
2.3
).
∎

At this point in the analysis of
[
28
]
, the authors make a vague invocation of linearity and jump to their erroneous conclusion. As we have seen, there are examples for which the map
symbol map
\gamma
is not affine. We now show that under the further assumption that
\Phi
is one-to-one, the symbol map is in fact affine.

2.2 LEMMA
.

Under the further assumption that
\Phi
is one-to-one, its symbol map
\gamma
is affine, and in particular,
D\gamma_{Q}
does not depend on
Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
.

Proof.

Because
\Phi
is one-to-one, the map
\gamma
is one-to-one. Indeed,
\gamma(Q)=\gamma(Q^{\prime})
if and only if
\Phi(\rho_{Q})=\Phi(\rho_{Q^{\prime}})
, and since
\Phi
is one-to-one,
this entails
\rho_{Q}=\rho_{Q^{\prime}}
, and then by the final part of Lemma
1.6
,
Q=Q^{\prime}
.

Fix any
Q_{0}\in\{Q\ :\ 0<Q<{\mathds{1}}\}
and any non-zero
\psi,\phi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Let
r>0
be such that
Q_{0}+s|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|+t|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|\in\{Q\ :\ 0<Q<{\mathds{1}}\}
for
s^{2}+t^{2}<r^{2}
. Define

(2.8)

\pm|\eta_{t}\rangle\langle\eta_{t}|:=D\gamma_{Q_{0}+t|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|}(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)\quad{\rm and}\quad\pm|\xi_{s}\rangle\langle\xi_{s}|:=D\gamma_{Q_{0}+s|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|}(|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)

which is justified because Lemma
2.1
says that for any
Q
,
D_{Q}
applied to a rank one projector yields a multiple of a rank one projector.

Using (
2.3
) twice

(2.9)

\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{0}+s|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|+t|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{0})+s|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)+tD\gamma_{Q_{0}+s|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|}(|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{0})+sD\gamma_{Q_{0}}(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)+tD\gamma_{Q_{0}+s|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|}(|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{0})\pm s|\eta_{0}\rangle\langle\eta_{0}|\pm t|\xi_{s}\rangle\langle\xi_{s}|\ ,

where the first sign is independent of
s
, and the second is independent of
t
.
In the same way,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{0}+s|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|+t|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{0})+t|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)+sD\gamma_{Q_{0}+t|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|}(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{0})+tD\gamma_{Q_{0}}(|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)+sD\gamma_{Q_{0}+t|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|}(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{0})\pm t|\xi_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}|\pm s|\eta_{t}\rangle\langle\eta_{t}|\ ,

where the first sign is independent of
t
, and the second is independent of
s
.
By continuity then, the signs that appear in
(
2.8
) do not depend on
s
or
t
for
s,t
sufficiently small.
It follows that for all such
s,t
,

(2.11)

s(|\eta_{0}\rangle\langle\eta_{0}|-|\eta_{t}\rangle\langle\eta_{t}|)=\pm t(|\xi_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}|-|\xi_{s}\rangle\langle\xi_{s}|)\

One possibility is that

(2.12)

|\eta_{t}\rangle\langle\eta_{t}|=|\eta_{0}\rangle\langle\eta_{0}|\quad{\rm and}\quad|\xi_{s}\rangle\langle\xi_{s}|=|\xi_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}|

for all sufficiently small
s,t
.
In fact, this is the only possibility. To see this,
divide both sides of (
2.11
) by
s
and take the limit
s\to 0
to conclude

|\eta_{t}\rangle\langle\eta_{t}|=|\eta_{0}\rangle\langle\eta_{0}|\pm t\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}|\xi_{s}\rangle\langle\xi_{s}|\bigg|_{s=0}\ .

The derivative on the right side has rank less than
2
if and only if for some real-valued differentiable function
f(s)
,
|\xi_{s}\rangle\langle\xi_{s}|=f(s)|\xi_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}|
.
Since the left side of (
2.11
) is linear in
s
, it must be that for some
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
f(s)=1+as
.
In the same way one sees that for some

b\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
|\eta_{t}\rangle\langle\eta_{t}|=(1+bt)|\eta_{0}\rangle\langle\eta_{0}|
. Now (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) become

(2.13)

\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{0}+s|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|+t|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{0})\pm s|\eta_{0}\rangle\langle\eta_{0}|+t(1+as)|\xi_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma(Q_{0})\pm s(1+tb)|\eta_{0}\rangle\langle\eta_{0}|+t|\xi_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}|\ .

It follows that
b|\eta_{0}\rangle\langle\eta_{0}|=a|\xi_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}|
.

If either one of
a
or
b
, is zero, then both are, and (
2.12
) is satisfied. Assume this is not the case. Then with
c=|a/b|
,

|\eta_{0}\rangle\langle\eta_{0}|=c|\xi_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}|
. Going back to (
2.9
), we can now rewrite it as

(2.14)

\gamma(Q_{0}+s|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|+t|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)=\gamma(Q_{0})\pm\left(cs\pm t(1+as)\right)|\xi_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}|\ .

This would mean that
\gamma(Q_{0}+s|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|+t|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)=\gamma(Q_{0})
whenever
t=\frac{cs}{1+as}
, and since
c>0
, this would mean that
\gamma
is not one-to-one. Therefore, under the hypothesis that
\Phi
is one-to-one, (
2.12
) is satisfied, and (
2.9
) becomes

(2.15)

\gamma(Q_{0}+s|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|+t|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)=\gamma(Q_{0})\pm s|\eta_{0}\rangle\langle\eta_{0}|\pm t|\xi_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}|\ .

Therefore, the Hessian of
\gamma
is constant on the interior of
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
.
∎

Define a real linear transformation
\Psi
on the space of self-adjoint operators on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
by

\Psi(H)=D_{\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathds{1}}}(H)\ .

By Lemma
2.2
, the choice of the reference point
\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathds{1}}
in
\{Q\>\ 0<Q<{\mathds{1}}\}
is arbitrary, we make this choice for convenience.
By Lemma
2.2
, when
\Phi
is one-to-one, there is a linear operator
\Psi
acting on the space of self-adjoint operators on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that for all
Q_{0},Q_{1}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}

(2.16)

\gamma(Q_{1})=\gamma(Q_{0})+\Psi(Q_{1}-Q_{0})\ .

This is because
\gamma
is an affine function by Lemma
2.2
.

Combining Lemma
2.1
and Lemma
2.2
, we have:

2.3 THEOREM
.

Let
\Phi
be a one-to-one linear map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. Then the symbol map
\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}
is affine, and hence has the form

(2.17)

\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}(Q)=\gamma_{\Phi}^{\phantom{I}}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathds{1}}\right)+\Psi\left(Q-\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathds{1}}\right)

for some real linear map
\Psi
on the self-adjoint operators on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Moreover,
\Psi
is one to one, and has the property that for all rank one projections
|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
,

(2.18)

\Psi(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)=\pm|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|\ .

for some
\eta\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.

Any real linear map on the self-adjoint operators on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
has a unique extension to a linear operator on
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})
given by

\Psi(H+iK)=\Psi(H)+i\Psi(K)

for self-adjoint
H
and
K
. Evidently, this extension, also denoted by
\Psi
, is Hermitian; that is for all
X
,
\Psi(X^{*})=\Psi(X)^{*}
.

With Theorem
2.3
at our disposal, it remains to determine the structure of maps
\Psi
satisfying
(
2.18
). The first question concerns the choice of signs in (
2.18
), which we deal with in the Lemma
2.4
below.
The troublesome aspect of the results proven so far is the possibility of both positive or negative signs, as in (
2.3
). The next lemma gives conditions under which the signs must be consistent, so that either
\Phi
is positivity preserving, or
-\Phi
is positivity preserving. This puts more tools at our disposal such as Kadison’s Schwarz inequality for positive maps, which will be used in the proof of the main theorem of this section.

2.4 LEMMA
.

Let
\Psi
be an Hermitian linear map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})
that maps rank one projectors onto multiples of rank one projectors. That is, for every unit vector
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
there is a unit vector
\zeta\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and a
t\in R
such that
\Psi(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)=t|\zeta\rangle\langle\zeta|
.
If there are vectors
\psi
and
\varphi
such that

(2.19)

\Psi(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)=|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|>0\quad{\rm and}\quad\Psi(|\varphi\rangle\langle\varphi|)=-|\zeta\rangle\langle\zeta|<0\ ,

then there exists a vector
\xi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and a self-adjoint operator
A
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that for all operators
X
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

(2.20)

\Psi(X)={\rm Tr}[AX]|\xi\rangle\langle\xi|\ .

Consequently, if
\Psi
does not have the form (
2.20
), in particular, if
\Psi
is one-to-one, then either
\Psi
is positivity preserving or else
-\Psi
is positivity preserving.

Proof.

For all
t\geq 0
,

(2.21)

\Psi(|\psi+t\varphi\rangle\langle\psi+t\varphi|)=|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|-t^{2}|\zeta\rangle\langle\zeta|+t\Psi(|\psi\rangle\langle\varphi|+|\varphi\rangle\langle\psi|)\ .

Define
f(t)={\rm Tr}[\Psi(|\psi+t\varphi\rangle\langle\psi+t\varphi|)]
. By hypothesis
f(0)>0
while for all
t
sufficiently large,
f(t)<0
. By the Intermediate Value Thoerem, there exists
t_{0}\in(0,\infty)
such that
f(t_{0})=0
, and then since
\Psi(|\psi+t_{0}\varphi\rangle\langle\psi+t_{0}\varphi|)
is self-adjoint and rank, one

(2.22)

\Psi(|\psi+t_{0}\varphi\rangle\langle\psi+t_{0}\varphi|)=0\ .

Then by (
2.21
),
\Psi(|\psi\rangle\langle\varphi|+|\varphi\rangle\langle\psi|)=\frac{1}{t_{0}}|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|-t_{0}|\zeta\rangle\langle\zeta|
, and hence for all
t>0
,

\Psi(|\psi+t\varphi\rangle\langle\psi+t\varphi|)=\left(1+\frac{t}{t_{0}}\right)|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|-(t^{2}+tt_{0})|\zeta\rangle\langle\zeta|\ .

By hypothesis, this is rank one for all
t
, which is possible if and only if and only if
\zeta
is a multiple of
\eta
. Therefore, for some constant
c_{\varphi}
, (
2.19
) becomes

(2.23)

\Psi(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)=|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|>0\quad{\rm and}\quad\Psi(|\varphi\rangle\langle\varphi|)=-c_{\varphi}|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|<0\ ,

and this is true for any vector
\varphi
for which
\Psi(|\varphi\rangle\langle\varphi|)\leq 0
.
Then by symmetry we must also have
\Psi(|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)=c_{\phi}|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|
for all
\phi
such that
\Psi(|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)>0
.

Since
\Psi
is Hermitian, it is determined by its action on self-adjoint operators
X
. Let
X=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}|\phi_{j}\rangle\langle\phi_{j}|
be an eigenvector-eigenvalue decomposition of
X
. Then by what we have just proved,

\Psi(X)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}c_{j}|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|\ .

The map
X\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}c_{j}
is a linear functional because
\Psi
is linear, and therefore it can be written in the form
X\mapsto{\rm Tr}[AX]
for some operator
A
on

{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and
A
must be self-adjoint because otherwise
\Psi
would not be Hermitian. The final statement is clear.
∎

By Theorem
2.3
and Lemma
2.4
, the symbol map of a one to one quantum operation
\Phi
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself is affine
and its constant derivative
\Psi
is either positivity preserving or else
-\Psi
is positivity preserving.
Since the symbol map of
\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm ph}}
is
Q\mapsto{\mathds{1}}-Q^{T}
, where the transpose is computed with respect to the preferred complex conjugation

C
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, if the derivative
\Psi
corresponding to
\Phi
is no positivity preserving, then that of
\Phi\circ\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm ph}}
will be, so exactly as in the case of the
Hugenholtz-Kadison Theorem, we need only consider the case that
\Phi
is positivity preserving. The following theorem completely specifies the structure of
\Psi
in this case.

2.5 THEOREM
.

Let
\Psi
be an invertible positive linear map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})
such that for all
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,
\Psi(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)=|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|
for some
\eta\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.
Then there is a linear operator
A
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that for all operators
X
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

\Psi(X)=AXA^{*}\ ,

or else
for all all operators
X
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

\Psi(X)=AX^{T}A^{*}

where
T
is the transpose associated to the complex conjugation on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

Let
\{\psi_{1},\dots\psi_{N}\}
be any real basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Then by hypothesis, there are vectors
\{\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{N}\}
such that for each
j=1,\dots,N
,

(2.24)

\Psi(|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|\ ,

but these vectors are not uniquely determined;
we may replace each
\eta_{j}
by
e^{i\theta_{j}}\eta_{j}
,
\theta_{j}\in[0,2\pi)
without affecting (
2.24
).

Make some choice. We now claim that no matter how the phases have been chosen, for each
j\neq k
, there is a
w_{j,k}\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
|w|=1
such that

(2.25)

\Psi(|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|+|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=\overline{w_{j,k}}|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+w_{j,k}|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|\ .

To see this, for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
define
H_{t}:=|\psi_{k}+t\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}+t\psi_{j}|
.
Then

(2.26)

\Psi(H_{t})=|\xi_{t}\rangle\langle\xi_{t}|

where
\xi_{0}=\eta_{k}
, and we fix the phase on
\xi_{t}
by requiring that
\langle\eta_{k},\xi_{t}\rangle\geq 0
.

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}H_{t}\big|_{t=0}=\Psi(|\psi_{1}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|+|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{1}|)=:S\ .

Then since

(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|+|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)^{2}=|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|+|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|
,
Kadison’s inequality says

S^{2}=\left(\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|+|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)\right)^{2}\leq\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|+|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)\leq|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|\ .

It follows that the range of
S
is
{\rm span}(\{\eta_{j},\eta_{k}\})
, which is two dimensional since otherwise
\Psi
could not be one-to-one.

Going back to (
2.26
),

S=|\xi_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}^{\prime}|+|\xi^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}|=|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\xi_{0}^{\prime}|+|\xi^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|
.
It then follows that for some
z,w\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
\xi^{\prime}_{0}=z\eta_{k}+w\eta_{j}
. Therefore,

S=2\Re(z)|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+\bar{w}|\eta_{1}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+w|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|\ .

A similar argument with the roles of
\psi_{k}
and
\psi_{j}
interchanged yields that for some other
z,w\in{\mathbb{C}}
,

S=2\Re(z)|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+\bar{w}|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+w|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|\ .

By linear independence, the coefficients of
|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|
and
|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|
must vanish, and so for some
w\in{\mathbb{C}}
,

S=\bar{w}|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+w|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|\ .

This proves (
2.25
).

Now we leave the phase on
\eta_{1}
as it was chosen, but will change the phases, if needed, on
\eta_{j}
for
j>1
.
We have proved that for each
j>1
, there is
w_{j}\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
|w_{j}|=1
such that

(2.27)

\Psi(|\psi_{1}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|+|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{1}|)=\overline{w_{j}}|\eta_{1}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+w_{j}|\eta_{1}\rangle\langle\eta_{1}|\ .

Replace
\eta_{j}
by
w_{j}\eta_{j}
, and then

(2.28)

\Psi(|\psi_{1}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|+|\psi_{1}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=|\eta_{1}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{1}|\ .

We now claim that with this choice of the phases,

(2.29)

\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|+|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|\

is satisfied for all
j,k
.
To see this, compute using (
2.25
):

\displaystyle\Psi(|\psi_{1}+\psi_{j}+\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{1}+\psi_{j}+\psi_{k}|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\eta_{1}\rangle\langle\eta_{1}|+|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle|\eta_{1}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{1}|+|\eta_{1}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{1}|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\bar{w}|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+w|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\eta_{1}+\eta_{j}+w\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{1}+\eta_{j}+w\eta_{k}|

\displaystyle+
