\displaystyle(1-w)(|\eta_{1}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{1}|)

Since
\Psi(|\psi_{1}+\psi_{j}+\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{1}+\psi_{j}+\psi_{k}|)
is rank one, it must be that
w=1
. Thus choosing the relative phases with respect to
\eta_{1}
so that (
2.28
) is true for all
j
ensures that (
2.29
) is true for all
j
and
k
.

Now replacing
\psi_{j}
by
i\psi_{j}
, the argument leading to (
2.24
) yields

\Psi(-i|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|+i|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)=\overline{z_{j,k}}|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+z_{j,k}|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|

for some
z_{j,k}\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
|z_{j,k}|=1
.
Since
\Psi
is linear,

(2.30)

\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|-|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)=i\overline{z_{j,k}}|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+iz_{j,k}|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|\ ,

and hence

\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=\frac{1+i\overline{z_{j,k}}}{2}|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+\frac{1+iz_{j,k}}{2}|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|

Since
\Psi
is positive, and hence Hermitian,
\Psi(|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)=\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)^{*}
, and hence

\Psi(|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)=\frac{1-iz_{j,k}}{2}|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+\frac{1-i\overline{z_{j,k}}}{2}|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|\ .

Now let
w\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
|w|=1
. Then

\displaystyle\Psi(|\psi_{k}+w\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}+w\psi_{j}|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+\bar{w}\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)+w\Psi(|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+\bar{w}\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)+w\Psi(|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+\bar{\alpha}|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+\alpha|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|

where

\alpha=\Re(w)+\Im(w)z_{j,k}\ .

Since
|w|=1
,
|\alpha|=1
if and only if
z_{j,k}=\pm i
, and unless
|\alpha|=1
,
\Psi(|\psi_{k}+w\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}+w\psi_{j}|)
would not be rank one.
For
z=i
, (
2.30
) becomes

(2.31)

\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|-|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)=|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|-|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|\ .

Combining (
2.29
) with (
2.31
) yields

(2.32)

\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|\ ,

and since
\Psi
is Hermitian, this is the same as
\Psi(|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)=|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|
.

For
z=-i
, (
2.30
) becomes

(2.33)

\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|-|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)=-|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|\ .

Combining (
2.33
) with (
2.31
) yields

(2.34)

\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|\ .

and since
\Psi
is Hermitian, this is the same as
\Psi(|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)=|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|
.

This shows that all distinct
j,k
, either,

(2.35)

\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|\quad{\rm or}\quad\Psi(|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|\ .

We now claim that either for any
\ell
either
\Psi(|\psi_{\ell}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=|\eta_{\ell}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|
for all
j
or else

\Psi(|\psi_{\ell}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{\ell}|
for all
j
. Suppose not. Then evidently
N\geq 3
and for some
j
and
k
,

\Psi(|\psi_{\ell}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|)=|\eta_{\ell}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|\quad{\rm while}\quad\Psi(|\psi_{\ell}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)=|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{\ell}|\ .

There are two possibilities for
\Psi(|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)
; either it equals
|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|
or it equals
|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|
.
Either way, for
\ell\neq j,k
.

\displaystyle\Psi(|i\psi_{\ell}+\psi_{j}+\psi_{k}\rangle\langle i\psi_{\ell}+\psi_{j}+\psi_{k}|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\eta_{\ell}\rangle\langle\eta_{\ell}|+|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|+|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle i(|\eta_{\ell}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|-|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{\ell}|)+i(|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{\ell}|-(|\eta_{\ell}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|+|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|i\eta_{\ell}+\eta_{j}+\eta_{k}\rangle\langle i\eta_{\ell}+\eta_{j}+\eta_{k}|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2i(|\eta_{\ell}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|-|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\eta_{\ell}|)\ ,

which is not rank one,
and this contradiction proves the claim.

Now suppose that
\Psi(|\psi_{1}\rangle\langle\psi_{2}|)=|\eta_{1}\rangle\langle\eta_{2}|
. Then by what was just proved,
\Psi(|\psi_{1}\rangle\langle\psi_{\ell}|)=|\eta_{1}\rangle\langle\eta_{\ell}|

for all
\ell
. Since
\Psi
is Hermitian,
\Psi(|\psi_{\ell}\rangle\langle\psi_{1}|)=|\eta_{\ell}\rangle\langle\eta_{1}|
for all
\ell
. Then once again by what was proved just above,

\Psi(|\psi_{\ell}\rangle\langle\psi_{m}|)=|\eta_{\ell}\rangle\langle\eta_{m}|
for all
\ell
and
m
. The same argument shows that if

\Psi(|\psi_{1}\rangle\langle\psi_{2}|)=|\eta_{2}\rangle\langle\eta_{1}|
, then
\Psi(|\psi_{\ell}\rangle\langle\psi_{m}|)=|\eta_{m}\rangle\langle\eta_{\ell}|
for all
\ell
and
m
.

Define an operator
A
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
by

A\varphi=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\langle\psi_{j},\varphi\rangle\eta_{j}\ .

The operator
A
depends on the choice of overall phase in
\{\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{N}\}
, but
|A\varphi\rangle\langle A\varphi|
does not. Since

|\varphi\rangle\langle\varphi|=\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}\langle\varphi,\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j},\varphi\rangle|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|\ ,

if it is the case that
\Psi(|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)=|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|
for all
j,k
, then

\Psi(|\varphi\rangle\langle\varphi|)=\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}\langle\varphi,\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j},\varphi\rangle|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|=|A\varphi\rangle\langle A\varphi|=A|\varphi\rangle\langle\varphi|A^{*}\ .

However, if it is the case that
\Psi(|\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|)=|\eta_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|
for all
j,k
, then since

|\bar{\varphi}\rangle\langle\bar{\varphi}|=\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}\langle\varphi,\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j},\varphi\rangle|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}|\ ,

\Psi(|\varphi\rangle\langle\varphi|)=\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}\langle\varphi,\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{j},\varphi\rangle|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{k}|=|A\bar{\varphi}\rangle\langle A\bar{\varphi}|=A(|\varphi\rangle\langle\varphi|)^{T}A^{*}\ .

∎

2.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.19

Proof of Theorem
1.19
.

By Lemma
2.2
, and Lemma
2.4
,
\gamma(Q)=\gamma(0)+\Psi(Q)
and either
\Psi
is positivity preserving, or else
-\Psi
is. In the first case, since the symbol of
\omega_{0}
is
0
and the symbol of
\omega_{1}
is
{\mathds{1}}
,
\gamma(\omega_{1})\geq\gamma(\omega_{0})
, while in the second case,

\gamma(\omega_{1})\leq\gamma(\omega_{0})
. If both were true, we would have
\gamma(\omega_{1})=\gamma(\omega_{0})
, but this is impossible since
\gamma
is one-to-one.

Then by Theorem
2.5
,
\gamma(Q)=\gamma(0)+AQA^{*}
for some operator
A
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Since the range of
\gamma
lies in
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,
Q(0)\geq 0
and

Q(0)+AA^{*}\leq{\mathds{1}}
. In particular
A
is a contraction. Write
S
to denote this contraction, and
R
to denote
\gamma(0)
. Then
(S,R)
is a compatible pair, and so the Evans-Hugenholtz-Kadison map
\Phi_{S,R}
for this
R
and
S
has
\gamma
as its symbol map. Because symbol maps completely determine linear maps on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

\Phi\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}=\Phi_{R,S}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}

is a quantum operation operation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
which has an extension, namely
\Phi_{S,R}
, to all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that is a GIG quantum operation.

On the other hand, if
\gamma(\omega_{1})\leq\gamma(\omega_{0})
, then by the same reasoning,
\gamma(Q)=\gamma(0)-AQ^{T}A^{*}
. Again,
R:=Q(0)\geq 0
and

S:=A
is a contraction with
R+SS^{*}\leq{\mathds{1}}
. Then
\Phi_{S,R}\circ\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
is a linear map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
with this symbol, and hence

\Phi\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}=\Phi_{R,S}\circ\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}\ .

As we have seen,
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
is the transpose map, and hence
\Phi_{R,S}\circ\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
is co-CP.
Of course, the composition of a CP map and the transpose can be completely positive: consider the case in which the completely positive map is
X\mapsto\tau(X){\mathds{1}}
,
which is even a GIG quantum operation. However, this only happens in case the completely positive map, as in this example, is not invertible.

More specifically, in our context, suppose that
S
is an invertible contraction. Then
\Phi_{S,R}
for and compatible
R
is invertible, and hence so is its Hilbert-Schmidt adjoint
\Phi_{S,R}^{\dagger}
. Then

(\Phi_{S,R}\circ\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}})^{\dagger}=\lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\Phi_{S,R}^{\dagger}
. Because
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
is not completely positive while
\Phi_{S,R}^{\dagger}
is completely positive and invertible,
(\Phi_{S,R}\circ\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}})^{\dagger}
is not completely positive, and hence neither is
\Phi_{S,R}\circ\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}})^{\dagger}
.

The remaining statements follow from this analysis using the fact that the symbol map of
\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm ph}}
is
Q\mapsto{\mathds{1}}-Q^{T}
.
∎

3.
The Evans-Hugenholtz-Kadison construction

3.1.
The EHK construction in general

The first part of this section is expository, serving largely to establish notation and provided a convenient summary of known results, but the theorems in the second subsection are new.

Recall that a
compatible pair

(S,R)
consists of a contraction
S
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and an operator
R
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
satisfying
0\leq R\leq{\mathds{1}}-SS^{*}
. This section is devoted to the
Evans-Hugenholtz-Kadison (EHK) construction of a GIG quantum operation
\Phi_{S,R}
on the CAR algebra
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself and has the symbol map

\gamma_{\Phi_{S,R}}(Q)=R+QSQS^{*}
.

We begin this section with a technical lemma that provides an alternate parameterization of the set of compatible pairs
(S,R)
that is more adapted to the EHK construction.

3.1 LEMMA
.

For every compatible pair
(S,R)
, on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, there is a unique
T\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
such that
{\ker}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})\subseteq{\rm ker}(T)
and
R=({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}T({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}
.

Proof.

Let
r
be the rank of
{\mathds{1}}-SS^{*}
, which, to avoid trivialities, we assume to be at least one, and let
\sum_{j=1}^{r}\lambda_{j}|\phi_{j}\rangle\langle\phi_{j}|
be a spectral decomposition of
({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})
. Then the
Moore-Penrose generalized inverse
of
({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})
,
({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{+}
, is defined by

(3.1)

({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{+}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\lambda_{j}^{-1}|\phi_{j}\rangle\langle\phi_{j}|\ .

Then define

(3.2)

T:=\left(({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{+}\right)^{1/2}R\left(({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{+}\right)^{1/2}\

Then evidently,
R=({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}T({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}
, and
{\ker}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})\subseteq{\rm ker}(T)
. Since
R\leq({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})
,
{\ker}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})\subseteq{\rm ker}(R)
.
Then since
({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{+}
is the inverse of
({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})
restricted to
{\ker}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{\perp}
, there is a unique operator
T
on
{\ker}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{\perp}
such that

R=({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}T({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}
, and it is given by (
3.2
). It remains to show that
T\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
.
Then for any
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

\displaystyle\langle\psi,T\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}\langle\psi,\phi_{j}\rangle\langle\phi_{k},\psi\rangle\lambda_{j}^{-1/2}\lambda_{k}^{-1/2}\langle R^{1/2}\phi_{j},R^{12}\phi_{k}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{r}|\langle\psi,\phi_{j}\rangle|^{2}\lambda_{j}^{-1}\langle\phi_{j},R\phi_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{r}|\langle\psi,\phi_{j}\rangle|^{2}\lambda_{j}^{-1}\langle\phi_{j},({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})\phi_{j}\rangle\leq\|\psi\|^{2}\ .

Therefore,
T\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
.
∎

Given a CAR field
Z
over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, extend it to the CAR field over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
acting on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}\otimes{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
by

(3.3)

Z(\psi\oplus\eta)=Z(\psi)\otimes W+{\mathds{1}}\otimes Z(\eta)\

where
W=e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}{\mathcal{N}}}
.
To simplify the notation below, we shall write

(3.4)

A(\psi):=Z(\psi\oplus 0)\quad{\rm and}\quad B(\psi):=Z(0\oplus\psi)\ ,

and write
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}
and
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}
be the CAR sub-algebras of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
generated by
\{A(\psi):\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\}
and

\{B(\psi):\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\}
respectively.

Let
S
be a contraction on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Let
V(S^{*}S)^{1/2}
be the polar decomposition of
S
where we take
V
to be unitary in case
S
has a non-trivial null-space.
Then
({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}=V({\mathds{1}}-S^{*}S)^{1/2}V^{*}
, from which it easily follows that
({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}S=S({\mathds{1}}-S^{*}S)^{1/2}
.
Therefore, the block-matrix operator

(3.5)

U_{S}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}-({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}&S\\
S^{*}&({\mathds{1}}-S^{*}S)^{1/2}\end{array}\right]

is a self-adjoint unitary on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Let
J_{1}:{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
be the partial isometry
J_{1}(\psi)=(\psi,0)
. Likewise define
J_{2}(\psi)=(0,\psi)
.
Then
S=J_{2}^{*}UJ_{1}
. This dilation of
S
was used by Evans
[
26
]
who cites Halmos
[
38
]
.

Let
{\mathcal{U}}_{U_{S}}
be the second-quantization of
U_{S}
, and let
let
\rho_{T}
be the density matrix in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}
of the GIG Gaussian state on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}
with symbol
T
. These are the basic ingredients of the EHK construction, which proceeds a follows:
Let
\rho
be any density matrix in
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}
.
Then
\rho_{T}
commutes with
\rho
so that
\rho_{T}\rho
is positive semi-definite. By the product property of the tracial state, Corollary
A.8
,
\tau(\rho_{T}\rho)=\tau(\rho_{T})\tau(\rho)=1
, and hence

\rho_{R}\rho
is a density matrix on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}\otimes{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
. By (
3.3
), as an operator on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}\otimes{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
,
\rho_{B}\rho
may be identified with

\rho_{T}\otimes\rho
. Therefore, the operation
\rho\mapsto\rho_{T}\rho
corresponds to the quantum operation of adding ancilla in state
\rho_{T}
.
We obtain a quantum operation by composing this with two more quantum operations, namely the quasi-free automorphism
\widehat{\alpha}_{U_{S}}
on

{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and the tracial conditional expectation onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus 0,\tau}
, Literally, this gives us a quantum operation mapping
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}={\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}={\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus 0}
, but we may identify both with

{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
as generated by the original CAR field
Z
with which we started.

3.2 DEFINITION
.

Let
(S,R)
be a compatible pair on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Let
T_{S,R}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
be specified in terms of
S
and
R
as in Lemma
3.1
. Then
\Phi_{S,R}
is the quantum operation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
given by

(3.6)

\Phi_{S,R}(\rho):={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus 0,\tau}(\widehat{\alpha}_{U_{S}}(\rho_{T_{S,R}}\rho))\ ,

and is the
EHK quantum operation determined by the compatible pair

(S,R)
.

To simplify the notation in what follows, we write
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}}
in place of
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus 0,\tau}
.
This construction is due to Evans
[
26
]
, building on earlier work of Hugenholtz and Kadison
[
39
]
who considered only the cases
T=0
corresponding the the vacuum state, and
T={\mathds{1}}
corresponding to the anti-vacuum state. Slightly later than Hugenholtz and Kadison, but independently, Schrader and Uhlenbrock
[
57
]
, building on work of Gross
[
35
]
, gave a related construction based on the tracial state. Their work was aimed at constructing “doubly stochastic” operations in the sense of Nelson
[
50
]
. It seems that the idea of extending the “second quantization functor” from untaries to contractions was in the air in the early 1970’s.

3.3 LEMMA
.

For every compatible pair
(S,R)
, the EHK quantum operation
\Phi_{S,R}
defined in (
3.6
) is gauge covariant.

Proof.

To simplify the notation, write
T
for
T_{S,R}
. Since
\rho_{T}
is gauge invariant, so that for all
t

\widehat{\alpha}_{e^{it}{\mathds{1}}}(\rho_{T})=\rho_{T}
, for all density matrices
\rho\in{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}
,

\rho_{B}\widehat{\alpha}_{e^{it}{\mathds{1}}}(\rho)=\widehat{\alpha}_{e^{it}{\mathds{1}}\oplus{\mathds{1}}}(\rho_{T}\rho)
.
Since
U_{S}
commutes with
e^{it}{\mathds{1}}\oplus{\mathds{1}}
,

\widehat{\alpha}_{e^{it}{\mathds{1}}}
commutes with

\widehat{\alpha}_{U_{S}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Phi_{S,T}(\widehat{\alpha}_{e^{it}{\mathds{1}}}(\rho))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}}(\widehat{\alpha}_{e^{it}{\mathds{1}}\oplus{\mathds{1}}}(\widehat{\alpha}_{U_{S}}(\rho_{T}\rho)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{e^{it}{\mathds{1}}}\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}}(\widehat{\alpha}_{e^{it}0\oplus{\mathds{1}}}(\widehat{\alpha}_{U_{S}}(\rho_{T}\rho)))\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{e^{it}{\mathds{1}}}\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}}((\widehat{\alpha}_{U_{S}}(\rho_{T}\rho))\right)\ .

where in the final equality we have used the fact that
\widehat{\alpha}_{e^{it}0\oplus{\mathds{1}}}
is given by a unitary conjugation with a unitary acting only on the first factor in

{\mathcal{K}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
, and then the partial cyclicity of the partial trace.
∎

3.4 LEMMA
.

For every compatible pair
(S,R)
, the EHK quantum operation
\Phi_{S,R}
defined in (
3.6
)
maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, and its symbol map is

(3.7)

Q\mapsto R+SQS^{*}\ .

Proof.

Let
T
be given in terms of
S
and
R
by Lemma
3.1
.
If
\rho=\rho_{Q}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
, then
\rho_{T}\rho_{Q}
is the GIG state on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
with symbol
T\oplus Q
and then

{\mathcal{U}}_{U_{S}}\rho_{T}\rho_{Q}{\mathcal{U}}_{U_{S}}
is the GIG state with symbol
U_{S}(T\oplus Q)U_{S}^{*}
. Finally then, the symbol of
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}(\widehat{\alpha}_{U_{S}}(\rho_{T}\rho))
is

J_{1}^{*}U_{S}(T\oplus Q)U_{S}^{*}J_{1}
. By the form of
U_{S}
,

\displaystyle\langle\phi,J_{1}^{*}U_{S}(Q\oplus R)U_{S}^{*}J_{1}\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi\\
S^{*}\phi\end{array}\right),\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}T&0\\
0&Q\end{array}\right]\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi\\
S^{*}\psi\end{array}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\phi,({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{\frac{1}{2}}T({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi\rangle+\langle\phi,SQS^{*}\psi\rangle\ .

This proves (
3.7
).
∎

Some properties of
\Phi_{S,R}
are most understood in terms of its Hilbert-Schmidt adjoint,
\Phi_{S,R}^{\dagger}
with respect to the inner product
\langle X,Y\rangle_{GNS,\tau}:=\tau(X^{*}Y)
.
The adjoint of
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}}
is the embedding of
{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus 0}
into
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. Since
U_{S}
is
self-adjoint,
\alpha_{U_{S}}
is self-adjoint. Finally, the adjoint of the “adjoining ancilla” map
\rho\mapsto\rho_{T_{S,R}}\rho
is the map

A\mapsto{\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1},\tau}(\rho_{T_{S,R}\oplus 0}A)={\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1},\tau}(A\rho_{T_{S,R}\oplus 0})={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}}(A\rho_{T_{S,R}\oplus 0})
.
Altogether then,
\Phi_{S,R}^{\dagger}

is the
completely positive unital (CPU) map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}
given by

(3.9)

\Phi_{S,R}^{\dagger}(X):={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}}(\alpha_{U_{S}}(X)\rho_{T_{R,S}\oplus 0})

where on the right side,
X
is regarded as belonging to the sub-algebra
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}={\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus 0}
of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. Literally, this gives us a map from
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}
, but we may identify both with
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, and do so.

For example,

(3.10)

\alpha_{U_{S}}(A(\psi))=-A(({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}\psi)+B(S^{*}\psi)\ ,

and hence,
\Phi^{\dagger}_{S,R}(A(\psi))=B(S^{*}\psi)
, or, after identifying
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}
and
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}
with
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(3.11)

\Phi^{\dagger}_{S,R}(Z(\psi))=Z(S^{*}\psi)\ ,

completely independent of
R
.

Next, consider
A^{*}(\psi)A(\phi)
. Then using (
3.10
) and
R=({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}T_{S,R}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{U_{S}}(A^{*}(\psi)A(\phi))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-A^{*}(({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}\psi)+B^{*}(S^{*}\psi))(-A(({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}\phi)+B(S^{*}\phi))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B^{*}(S^{*}\psi)B(S^{*}\phi)+A^{*}(({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}\psi)A(({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}\phi)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle{\rm terms\ that\ are\ odd\ in}\ {\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}\ .

Therefore,
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}}\bigl(\rho_{T_{R,S}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(A^{*}(\psi)A(\phi))\bigr)=B^{*}(S^{*}\psi)B(S^{*}\phi)+\langle\phi,R\psi\rangle{\mathds{1}}

and hence, after identifying
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}
and
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}
with
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(3.12)

\Phi^{\dagger}_{S,R}(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\phi))=Z^{*}(S^{*}\psi)Z(S^{*}\phi)+\langle\phi,R\psi\rangle{\mathds{1}}\ .

3.2.
An important special case

The formulas simplify further if
S=e^{-H}
,
H\geq 0
.
[H,R]=0
, or, what is the same thing,
[H,T_{S,R}]=0
. To simplify the notation below, we write
T
for
T_{S,R}
.
Then
R=({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T
, and we shall be studying
\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}
.

When
S=e^{-H}
for
H\geq 0
,
the dilation
U_{S}
of
S
given by (
3.5
) simplifies to

(3.13)

U_{e^{-H}}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}-({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})^{1/2}&e^{-H}\\
e^{-H}&({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})^{1/2}\end{array}\right]\ .

Let
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
consisting of eigenvectors of
H
;
H\psi_{j}=\lambda_{j}\psi_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,N
.
In the orthonormal basis
\{\psi_{1}\oplus 0,0\oplus\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{N}\oplus 0,0\oplus\psi_{N}\}
,
U_{e^{-H}}
has a block diagonal form with
2\times 2
blocks, the
j^{{\rm th}}
of which is

\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}-(1-e^{-2\lambda_{j}})^{1/2}&e^{-\lambda_{j}}\\
e^{-\lambda_{j}}&(1-e^{-2\lambda_{j}})^{1/2}\end{array}\right]\ .

The formula (
3.9
) becomes

(3.14)

\Phi_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H}T)}^{\dagger}(X)={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}}(\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(X)\rho_{T\oplus 0})

where
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is regarded as an element of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}={\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus 0}
.

The following is due to Evans, who proves a slightly more general result. (See the discussion around (3.3) in
[
26
]
).

3.5 LEMMA
.

Under the assumption that
H
and
T
commute, there is a symmetry between the roles of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus 0}
in the formula
(
3.14
). If instead we regard
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
as an element of
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}={\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, we have the equivalent expression

(3.15)

\Phi_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H}T)}^{\dagger}(X)={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}}(\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(X)\rho_{0\oplus T})\ .

Moreover, for all
X,Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(3.16)

\rho_{T}\bigl(X^{*}\Phi_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H}T)}^{\dagger}(Y)\bigr)=\rho_{T}\bigl((\Phi_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H}T)}^{\dagger}(X))^{*}Y\bigr)\ .

Consequently,
\Phi_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H}T)}^{\dagger}
is self-adjoint with respect to the GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{T}
.

3.6 Remark
.

The GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{T}
is only non-degenerate when
0<T<{\mathds{1}}
, but (
3.16
) is valid for all
T\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
.

Proof.

Under the assumption that
S=e^{-H}
,
(
3.10
) becomes

(3.17)

\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(A(\psi))=-A(({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})^{1/2}\psi)+B(e^{-H}\psi)\ ,

and we also have

(3.18)

\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(B(\psi))=A(e^{-H}\psi)+B(({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})^{1/2}\psi)\ .

Apart from the the minus sign present in (
3.17
), but not present in (
3.18
),
swapping roles of the
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}
and
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}
algebras changes the one formula into the other.

However, this minus sign plays no role in the computation of the right side of either (
3.14
) or (
3.15
):
When we apply
\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}
to a monomial
X
in
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}
, we replace each term in the monomial according to
(
3.18
). To compute the right side of (
3.15
) we expand, and in each term of the sum, move all of the
B
terms to the left of all of the
A
terms, using the CAR, and take the expectation of the the resulting product of the
B
terms in the state
\rho_{T}
. Since
\rho_{T}
is a GIG state, the terms
B(({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})^{1/2}\psi)
enter only quadratically,
just as the terms
-A(({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})^{1/2}\psi)
enter only quadratically when computing the right side of (
3.14
), and hence the minus sign drops out, so that there is complete symmetry. This proves that (
3.15
) is equivalent to (
3.14
).

To prove (
3.16
), we compute

(3.19)

\displaystyle\rho_{T}\left(X^{*}\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(Y)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(X^{*}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}}(\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(X)\rho_{T\oplus 0})\rho_{0\oplus T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}}(X^{*}\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(X)\rho_{T\oplus 0})\rho_{0\oplus T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(\rho_{T\oplus T}X^{*}\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(Y))\ .

Because
U_{e^{-H}}^{2}={\mathds{1}}
and
H
commutes with
T
,
U_{e^{-H}}(T\oplus T)U_{e^{-H}}=T\oplus T
, and therefore
\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(\rho_{T\oplus T})=\rho_{T\oplus T}
. Returning to (
3.19
), and using
\alpha^{2}_{U_{e^{-H}}}={\mathds{1}}
,

\displaystyle\tau(\rho_{T\oplus T}X^{*}\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(Y))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(\rho_{T\oplus T}X^{*}\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(Y)))=\tau(\rho_{T\oplus T}\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(X^{*})Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{T}\bigl((\Phi_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H}T)}^{\dagger}(X))^{*}Y\bigr)\ .

∎

There is more that one can say under these hypotheses.
Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be any non-trivial subspace of
{\mathord{\mathscr{H}}}_{1}
that is invariant under
H
. Then, it is clear from (
3.13
) that
{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}
are invariant under
U_{e^{-H}}
. It follows that if
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}}
and
Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
, then

(3.20)

\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(XY)=\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(X)\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(Y)

where
\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(X)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}}
and
\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(Y)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
.

Now suppose that
[H,T]=0
, and that
{\mathcal{H}}
is invariant under
T
as well as
H
, so that
T=T_{\mathcal{H}}\oplus T_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
as an operator on
{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}
. Then there is a factorization of the GIG density matrix
\rho_{T}
of the form

\rho_{T}=\rho_{T_{\mathcal{H}}\oplus T_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}=\rho_{T_{\mathcal{H}}}\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}

and
\rho_{T_{\mathcal{H}}}={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{T})
and
\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp},\tau}(\rho_{T})
. Note that since
\rho_{T_{\mathcal{H}}}
is even, it belongs to the commutant of

{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
, and likewise,
\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}
belongs to the commutant of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
. In fact.
regarded as an element of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}}
,
\rho_{{\mathcal{H}}}
belongs to the
commutant of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
. Likewise,
\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}
belongs to the commutant of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}}
.
Then from (
3.20
),

(3.21)

\rho_{T}\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(XY)=\rho_{T_{\mathcal{H}}}\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(X)\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(Y)\ .

This factorization leads easily to a factorization property for
\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}
:

3.7 THEOREM
.

Let
H
be positive semidefinite on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and let
T\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
be such that
{\rm\ker}(H)\subseteq{\rm ker}(T)
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a non-trivial subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
invariant under both
H
and
T
. Let
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
and
Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
.
Then

(3.22)

\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(XY)=\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(X)\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(Y)\ .

Proof.

Identify
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
with a element of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\oplus 0}
in the obvious way. Likewise, identify
Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
with a element of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}\oplus 0}
.
By definition,

\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(XY)={\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1},\tau}(\rho_{T}\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(XY))

Then by (
3.21
) and then Theorem
A.9
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1},\tau}(\rho_{T}\alpha_{U_{e^{-H}}}(XY))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1},\tau}(\rho_{T_{\mathcal{H}}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(X)\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(Y))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}},\tau}\left(\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(X)\right){\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp},\tau}\left(\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(Y)\right)

Now we claim that

(3.23)

{\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1},\tau}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}},\tau}={\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}},\tau}\

This is a consequence of Corollary
A.16
.
Because the symbol of the tracial state
\tau
is
Q=\frac{1}{2}{\mathds{1}}
, the requirement of invariance under the symbol
Q
is automatically satisfied. We apply the Corollary in
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
to the pair of subspaces
{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}
and
0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. The orthogonal projections onto these subspaces clearly commute, and their intersection is
0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}
. Therefore,
(
3.23
) is a consequence of Corollary
A.16
.

Then since
\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(X)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}}
so that
\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(X)={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(X))
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}},\tau}\left(\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(X)\right)={\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1},\tau}\left(\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(X)\right)=\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(X)\ .

The same sort of reasoning yields

{\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp},\tau}\left(\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(Y)\right)={\mathbb{E}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1},\tau}\left(\rho_{T_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}\alpha_{U_{S}}(Y)\right)=\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(Y)\ .

∎

Theorem
3.7
allows us to easily construct an orthogonal basis of eigenvectors of
\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}
by taking products of eigenfunctions involving a single Fermionic degree of freedom.

3.8 DEFINITION
.

Let
H
be positive semidefinite on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and let
T\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
be such that
{\rm\ker}(H)\subseteq{\rm ker}(T)
.
Let
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
consisting of simultaneous eigenvectors of
H
and
T
;
For all
j
,
H\psi_{j}=\lambda_{j}\psi_{j}
and
T\psi_{j}=\mu_{j}\psi_{j}
. For each
j=1,\dots,N
, define
Z_{j}:=Z(\psi_{j})
and

(3.24)

H_{j,(0,0)}:={\mathds{1}}\ ,\ H_{j,(1,0)}:=Z^{*}_{j}\ ,\ H_{j,(0,1)}:=Z_{j}\quad{\rm and}\quad H_{j,(1,1)}:=Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}-\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}}\ .

For
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\})^{N}
, define

(3.25)

H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:=\prod_{j=1}^{N}H_{j,\alpha_{j}}

where the terms are arranged in the product so that the indices increase form left to right. Note that the
H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
depend on
T
, though we have suppressed this in our notation.

The operators
H_{j,(a,b)}
differ from the operators
K_{j,(a,b)}
in Lemma
A.15
only in that they are not normalized. The advantage of this is that we do not need to require
0<\mu_{j}<1
;
\{H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ {\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\})^{N}\}
is a basis of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
for all
T
. However,
T
is such that
\mu_{j}=0
for some
j
, then the
j^{{\rm th}}
factor of
\rho_{T}
is
2Z_{j}Z_{j}^{*}
, and hence
Z_{j}\rho_{T}=0
, and hence
\langle Z_{j},Z_{j}\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{T}}=0
. In fact, for this reason

\langle H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{T}}=0
if the second component of
\alpha_{j}
is not zero. Likewise, if
\mu_{j}=1
for some
j
,

\langle H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{T}}=0
if the first component of
\alpha_{j}
is not zero.

In any case, because the normalization does not affect orthogonality, it follows from
Lemma
A.15
that
\{H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ {\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\})^{N}\}
is a
mutually orthogonal set with respect to the GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{T}
, whether this inner product is degenerate or not.

These operators may be regarded as
un-normalized Hermite polynomials for the GIG state
\rho_{T}
.
Indeed, for
{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
define

(3.26)

Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}(Z^{*}(\psi_{j}))^{\beta_{j}}(Z(\psi_{j}))^{\gamma_{j}}\ ,

where the terms in the product are arranged so that the indices increase from left to right. Define
|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\beta_{j}
and likewise for
|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|
. It is evident that
\{Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\ :\ {\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}\}
is a basis, but not an orthonormal basis, for
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. It is natural to refer to the span of

\{Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\ :\ {\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}\ {\rm and}\ |{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|\leq n\}

the space of
polynomials of degree
n
or less
. It is easy to see that this space does not depends on the choice of the orthonormal basis
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{N}\}
. Now fix a GIG state
\rho_{T}
where
0<T<{\mathds{1}}
.
If one then applies the Gram-Schmidt procedure to the polynomial basis (
3.26
), one arrives at the orthonormal basis

\{K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ {\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\})^{N}\}
of Lemma
A.15
. This is exactly how the classical Hermite polynomials arise. The connection between Hermite polynomials and so-called
Wick ordering
is well developed in the case of the CCR for Boson fields where the corresponding procedure leads to the classical Hermite polynomials applied to Boson fields
[
63
,
33
]
.

3.9 LEMMA
.

Let
H
be positive semidefinite on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and let
T\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
be such that
{\rm\ker}(H)\subseteq{\rm ker}(T)
, and for
j\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and
(a,b)\in\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}
, let
H_{j,(a,b)}
be defined as in Definition
3.8
. Then

(3.27)

\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(H_{j,(a,b)})=e^{(a+b)\lambda_{j}}H_{j,(a,b)}\ .

Proof.

Because
\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}
is unital,
\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(H_{j,(0,0)})=e^{-\lambda_{j}}H_{j,(0,0)}
.
Under the hypotheses on
S=e^{-H}
and
R=({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T
,
(
3.11
) becomes

(3.28)

\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(Z_{j})=e^{-\lambda_{j}}Z_{j}\ .

This is the same as
\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(H_{j,(0,1)})=e^{-\lambda_{j}}H_{j,(0,1)}
. Taking adjoints, one has

\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(H_{j,(1,0)})=e^{-\lambda_{j}}H_{j,(1,0)}
.

Likewise, (
3.12
) becomes

(3.29)

\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(Z^{*}_{j}Z_{k})=e^{-\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}Z^{*}_{j}Z_{k}+\delta_{j,k}(1-e^{-2\lambda_{j}})\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}}\ .

Incidentally, this shows that for
j\neq k
,
Z^{*}_{j}Z_{k}
is an eigenvector of
\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}
with eigenvalue
e^{-\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}
.
We are interested in the case
j=k
. Since
\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}
is unital,
{\mathds{1}}
is an eigenvector of
\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}
with eigenvalue
1
. For
k=j
,
(
3.29
) becomes

(3.30)

\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(Z^{*}_{j}Z_{j})=e^{-2\lambda_{j}}Z^{*}_{j}Z_{j}+(1-e^{-2\lambda_{j}})\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}}\ .

Therefore,

(3.31)

\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(Z^{*}_{j}Z_{j})=e^{-2\lambda_{j}}Z^{*}_{j}Z_{j}+(1-e^{-2\lambda_{j}})\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}}\ ,

and hence

(3.32)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(Z^{*}_{j}Z_{j}-\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-2\lambda_{j}}Z^{*}_{j}Z_{j}+(1-e^{-2\lambda_{j}})\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}}-\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-2\lambda_{j}}(Z^{*}_{j}Z_{j}-\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}})\ .

Therefore,
H_{j,(1,1)}=Z^{*}_{j}Z_{j}-\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}}
is an eigenvector of
\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}
with eigenvalue
e^{-2\lambda_{j}}
.
∎

3.10 THEOREM
.

Let
T\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
, and let
H\geq 0
be an operator on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that
[H,T]=0
and
{\rm ker}(H)\subseteq{\rm ker}(T)
.
Then
(S,R):=(e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T)
is a compatible pair, and
T
is the element of
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
associated to
S
and
R
by Lemma
3.1
.
Let
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that for each
j
,
H\psi_{j}=\lambda_{j}\psi_{j}
and
T\psi_{j}=\mu_{j}\psi_{j}
.
Let
Z_{j}=Z(\psi_{j})
for each
j
, and let
H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
be given by (
3.24
) and (
3.25
).
For
\alpha=(a,b)\in\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}
, define
|\alpha|=a+b
. For each
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\})^{N}
,

(3.33)

\Phi_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}^{\dagger}(H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=e^{-\sum_{j=1}^{N}|\alpha_{j}|\lambda_{j}}H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ .

Consequently,
\Phi_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}^{\dagger}
is with respect to the GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{T}
.

Likewise, for
each
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\})^{N}
,

(3.34)

\Phi_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}(H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rho_{T})=e^{-\sum_{j=1}^{N}|\alpha_{j}|\lambda_{j}}H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rho_{T}\ .

Proof.

Everything except the final statement is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.5
, Theorem
3.7
, Definition
3.8
and Lemma
3.9
.
To prove the final statement, let
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}
be any operator on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that is self-adjoint with respect to the GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{T}
.
Then for all
Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}

(3.35)

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}(Y\rho_{T})={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}(Y)\rho_{T}\ .

To see this, suppose for the moment that
0<T<{\mathds{1}}
so that
\rho_{T}
is a faithful state. Let
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and then

\displaystyle\tau(X^{*}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}(Y)\rho_{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle X,{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}(Y)\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}(X),Y\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{T}}=\tau(({\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}(X)^{\dagger})^{*}Y\rho_{T})=\tau(X^{*}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}(Y\rho_{T}))\ .

Since
X
is arbitrary and
\rho_{T}
is faithful, this proves (
3.35
) for
0<T<{\mathds{1}}
. Now by a limiting argument on obtains
(
3.35
) in general.
Now apply (
3.35
) with
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}=\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2H})T}
and
Y=H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
to conclude (
3.34
) from (
3.33
).
∎

3.11 Remark
.

When
0<T<{\mathds{1}}
,
\{H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rho_{T}\ :\ {\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\})^{N}\}
is a basis of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
because
the density matrix
\rho_{T}
is invertible. Moreover, it is a
mutually orthogonal set with respect to the
dual GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{T}
, namely
\tau(X^{*}Y\rho_{T}^{-1})
, as a direct consequence of the orthogonality
of
\{H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ {\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\})^{N}\}
with respect to the GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{T}
.
However, as explained below Definition
3.8
, when
0
or
1
is an eigenvalues of
T
, there will be

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
such that
H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rho_{T}=0
.

4.
Semigroups of quantum operations on the CAR algebra

In this section we are concerned with semigroups of quantum operations, both on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. By a semigroup of quantum operations,
we always mean a continuous semigroup. That is, a set
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
such that
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{0}={\mathds{1}}
, and
\lim_{s\to t}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{s}={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
for all
t
(the continuity property), and
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{s}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{s+t}
for all
s,t\geq 0
(the semigroup property) . Note in particular that we require
\lim_{t\to 0}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}={\mathds{1}}
.
This implies the
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
is one-to-one for all sufficiently small
t
, and then by the semigroup property
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
is one-to-one for all
t
. This is essential for applying Theorem
1.19
.

4.1.
Semigroups on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that map
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself.

4.1 LEMMA
.

Let
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be a semigroup of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, each of which maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself.
Let
\gamma_{t}
denote the symbol map of
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
. Then for all
t>0
,

(4.1)

\gamma_{t}(Q)=R_{t}+e^{tG}Qe^{tG^{*}}\ .

where
\{e^{tG}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a contraction semigroup on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, uniquely determined up to a global phase, and where
t\mapsto R_{t}
is a differentiable function with
values in the the space of operators on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that

(4.2)

0\leq R_{t}\leq{\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}}

and

(4.3)

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}R_{t}=:A\geq 0\ .

Finally, for all
s,t\geq 0

(4.4)

R_{s+t}=R_{s}+e^{sG}R_{t}e^{sG^{*}}\ .

Proof.

Since
\lim_{t\to 0}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}={\mathds{1}}
,
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
is invertible for all
t\geq 0
, and hence Theorem
1.19
applies. Also because

\lim_{t\to 0}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}={\mathds{1}}
,
\lim_{t\to 0}\gamma_{t}
is the identity on
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
, Therefore,

\gamma_{t}({\mathds{1}})\geq\gamma_{t}(0)
for all sufficiently small
t
, say, all
t\leq t_{0}
,
t_{0}>0
. Then because each
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
is completely positive, it follows from Theorem
1.19
that for each
t\leq t_{0}
,

\gamma_{t}
has the form

Q\mapsto R_{t}+S_{t}QS_{t}^{*}
for some operator
S_{t}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
that is determined up to a global phase. Then because
\gamma_{t}(Q)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
for all
Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,

0\leq R_{t}\leq{\mathds{1}}-S_{t}S_{t}^{*}
, and hence
S_{t}
is a contraction. This shows that

(4.5)

\gamma_{t}(Q)=R_{t}+S_{t}QS_{t}^{*}

for ll
t\leq t_{0}
, and then
since
\gamma_{t}(Q)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
for all
Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,

(4.6)

0\leq R_{t}\leq{\mathds{1}}-S_{t}S_{t}^{*}

for all such
t
.

By the semigroup property,
for all such
s,t\leq t_{0}

(4.7)

\gamma_{s+t}(Q)=R_{s}+S_{s}R_{t}S_{s}^{*}+S_{s}S_{t}QS_{t}^{*}S_{s}^{*}\ .

Therefore,
\gamma_{t}
has the form
Q\mapsto R_{t}+S_{t}QS_{t}^{*}
and (
4.6
) is satisfied for all
t\leq 2t_{0}
. By simple induction, this is true for all
t\geq 0
.

Because
t\mapsto{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
is differentiable and right differentiable at
t=0
,
t\mapsto\gamma_{t}
is differentiable and right differentiable at
t=0
. Since
R_{t}=\gamma_{t}(0)
,
t\mapsto R_{t}
also has these properties. Since
\lim_{t\to 0}\gamma_{t}(Q)=Q
for all
Q
, it must be that
\lim_{t\to 0}R_{t}=0
. Then by the right differentiability of
R_{t}
at
t=
,
A:=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}t^{-1}R_{t}
exists, and is positive semi-defnite. This proves (
4.3
).

By (
4.7
), for all
Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
, and all
s,t\geq 0
.

(4.8)

S_{s+t}QS^{*}_{s+t}=S_{s}S_{t}QS_{t}^{*}S_{s}^{*}\ .

Let
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
be the Hilbert space consisting of operators on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
equipped with the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product.
Define the operator
\Gamma_{t}
acting on
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
by

(4.9)

\Gamma_{t}(X)=S_{t}XS_{t}^{*}\ .

Then
\|\Gamma_{t}\|=\|S_{t}\|^{2}
, so that each
\Gamma_{t}
is a contraction on
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
. By (
4.8
) and the differentiability of
t\mapsto\gamma_{t}
,
\{\Gamma_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

is a continuous contraction semigroup on
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
. The generator of
\{\Gamma_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
has the form

(4.10)

\Lambda_{G}(X)=GX+XG^{*}

for some operator
G
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
uniquely determined up to an additive term
ic{\mathds{1}}
for some
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We may fix
G
by requiring
\Im({\rm Tr}[G])=0
, but in any case
G
is the generator of a contraction semigroup on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. We fix the phase on
S_{t}
by requiring
S_{t}=e^{tG}
for all
t
. Then (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) become
(
4.1
) and (
4.2
)
∎

.

The equation (
4.4
) was studied in
[
31
]
, but not the condition (
4.3
). Perhaps for this reason, the authors were not able to slove it in any generality.
(See the remark just above Lemma III.1 in
[
31
]
).
The next theorem provides the general solution of (
4.4
) subject to (
4.3
). Before stating the theorem, recall that
G
is the generator of a contraction semigroup on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
if and only if for all
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,
\Re\langle\psi,G\psi\rangle\leq 0
, and this is the same as
-G-G^{*}\geq 0
.

4.2 THEOREM
.

Let
\{e^{tG}\}_{t\geq 0}
be a contraction semigroup on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Then a differentiable function
R_{t}
on
[0,\infty)
with values in
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
satisfies

(4.11)

R_{t+h}=R_{h}+e^{hG}R_{t}e^{hG^{*}}

for all
t,h\geq 0

together with

(4.12)

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{t}R_{t}=A\geq 0\quad{\rm and}\quad 0\leq R_{t}\leq{\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}}

if and only if

(4.13)

R_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s

and

(4.14)

0\leq A\leq-(G+G^{*})\ .

Proof.

Fix any
A\geq 0
, and define
R_{t}
by (
4.13
). Then for all
t,h>0

e^{hG}R_{t}e^{hG^{*}}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{(h+s)G}Ae^{(h+s)G^{*}}{\rm d}s=\int_{h}^{h+t}e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s=R_{t+h}-R_{h}\ ,

so that (
4.11
) is satisfied. Evidently,
\lim_{t\to 0}R_{t}=0
, and
0\leq R_{t}
for all
t
. If furthermore (
4.14
) is satisfied,

R_{t}\leq\int_{0}^{t}e^{sG}(-G-G^{*})e^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s=\int_{0}^{t}-\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}e^{sG}e^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s={\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}}

so that all requirements in (
4.12
) are satisfied.

Conversely, suppose that (
4.11
) and (
4.12
) are satisfied. Subtracing
R_{t}
from both sides in (
4.11
), and dividing bh
h>0
yields

(4.15)

\frac{1}{h}(R_{t+h}-R_{t})=\frac{1}{h}R_{h}+\frac{1}{h}(e^{hG}R_{t}e^{hG^{*}}-R_{t})\ .

Define

A:=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{h}R_{h}

and define
\Lambda_{G}
to be the operator on
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})
given by

\Lambda_{G}(X)=GX+XG^{*}\ ,

noting that
\Lambda_{G}
is the generator of the semigroup
X\mapsto e^{tG}Xe^{tG^{*}}
. Then taking the limit
h\downarrow 0
in (
4.15
),

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}R_{t}=A+\Lambda_{G}(R_{t})\ .

The unique solution of this differential equation is

R_{t}=e^{t\Lambda}R_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\Lambda}A{\rm d}s=e^{t\Lambda}R_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{s\Lambda}A{\rm d}s\ .

For
R_{0}=0
, this reduces to (
4.13
). We have already seen that when
A\leq-(G+G^{*})
, then
R_{t}\leq{\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}}
.
Now suppose that
R_{t}
is given by (
4.13
), and that
R_{t}\leq{\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}}
. Then for
t
near
0
,

R_{t}=tA+o(t)
and
e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}}={\mathds{1}}+tG+tG^{*}+o(t)
. Therefore,
A\leq-(G+G^{*})
is necessary for
R_{t}\leq{\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}}
to be true for all
t>0
.
∎

Lemma
4.1
and Theorem
4.2
lead to a complete description of semigroups of quantum operations on the Araki-Wyss algebra
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that map
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself.

4.3 THEOREM
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between:

(1)
Semigroups
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
such that for each
t\geq 0

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself

and

(2)
Pairs
\left(G,A\right)
consisting of
a contraction semigroup generator
G
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
together with an operator
A
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}

satisfying
0\leq A\leq-G-G^{*}
.

More specifically, let
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be as in (1).
Then there is a pair
\left(G,A\right)
as in (2) such that with
R_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s
,

(4.16)

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}=\Phi_{e^{tG},R_{t}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}\

for all
t\geq 0
, where
\Phi_{S,R}
is the GIG quantum operation produced by the EHK construction.

Conversely, let
\left(G,A\right)
be as in (2). Define
R_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s
.
Then for each
t
,
(e^{tG},R_{t})
is a compatible pair so that the EHK construction yields a GIG quantum operation
\Phi_{e^{tG},R_{t}}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, and with
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
defined by
(
4.16
),
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
such that each
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself.

Proof.

Let
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be as in
(1)
. Let
\gamma_{t}
denote the symbol map of

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
. By Lemma
4.1
there is a contraction semigroup
\{e^{tG}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and a differentiable function
t\mapsto R_{t}
from
[0,\infty)
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
such
that for all
Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,
\gamma_{t}(Q)=R_{t}+e^{tG}Qe^{tG^{*}}
and such that
\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}R_{t}=A\geq 0
and such that
0\leq R_{t}\leq{\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}}
so that for each
t
,
(e^{tG},R_{t})
is a compatible pair. By
Theorem
4.2
,
0\leq A\leq-G-G^{*}
, so that
\left(G,A\right)
is a pair as in
(2)
.

The EHK construction gives us a GIG quantum operation
\Phi_{e^{tG},R_{t}}
whose symbol map is
Q\mapsto R_{t}+e^{tG}Qe^{tG^{*}}=\gamma_{t}(Q)
. By the Araki-Wyss Theorem, Theorem
1.8
,
every quantum operation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself is completely determined by its symbol map. Therefore,
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
satisfies (
4.16
).

Conversely, given a pair
\left(G,A\right)
as in
(2)
, define
R_{t}
in terms of

G
and
A
by (
4.13
). Then by Theorem
4.2
, for all
s,t\geq 0
,
R_{s+t}=R_{s}+e^{sG}R_{t}e^{sG^{*}}
and
0\leq R_{t}\leq{\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}}
.
In particular, for all
t\geq 0
,
(e^{tG},R_{t})
is a compatible pair, and the EHK construction gives us a GIG quantum operation
\Phi_{e^{tG},R_{t}}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
with symbol map
\gamma_{t}(Q)=R_{t}+e^{tG}Qe^{tG^{*}}
.
Then for all
Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,

\gamma_{s+t}(Q)=R_{s+t}+e^{(s+t)G}Qe^{(s+t)G^{*}}=R_{s}+e^{sG}\gamma_{t}(Q)e^{sG^{*}}=\gamma_{s}(\gamma_{t}(Q))\ .

Because a linear map on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is completely determined by its action on
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
, if we define
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
by (
4.16
), then

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{s+t}={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{s}\circ{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\ .

Also, since
\lim_{t\to 0}\gamma_{t}
is the identity on
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,
\lim_{t\downarrow 0}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}={\mathds{1}}
. Therefore,
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps

{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, as in
(1)
.
∎

5.
GIG semigroups on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}

Every GIG semigroup on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
leaves
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
invariant since the span on
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
is
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. Therefore, the restriction of any such semigroup to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is (the dual of) one of the semigroups described in Theorem
4.3
, and hence of the form
\left\{\Phi_{e^{tG},R_{t}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
where
G
is a contraction semigroup generator
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,
R_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s
and
0\leq A\leq-G-G^{*}
.
In this section we study the problem of extending such semigroups on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
to all of

{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.

Given a pair
(G,A)
as in Theorem
4.3
, let
R_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s
and define an operator
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
by

(5.1)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}(X)=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}\left(\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{tG},R_{t}}X-X\right)\ .

(In this finite dimensional setting, the limit clearly exists for all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, but in fact we will calculate it below).
By Theorem
4.3
, with
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}^{\dagger}:=\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{tG},R_{t}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
,
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}^{\dagger}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, and hence

{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is invariant under
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}
, and
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
is the generator of this semigroup.

Theorem
5.1
below shows that
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}
can be written in Lindblad form as an operator on all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, and even shows how to do this
explicitly in terms of the data
(G,A)
.
Since
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is not a factor, it does not follow from
Lindblad’s Theorem
[
44
]
that

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
can be written in Lindblad form. While
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is a factor, so that by Lindblad’s Theorem
the generator of any semigroup of unital CP operators on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
can be written in
Lindblad form, the results at our disposal so far do not show that
\{\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{tG},R_{t}}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup on all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.
However, we shall now prove that, in fact,
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}
can be written in Lindblad form.

5.1 THEOREM
.

Let
(G,A)
be a pair consisting of a contraction semigroup generator
G
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and an operator on
A
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
satisfying
0\leq A\leq-G-G^{*}
, as in Theorem
4.3
. Define

(5.2)

H:=-\frac{1}{2}(G+G^{*})\quad{\rm and}\quad K:=\frac{1}{2i}(G-G^{*})

so that
G^{*}=-H-iK
. Define

(5.3)

T:=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}(2H+\epsilon{\mathds{1}})^{-1/2}A(2H+\epsilon{\mathds{1}})^{-1/2}

and let
\{\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
consisting of eigenvectors of
T
, and for
j=1,\dots,N
, define
\lambda_{j}
by
T\phi_{j}=\lambda_{j}\phi_{j}
.
Then define
\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{N}\}
by

(5.4)

\phi_{j}:=(2H)^{1/2}\eta_{j}\ ,\quad j=1,\dots,N\ .

Finally, define the operator
\widehat{K}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
by “second quantizing” the operator
K
defined in (
5.2
).
Then the action of
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}
as defined in (
5.1
) on

{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is given by

(5.5)

\displaystyle{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-\lambda_{j})\bigl(Z^{*}(\phi_{j})WXWZ(\phi_{j})-\tfrac{1}{2}Z^{*}(\phi_{j})Z(\phi_{j})X-\tfrac{1}{2}XZ^{*}(\phi_{j})Z(\phi_{j})\bigr)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}\bigl(Z(\phi_{j})WXWZ^{*}(\phi_{j})-\tfrac{1}{2}Z(\phi_{j})Z^{*}(\phi_{j})X-\tfrac{1}{2}XZ(\phi_{j})Z^{*}(\phi_{j})\bigr)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle i\tfrac{1}{2}[\widehat{K},X]\ .

where
W:=e^{i\frac{pi}{2}{\mathcal{N}}}
is the unitary figuring in the principle automorphism.

While the proof of Theorem
5.1
is essentially a computation, it is somewhat involved and is distributed over several lemmas.

Before turning to these lemmas, we formulate a simple corollary that addresses some questions raised in the introduction.

5.2 COROLLARY
.

Let
(G,A)
be a pair consisting of a contraction semigroup generator
G
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and an operator on
A
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
satisfying
0\leq A\leq-G-G^{*}
, as in Theorem
4.3
. Let
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{G,A}
be the Lindblad generator on the right side of (
5.5
). Then the dual semigroup
\left\{e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
is a GIG quantum semigroup extending the semigroup
\left\{\Phi_{e^{tG},R_{t}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
associated to
(G,A)
by Theorem
4.3
, so that the symbol map of
e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}}
is
Q\mapsto R_{t}+e^{tG}Qe^{tG^{*}}
. Moreover, for all
t\geq 0
,

(5.6)

e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\Phi_{e^{(t/n)G},R_{(t/n)}}\right)^{n}\ .

In this sense, every GIG quantum semigroup arises from the EHK construction.

Proof.

Since
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
is the generator of the semigroup
\left\{\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{tG},R_{t}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
associated to

(G,A)
by Theorem
4.3
,
\left\{e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
is an extension of this semigroup to all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. Since all GIG density matrices belong to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,
for any
\rho_{Q}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
,

e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}}\rho_{Q}=\Phi_{e^{tG},R_{t}}\rho_{Q}

and hence the symbol map of
e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}}
is
Q\mapsto R_{t}+e^{tG}Qe^{tG^{*}}
. It is also clear from the explicit form of
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}
that for all
t
,

[{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger},\alpha_{{\rm G},t}]=0
. Consequently
[{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A},\alpha_{{\rm G},t}]=0
for all
t
, so that
\{e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a GIG semigroup of quantum operations.
Finally, (
5.6
) follows from a theorem of Chernoff
[
19
]
.
∎

For a discussion of Chernoff’s Theorem with a simple proof, see
[
49
, p. 108]
. While Corollary
5.2
shows that every semigroup of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself has an extension to a
GIG semigroup on all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that is “natural” from the point of view of the EHK construction, it does not
does not say that
\{e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}}\}_{t\geq 0}
is the only such extension, nor does it say that for all
t
,

e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}^{\dagger}}=\Phi_{e^{tG},R_{t}}
although this identity is true restricted to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. By a ,
what is a direct consequence of Theorem
5.1
is that for all
t>0
,

The computation justifying (
5.5
) will be carried out using a basis for
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that is now introduced.
Let
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
consisting of eigenvectors of
A
; that is
A\psi_{j}=\nu_{j}
for each
j
. Define
Z_{j}=Z(\psi_{j})
,
j=1,\dots,N
. For
{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
, define
Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}
as in (
3.26
). Then

\{Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\ :\ {\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}\}
is a basis of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.

5.3 LEMMA
.

Let
(G,A)
be a pair as in part (2) of Theorem
4.3
, and for
t\geq 0
let
R_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s
. Then for all
{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
,

(5.7)

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}\left(\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{tG^{*}},R_{t}}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}-Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right)=\\
\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\prod_{1\leq j<k}(Z_{j}^{*})^{\beta_{j}}Z_{j}^{\gamma_{j}}\right)F_{k}\left(\prod_{k<\ell\leq N}(Z_{j}^{*})^{\beta_{j}}Z_{j}^{\gamma_{j}}\right)

where

(5.8)

F_{k}:=\delta_{\beta_{k},1}Z^{*}(G^{*}\psi_{k})^{\beta_{k}}Z_{k}^{\gamma_{k}}+\delta_{\gamma_{k},1}(Z^{*}_{k})^{\beta_{k}}Z(G^{*}\psi_{k})+\delta_{(\beta_{k},\gamma_{k}),(1,1)}\langle\psi_{k},A\psi_{k}\rangle\ .

In particular, with
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{G,A}
given by (
5.1
),

(5.9)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{G,A}(Z_{j}^{*})=Z^{*}(G^{*}\psi_{j})

and

(5.10)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{G,A}(Z_{j}^{*}Z_{k})=Z^{*}(G^{*}\psi_{j})Z_{k}+Z_{j}^{*}Z(G^{*}\psi_{k})+\langle\psi_{j},A\psi_{k}\rangle{\mathds{1}}\ .

Proof.

We use formula (
3.10
) with
S=e^{tG}
, and with
V
in place of
A
(which has another meaning at present) and
Z
in place of
B

(5.11)

\alpha_{U_{e^{tG}}}(Z(\psi_{j}))=-Z(({\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}})^{1/2}\psi_{j})+V(e^{tG^{*}}\psi_{j})\ .

Therefore,
\alpha_{U_{e^{tG}}}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})
is a sum over all the products obtained by selecting either
V^{*}(e^{tG^{*}}\psi_{j})
or
-Z^{*}(({\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}})^{1/2}\psi_{j})

to replace
Z^{*}_{j}
and selecting either
V(e^{tG^{*}}\psi_{j})
or
-Z(({\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}})^{1/2}\psi_{j})

to replace
Z_{j}
. The simplest of these terms is the one in which there are no
Z
terms. This yields the product

\prod_{j=1}^{N}(V^{*}(e^{tG^{*}}\psi_{j})^{\beta_{j}}(V(e^{tG^{*}}\psi_{j}))^{\gamma_{j}}\ ,

which already belongs to
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
so there is no conditional expectation to take in formula (
3.10
).
Identifying
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{0\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
with
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
(that is, replacing
V
by
Z
), the contribution of this term to the generator is simply

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\prod_{j=1}^{N}(V^{*}(e^{tG^{*}}\psi_{j})^{\beta_{j}}(V(e^{tG^{*}}\psi_{j}))^{\gamma_{j}}\bigg|_{t=0}\ .

This produces the first two of the three terms in each
F_{k}
in (
5.8
). Note that have already made full use of the
-Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}
on the left in (
5.7
).

To compute the contribution of the remaining terms, which do involve some
Z
factors,
we must
bring all of the
Z
terms together and take their expectation in the state
\rho_{T_{e^{tG},R_{t}}}
.
Since
\rho_{T_{e^{tG},R_{t}}}
is a GIG state, the expectations are all determined by the expectations of the form

\rho_{T_{e^{tG},R_{t}}}(Z^{*}(({\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}})^{1/2}\psi_{j})Z(({\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}})^{1/2}\psi_{k}))=\langle\psi_{j},R_{t}\psi_{k}\rangle.

Then since
R_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s
,

\frac{1}{t}\langle\psi_{j},R_{t}\psi_{k}\rangle=\langle\psi_{j},A\psi_{k}\rangle+{\mathcal{O}}(t)=\delta_{j,k}\langle\psi_{k},A\psi_{k}\rangle+{\mathcal{O}}(t)\ .

Therefore, the only pairings that contribute to the generator are those coming from pairs in which
\beta_{j}=\gamma_{j}=1
. This gives us the remaining contribution to
F_{k}
.
Finally, (
5.10
) is a consequence of (
5.7
) and (
5.8
)
∎

Our goal now is to write (
5.10
) in Lindblad form. The next lemmas provide the means to do this.

5.4 LEMMA
.

Let
Z
be an CAR field over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
acting irreducibly on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
. Let
W=e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}{\mathcal{N}}}
, and for any non-zero vector
\phi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, define the Lindblad generators

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{Z^{*}(\phi)}
and
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{Z(\phi)}

by

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{Z^{*}(\phi)}(X)=2Z^{*}(\phi)WXWZ(\phi)-Z^{*}(\phi)Z(\phi)X-XZ^{*}(\phi)Z(\phi)

and

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{Z(\phi)}(X)=2Z(\phi)WXWZ^{*}(\phi)-Z(\phi)Z^{*}(\phi)X-XZ(\phi)Z^{*}(\phi)\ .

Then for any
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, the following are valid

(5.12)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{Z^{*}(\phi)}(Z(\psi))={\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{Z(\phi)}(Z(\psi))=Z(|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|\psi)\ ,

(5.13)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{Z^{*}(\phi)}(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\psi))=-\langle\phi,\psi\rangle Z^{*}(\phi)Z(\psi)-\langle\psi,\phi\rangle Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\phi)

and

(5.14)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{Z(\phi)}(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\psi))=2|\langle\phi,\psi\rangle|^{2}{\mathds{1}}-\langle\phi,\psi\rangle Z^{*}(\phi)Z(\psi)-\langle\psi,\phi\rangle Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\phi)\ .

Proof.

Since
W
is a self-adjoint unitary commuting with
Z(\psi)
(and hence
Z^{*}(\psi)
) for all
\psi
,

2Z^{*}(\phi)WZ(\psi)WZ(\phi)=-2Z^{*}(\phi)Z(\psi)Z(\phi)=\\
(\langle\psi,\phi\rangle{\mathds{1}}-Z(\psi)Z^{*}(\phi))Z(\phi)+Z^{*}(\phi)Z(\phi)Z(\psi)\ .

Therefore,
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{Z^{*}(\phi)}(Z(\psi))=-\langle\psi,\phi\rangle Z(\phi)=Z(\langle\phi,\psi\rangle\phi)=Z(|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|\psi)
.
This proves the first formula in (
5.12
). All of the other formulas are proved
making similar computations using the CAR.
∎

Now let
\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{N}\}
be a set of
N
vectors in
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, not necessarily a basis.
Let
\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}\}
be a set of
N
numbers in
[0,1]
. Define positive semidefinite operators
H_{0}
,
H_{1}
and
K
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
by

H_{0}:=\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-\lambda_{j})|\phi_{j}\rangle\langle\phi_{j}|\ ,\quad H_{1}:=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}|\phi_{j}\rangle\langle\phi_{j}|\quad{\rm and}\quad 2H=H_{0}+H_{1}

and define two Lindblad generators
{\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{1}
by

(5.15)

{\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{0}:=\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-\lambda_{j}){\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{Z^{*}(\phi_{j})}\quad{\rm and}\quad{\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{1}:=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{Z(\phi_{j})}

Then because

\langle\phi,\psi\rangle Z^{*}(\phi)Z(\psi)=Z^{*}(|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|\psi)Z(\psi)\quad{\rm and}\quad\langle\psi,\phi\rangle Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\phi)=Z^{*}(\psi)Z(|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|\psi)\ ,

{\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{0}(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\psi))=-Z^{*}(H_{0}\psi)Z(\psi)-Z^{*}(\psi)Z(H_{0}\psi)

and

{\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{1}(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\psi))=2\langle\psi,H_{1}\psi\rangle{\mathds{1}}-Z^{*}(H_{1}\psi)Z(\psi)-Z^{*}(\psi)Z(H_{1}\psi)

Therefore,

(5.16)

({\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{0}+{\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{1})(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\psi))=2\langle\psi,H_{1}\psi\rangle{\mathds{1}}-Z^{*}(2H\psi)Z(\psi)-Z^{*}(\psi)Z(2H\psi)

We now show that we may choose
\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{N}\}
and
\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}\}
so that
2H=-G-G^{*}
and
A=H_{1}
. This is a consequence of the following lemma.

5.5 LEMMA
.

Let
0\leq H_{1}\leq 2H
be positive semidefinite operators on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Then there is a set of vectors
\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{N}\}
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and a set of numbers

\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}\}
in
[0,1]
such that

\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}|\phi_{j}\rangle\langle\phi_{j}|=H_{1}\quad{\rm and}\quad\sum_{j=1}^{N}|\phi_{j}\rangle\langle\phi_{j}|=2H\ .

Proof.

Let
T:=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}(2H+\epsilon{\mathds{1}})^{-1/2}H_{1}(2H+\epsilon{\mathds{1}})^{-1/2}
. Then
0\leq T\leq{\mathds{1}}
. Let
\{\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
consisting of eigenvectors of
T
, and for each
j
, define
\lambda_{j}
by
T\eta_{j}=\lambda_{j}\eta_{j}
. Then
T=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}|\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|
, and

{\mathds{1}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\ |\eta_{j}\rangle\langle\eta_{j}|

Then from the definition of
T
, it follows that
H_{1}=(2H)^{1/2}T(2H)^{1/2}
and of course
2H=(2H)^{1/2}{\mathds{1}}(2H)^{1/2}
. Therefore,

H_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}|(2H)^{1/2}\eta_{j}\rangle\langle(2H)^{1/2}\eta_{j}|\quad{\rm and}\quad H=\sum_{j=1}^{N}|(2H)^{1/2}\eta_{j}\rangle\langle(2H)^{1/2}\eta_{j}|\ .

Therefore we take
\phi_{j}=(2H)^{1/2}\eta_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,N
.
∎

We now apply Lemma
5.5
with

(5.17)

H_{1}=A\quad{\rm and}\quad 2H=-G-G^{*}\ ,

which we may do since
0\leq A\leq-G-G^{*}
. Using the choice of
\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{N}\}
and
\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}\}
provided by the Lemma for this choice if
H_{1}
and
H
,
(
5.16
) becomes

(5.18)

({\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{0}+{\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{1})(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\psi))=2\langle\psi,A\psi\rangle{\mathds{1}}-Z^{*}(2H\psi)Z(\psi)-Z^{*}(\psi)Z(2H\psi)

Now define the Lindblad generator
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}
by

(5.19)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(X)=\frac{1}{2}({\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{0}(X)+{\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{1}(X)+i[\widehat{K},X])\

where
K
is specified in (
5.2
) so that
G^{*}=-H-iK
.
Note that
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(X)
is exactly what appears on the right side of (
5.5
). Therefore, to prove Theorem
5.5
we must show that

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(X)
as specified in (
5.19
) agrees with the right side of (
5.7
) in Lemma
5.3
.

Recalling that
[\widehat{K},Z(\psi)]=Z(K\psi)
,

(5.20)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Z^{*}(\psi)Z(\psi))=\langle\psi,A\psi\rangle{\mathds{1}}-Z^{*}(G^{*}\psi)Z(\psi)-Z^{*}(\psi)Z(G^{*}\psi)\ .

Even more simply, using (
5.12
), one finds.

(5.21)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Z(\psi))=Z(G^{*}\psi)\ .

Since
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}
is Hermitian, and evidently satisfied
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}{\mathds{1}}=0
, comparison with Lemma
5.3
shows that
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}
is given by the right side of (
5.7
) whenever
|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|\leq 2
.

5.6 LEMMA
.

Let
L\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, and let
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{L}^{\dagger}
denote the Lindblad generator acting on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
according to

(5.22)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{L}^{\dagger}(X):=2LXL^{*}-LL^{*}X-XLL^{*}=[L,X]L^{*}-L[L^{*},X]\ .

Then for
X,Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(5.23)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{L}^{\dagger}(XY)-{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{L}(X)Y-X{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{L}(Y)=-2[L,X][L^{*},Y]\ .

5.7 Remark
.

Probabilists will recognize the left side of (
5.23
) as the
carré du champ
bilinear form as defined by P. -A. Meyer
[
48
]
which is fundamental to the study of classical Markovian semigroups, especially in the diffusive case
[
8
]
.

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

For any operator
X
,

(5.24)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{L}^{\dagger}(X):=2LXL^{*}-LL^{*}X-XLL^{*}=[L,X]L^{*}-L[L^{*},X]\ .

Next,

\displaystyle LXYL^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle LXL^{*}Y+LX[Y,L^{*}]=LL^{*}XY+L[X,L^{*}]Y+LX[Y,L^{*}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle LL^{*}XY+L[X,L^{*}]Y+XL[Y,L^{*}]+[L,X][Y,L^{*}]\ .

Likewise,

\displaystyle LXYL^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle XLYL^{*}+[L,X]YL^{*}=XYLL^{*}+[L,X]YL^{*}+X[L,Y]L^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle XYLL^{*}+[L,X]L^{*}Y+X[L,Y]L^{*}+[L,X][Y,L^{*}]\ .

Combining the last two calculations with (
5.24
) yields (
5.23
).
∎

We apply this to the Lindblad generators
{\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}_{1}
defined in
(
5.15
) to obtain

(5.25)

{\mathcal{K}}_{0}^{\dagger}(XY)-{\mathcal{K}}_{0}^{\dagger}(X)Y=X{\mathcal{K}}_{0}^{\dagger}(Y)=-2\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-\lambda_{j})[Z^{*}(\phi_{j})W,X][WZ(\phi_{j}),Y]

and

(5.26)

{\mathcal{K}}_{1}^{\dagger}(XY)-{\mathcal{K}}_{1}^{\dagger}(X)Y-X{\mathcal{K}}_{1}^{\dagger}(Y)=-2\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}[Z(\phi_{j})W,X][WZ^{*}(\phi_{j}),Y]\ .

To carry out an inductive proof of Theorem
5.1
, it is convenient to rewrite the
carré du champ
operators in (
5.25
) and (
5.26
) in terms of a differential calculus of Gross, Shale and Stinespring. The differential operators specified in the next definition were introduced in
[
62
, Section 3]
in connection with what Shale and Stinespring called the
holomorphic spinor representation
of the CAR; it is this representation that corresponds to the GIG quantum states that they studied. The explicit formulas in the definition are due to Gross
[
36
]
, as is
Lemma
5.9
and (in an equivalent form) Lemma
5.10
below. (Lemma
5.10
says that these differentiation operators do not raise the degree of polynomials.) Gross
[
36
]
utilized this differential calculus to study certain GIG semigroups, and we now give a further demonstration of its utility in this same context.

5.8 DEFINITION
.

For all
\phi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, define the operators
\partial_{\phi}
and
\bar{\partial}_{\phi}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
by

\partial_{\phi}(X):=Z(\phi)Y-\Gamma(X)Z(\phi)\quad{\rm and}\quad\bar{\partial}_{\phi}(X):=Z^{*}(\phi_{j})X-\Gamma(X)Z^{*}(\phi_{j})\ .

where
\Gamma
is the principle automorphism; that is,
\Gamma(X)=WXW
.

5.9 LEMMA
.

The operators
\partial_{\phi}
and
\bar{\partial}_{\phi}
are skew-derivations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. That is, for all
XY\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(5.27)

\partial_{\phi}(XY)=\partial_{\phi}(X)Y+\Gamma(X)\partial_{\phi}(Y)

and

(5.28)

\bar{\partial}_{\phi}(XY)=\bar{\partial}_{\phi}(X)Y+\Gamma(X)\bar{\partial}_{\phi}(Y)\ .

Proof.

Subtracting and adding
\Gamma(X)Z(\phi)Y
in the definition of
\partial_{\phi}(XY)
yields

\displaystyle\partial_{\phi}(XY)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Z(\phi)Y-\Gamma(X)Z(\phi)Y+\Gamma(X)Z(\phi)Y-\Gamma(XY)Z(\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl(Z(\phi)-\Gamma(X)Z(\phi)\bigr)Y+\Gamma(X)\bigl(Z(\phi)Y-\Gamma(Y)Z(\phi)\bigr)\ .

This proves (
5.28
), and the proof of (
5.28
) is essentially the same.
∎

5.10 LEMMA
.

For all
\phi,\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

(5.29)

\partial_{\phi}Z(\psi)=0\quad{\rm and}\quad\partial_{\phi}Z^{*}(\psi)=\langle\phi,\psi\rangle{\mathds{1}}\ .

and

(5.30)

\bar{\partial}_{\phi}Z^{*}(\psi)=0\quad{\rm and}\quad\bar{\partial}_{\phi}Z(\psi)=\langle\psi,\phi\rangle{\mathds{1}}\ .

Moreover, for
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
and
j=1,\dots,N
, define
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}_{j}
to be the same as
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, but with the
j^{{\rm th}}
entry set equal to
0
. Then

(5.31)

\partial_{\phi}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\langle\phi,\psi_{j}\rangle\beta_{j}(-1)^{\sum_{i<j}(\beta_{i}+\gamma_{i})}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}

and

(5.32)

\bar{\partial}_{\phi}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\gamma_{j}\langle\phi,\psi_{j}\rangle\gamma_{j}(-1)^{\beta_{j}+\sum_{i<j}(\beta_{i}+\gamma_{i})}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}_{j}}

Proof.

Since

Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}(Z^{*}(\psi_{j})^{\beta}_{j}Z(\psi_{j})^{\gamma}_{j}
is a product, we may apply the skew-derivation property from Lemma
5.9
. Using the evident identity
\partial_{\phi}{\mathds{1}}=0
and using the first part of (
5.29
) from Lemma
5.10
,
one obtains

\partial_{\phi}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Gamma\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j-1}(Z^{*}(\psi_{i})^{\beta}_{i}Z(\psi_{i})^{\gamma}_{i}\right)\left(\partial_{\phi}(Z^{*}(\psi_{j})^{\beta}_{j})Z(\psi_{j})^{\gamma}_{j}\right)\left(\prod_{k=j+1}^{N}(Z^{*}(\psi_{k})^{\beta}_{k}Z(\psi_{k})^{\gamma}_{k}\right)\ .

By the second part of (
5.29
) from Lemma
5.10
,
\partial_{\phi}(Z^{*}(\psi_{j})^{\beta}_{j})=\langle\phi,\psi_{j}\rangle\beta_{j}{\mathds{1}}
. Then since

\Gamma\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j-1}(Z^{*}(\psi_{i})^{\beta}_{i}Z(\psi_{i})^{\gamma}_{i}\right)=(-1)^{\sum_{i<j}(\beta_{i}+\gamma_{i})}\prod_{i=1}^{j-1}(Z^{*}(\psi_{i})^{\beta}_{i}Z(\psi_{i})^{\gamma}_{i}\ ,

this proves (
5.31
). The proof of (
5.32
) is essentially the same.
∎

5.11 Remark
.

Gross used the differential calculus to build a non-commutative theory of the Dirichlet forms associated to the generators of symmetric Markov semigroups in classical probability.
He proved
[
36
]
a logartithmic Sobolev inequality involving such a Dirichlet form associated to the Maximally symmetric Fermionic Mehler semigroup with parameter
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
, and conjectured that the sharp version has the same constants that he found. in the classical case
[
37
]
. His conjecture was later proved in
[
15
]
. The theory of non-commutative Dirichlet forms has been further developed in
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
. Moreover, the differential calculus defined in Definition
5.8
plays an important role in the theory of non-commutative optimal transport associated the the Fermionic Maehler semigroups
[
18
,
16
,
17
]
.

5.12 LEMMA
.

Let
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}
be given by (
5.19
). Then the corresponding carré du champ operator is given by

(5.33)

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(XY)-{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(X)Y-X{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Y)=\\
-2\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left((1-\lambda_{j})\bar{\partial}_{\phi_{j}}\Gamma(X)\partial_{\phi_{j}}Y+\lambda_{j}\partial_{\phi_{j}}\Gamma(X)\bar{\partial}_{\phi_{j}}Y\right)\ .

Proof.

Because
X\mapsto i[\widehat{K},X]
is a derivation on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, it does not contribute to the
carré du champ
.
Then since
X=\Gamma(\Gamma(X))
,

\displaystyle[Z^{*}(\phi_{j})W,X][WZ(\phi),Y]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Z^{*}(\phi_{j})\Gamma(X)-XZ^{*}(\phi_{j}))(Z(\phi_{j})Y-\Gamma(Y)Z(\phi_{j}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{\phi}\Gamma(X)\partial_{\phi}Y\ .

Likewise,
[Z(\phi_{j})W,X][WZ^{*}(\phi_{j}),Y]=\partial_{\phi}\Gamma(X)\bar{\partial}_{\phi}Y
. Combining these computations with (
5.25
) and (
5.26
) yields (
5.33
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

we proceed by induction on
|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|
, recalling at the outset that the identity has been proved for

|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|\leq 2
