. Let
n
be an integer with
2\leq n<2N
.
We suppose that
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}={\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{G,A}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}
has been proved whenever
|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|\leq n
. Let
({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
be such that

|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|=n+1
. We must prove
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}={\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{GA}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}
for this choice of

({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
.

Suppose for some
j
,
|\beta_{j}|+|\gamma_{j}|=1
. Define
j_{0}
to be the least such
j
, so that for
j<j_{0}
,
|\beta_{j}|+|\gamma_{j}|\in\{0,2\}
. Either
\beta_{j_{0}}=1
and
\gamma_{j_{0}}=0
or

\beta_{j_{0}}=0
and
\gamma_{j_{0}}=1
. We shall suppose that

\beta_{j_{0}}=1
; the other case is similar. Then with
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}}
defined as in Lemma
5.10
,

(5.34)

Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}=Z(\psi_{j_{0}})^{*}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\ .

because in commuting the factor of
Z^{*}(\psi_{j_{0}})
through to the left, it moves past an even number of terms with which it anti-commutes. Then because
\bar{\partial}_{\phi_{k}}Z^{*}(\psi_{j_{0}})=0

for all
k
by Lemma
5.10
, Lemma
5.12
yields

(5.35)

\displaystyle{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Z^{*}(\psi_{j_{0}}))Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}+Z^{*}(\psi_{j_{0}}){\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\lambda_{k}\partial_{\phi_{k}}(Z^{*}(\psi_{j_{0}}))\bar{\partial}_{\phi_{k}}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})\ .

By Lemma
5.10
,
\partial_{\phi_{k}}(Z^{*}(\psi_{j_{0}}))=\langle\psi_{j_{0}},\phi_{k}\rangle{\mathds{1}}
, and

(5.36)

\bar{\partial}_{\phi_{k}}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\gamma_{\ell}\langle\phi_{k},\psi_{\ell}\rangle\gamma_{\ell}(-1)^{\beta_{\ell}+\sum_{i<\ell}(\beta_{i}+\gamma_{i})}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}_{\ell}}\ .

Therefore since

(5.37)

\gamma_{\ell}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\lambda_{k}\langle\psi_{j_{0}},\phi_{k}\rangle\langle\phi_{k},\psi_{\ell}\rangle=\gamma_{\ell}\langle\psi_{j_{0}},A\psi_{\ell}\rangle=\gamma_{\ell}\delta_{j_{0},\ell}\langle\psi_{j_{0}},A\psi_{j_{0}}\rangle\ ,

and since
\gamma_{j_{0}}=0
,
\gamma_{\ell}\delta_{j_{0},\ell}=0
for all
\ell
. The
carré du champ
term vanishes and

\displaystyle{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Z^{*}(\phi_{j_{0}}))Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}+Z^{*}(\psi_{j_{0}}){\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Z^{*}(G^{*}\phi_{j_{0}})Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}+Z^{*}(\psi_{j_{0}}){\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{G,A}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{G,A}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})

where in the second equality we have used (
5.21
) and the inductive hypothesis, and in the third equality we have used (
5.7
) and the fact that the third term in

F_{j_{0}}
as specified in
(
5.8
) is zero since
\beta_{j_{0}}+\gamma_{j_{0}}=1
. The case in which i
\gamma_{j_{0}}=1
instead of
\beta_{j_{0}}
is essentially the same.

Next, consider the case in which
|\beta_{j}|+|\gamma_{j}|\in\{0,2\}
for all
j
. The case in which
|\beta_{j}|+|\gamma_{j}|=0
for all
j
is trivial. Therefore, fix
j_{0}
to be
the least value of
j
such that

|\beta_{j_{0}}|+|\gamma_{j_{0}}|=2
. Then with
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}}
as above, we again have the factorization (
5.34
), and the identities (
5.35
),
(
5.36
) and (
5.37
). The difference now is that
\gamma_{j_{0}}=1
, and hence the
carré du champ
term is
\langle\psi_{j_{0}},A\psi_{j_{0}}\rangle Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}}}
.
(Note that
(-1)^{\beta_{j_{0}}+\sum_{i<{j_{0}}}(\beta_{i}+\gamma_{i})}=1
by our choice of
j_{0}
). Therefore in this case,

\displaystyle{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Z^{*}(\phi_{j_{0}}))Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}+Z^{*}(\psi_{j_{0}}){\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})+\langle\psi_{j_{0}},A\psi_{j_{0}}\rangle Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Z^{*}(G^{*}\phi_{j_{0}})Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}+Z^{*}(\psi_{j_{0}}){\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{G,A}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})+\langle\psi_{j_{0}},A\psi_{j_{0}}\rangle Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}_{j_{0}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}^{\dagger}_{G,A}(Z_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})

just as above, but now the
carré du champ
term provides the third term in
F_{j_{0}}
which is not zero when
\beta_{j_{0}}=\gamma_{j_{0}}=1
.
∎

In the next subsection, we investigate an important special case in which one can say much more about the semigroup
\{e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}}\}_{t\geq 0}

5.1.
The Fermionic Mehler semigroups

The dual gauge group operations
\widehat{\alpha}_{{\rm G},t}
arise through second quantizing the simplest unitary group on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, namely
\{e^{it}{\mathds{1}}\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
. We obtain a family of equally canonical GIG semigroups on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
starting with the contraction semigroup
\{e^{-t}{\mathds{1}}\}_{t\geq 0}
. The generator of this semigroup is
-{\mathds{1}}
, and hence the condition

0\leq A\leq-G-G^{*}
becomes
0\leq A\leq 2{\mathds{1}}
. Making the choice
A:=2\lambda{\mathds{1}}
,
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
, Theorem
5.1

yields a one parameter family of semigroups
\{e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{{\mathds{1}},2\lambda{\mathds{1}}}}\}_{t\geq 0}
of GIG quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.

Moreover, since in this case
e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}
is simply
2\lambda e^{-2s}{\mathds{1}}
.

R_{t}=\int-0^{t}e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s
reduces to
R_{t}=\lambda(1-e^{-2t}){\mathds{1}}
. Therefore, by Theorem
4.3
and Theorem
5.1
,
the restriction of
e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{{\mathds{1}},2\lambda{\mathds{1}}}}
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is the restriction of

\Phi_{e^{-t}{\mathds{1}},\lambda(1-e^{-2t}){\mathds{1}}}
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. In this case, as we shall see, we can dispense with the restrictions:
\{\Phi_{e^{-t}{\mathds{1}},\lambda(1-e^{-2t}){\mathds{1}}}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a GIG semigroup of quantum operations on all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, and not only restricted to the Araki-Wyss algebra
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. This is an immediate consequence of Theorem
5.15
below.

5.13 DEFINITION
.

Fix
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
, and for
t\geq 0
define

(5.38)

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{\lambda,t}:=e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{{\mathds{1}},2\lambda{\mathds{1}}}}

where
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{{\mathds{1}},2\lambda{\mathds{1}}}
is the Lindblad generator specified in Theorem
5.1
. Then
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{\lambda,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is the

maximally symmetric Fermionic Mehler semigroup with parameter
\lambda
.

The maximally symmetric Fermionic Mehler semigroups correspond exactly to the classical Mehler semigroups studied by Mehler in 1866
[
47
]
, as we shall see. However, it will be convenient to treat them as part of a somewhat more general class of semigroups that deserve to be called Fermionic Mehler semigroups. These are the semigroups generated by
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{G,A}
where
G
is self-adjoint, so that we write
G=-H
as in Theorem
5.1
, and where
[H,A]=0
.

5.14 DEFINITION
.

Fix
H\geq 0
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
(so that
-H
is a contraction semigroup generator, and an operator
A
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}

such that
0\leq A\leq 2H
and
[H,A]=0
.
For
t\geq 0
define

(5.39)

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,A),t}:=e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{-H,A}}\ .

Then
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{(H,A),t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is the

Fermionic Mehler semigroup with parameters
H
and
A
.

Note that
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{\lambda,t}
given by (
5.38
) is the same as
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{({\mathds{1}},\lambda{\mathds{1}}),t}
given by (
5.39
).

When
G=-H
with
H\geq 0
and
[H,A]=0
, the formula
R_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}s
becomes
R_{t}=\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2sH}{\rm d}s\right)A
. Then with

(5.40)

T=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}(2H+\epsilon{\mathds{1}})^{-1/2}A(2H+\epsilon{\mathds{1}})^{-1/2}

as in Theorem
5.1
,

(5.41)

R_{t}=({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2tH})T\ .

5.15 THEOREM
.

Let
H
and
A
be commuting operators on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that
0\leq A\leq 2H
and
[H,A]=0
. Then with

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,A),t}
given by (
5.39
) and
T
given by (
5.40
),

(5.42)

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,A),t}=\Phi_{e^{-tH},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2tH})T}\ ,

for all
t\geq 0
.
Moreover, for each
t\geq 0
,

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}_{(H,A),t}
is self adjoint with respect to the GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{T}
which is a steady state of the semigroup; that is

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}_{(H,A),t}\rho_{T}=\rho_{T}
for all
t
.

If
H>0
, so that
{\rm ker}(H)=0
, then
\rho_{T}
is the unique steady state, and and for all states
\rho
,
\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}\rho=\rho_{T}
.

5.16 Remark
.

The fact that
\{\Phi_{e^{-tH},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2tH})T}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup when
[H,T]=0
follows from a result of Evans (see
[
26
, (3.8)]
). However his
approach is quite different and does not involve the generator of this semigroup, which he did not compute.

5.17 Remark
.

Self-adjointness with respect to the GNS inner product induced by a steady state is a physically important property known in the physical literature as
detailed balance
.
It is closely connected with time-reversal and with the notion of thermodynamic equilibrium. See
[
4
,
7
,
24
,
29
,
30
,
43
,
46
]
.

Proof.

Temporarily define
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}:=\Phi_{e^{-tH},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2tH})T}
. We claim that
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup. By Corollary
5.2

e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{-H,A}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\Phi_{e^{-(t/n)H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2(t/n)H})T}\right)^{n}\ .

However when
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup,

(5.43)

\Phi_{e^{-tH},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2tH})T}=\left(\Phi_{e^{-(t/n)H},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2(t/n)H})T}\right)^{n}

for all
n
, and (
5.42
) follows trivially from this.

To prove that
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup, it is convenient to work with
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Let
s,t\geq 0
.
Then by Theorem
3.10
, and the definition (
5.42
), for all
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
,

(5.44)

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}_{s+t}H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=e^{-(s+t)\sum_{j=1}^{N}|\alpha_{j}|\lambda_{j}}H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{s}^{\dagger}(e^{-t\sum_{j=1}^{N}|\alpha_{j}|\lambda_{j}}H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{s}^{\dagger}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}^{\dagger}H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ .

By Theorem
3.10

\{H_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\>\ {\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\})^{N}\}
is a basis for
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and hence for all
s,t
,
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}_{s+t}={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}_{s}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}_{t}
.
This is all we need to establish (
5.43
), but the continuity that we require of semigroup, namely

\lim_{t\to 0}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}^{\dagger}={\mathds{1}}
, is also evident from the diagonalization of
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}^{\dagger}
provided by Theorem
3.10
.

By Theorem
3.10
,
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}_{(H,T),t}\rho_{T}=\rho_{T}
for all
t
. Also by Theorem
3.10
, the
1
-eigenspace of
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\dagger}_{(H,T),t}
is spanned by
{\mathds{1}}
,
and all other eigenvalues lie in
(0,1)
. Therefore, the
1
-eigenspace of
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}
is spanned by
\rho_{T}
, and all other eigenvalues of

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}
lie in
(0,1)
. Therefore, when
H>0
,
\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}(\rho)=\rho_{T}
for all states
\rho
.
∎

In the final part of Theorem
5.15
, the condition that
H>0
can be dispensed with using the factorization property proved in Theorem
3.7
. Let
{\mathcal{K}}
denote
{\rm ker}(H)
, and write
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}\oplus{\mathcal{K}}^{\perp}
. Then both
{\mathcal{K}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{\perp}
are invariant under
H
and
T
. By
Theorem
3.7
together with Theorem
5.15
, for all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{K}}
and all
Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{K}}^{\perp}}
,

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}(XY)={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}(X){\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}(Y)
,
and by Theorem
3.10
,
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}(X)=X
. That is,

(5.45)

{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}(XY)=X{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}(Y)

Thus we loose noting in restricting our attention to the action of
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{K}}^{\perp}}
, and by definition,
H>0
on
{\mathcal{K}}^{\perp}
. Suppose that
0<T<{\mathds{1}}
on
{\mathcal{K}}^{\perp}
. Then by Theorem
3.10
,
\rho_{T}
spans the null space of
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{-H,A}
, and
\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}(Y)=\tau(Y)\rho_{T}
.
Since
\tau(Y)X={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}},\tau}(XY)
,

(5.46)

\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}\rho={\mathbb{E}}_{{\rm ker}(H),\tau}(\rho_{0})\rho_{T}\ .

It follows that the density matrices
\rho
in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
satisfying
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{(H,T),t}\rho=\rho
for all
t
are precisely those of the form

(5.47)

\rho=\sigma\rho_{T}

where
\sigma
is any density matrix in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}
. (Since
\rho_{T}
is even,
\rho_{T}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}^{\prime}
). In particular, if
{\mathcal{K}}
is not trivial, there are non-Gaussian steady states.
The density matrix
\rho
in (
5.47
) is a GIG density matrix if and only if
\sigma
is a GIG density matrix in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}
. It follows that the GIG density matrix
\rho_{Q}
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is a steady sate density matrix if and only if

T\leq Q\leq T+P

where
P
is the orthogonal projection onto
{\mathcal{K}}
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. It is not hard to generalize these considerations to the case in which
0
or
1
or both are eigenvalues of the restriction of
T
to
{\mathcal{K}}^{\perp}
.

We close with some remarks on a further generalization of the Fermionic Mehler semigroups.
Given a contraction semigroup
\{e^{tG}\}_{t\geq 0}
, even when
G
is not self-adjoint, there is a natural one parameter family of “second quantizations” of it that we have already encountered in the case
G=-{\mathds{1}}
.
For
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
, define

A_{\lambda}=\lambda(-G+G^{*})\ ,

and then

R_{\lambda,t}:=\int_{0}^{t}e^{sG}A_{\lambda}e^{tG^{*}}{\rm d}s=\lambda\left({\mathds{1}}-e^{tG}e^{tG^{*}}\right)\ .

Then defining
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}:=\Phi_{e^{tG},R_{\lambda,t}}\big|_{{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}}
, by Theorem
4.3
,
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, and such that
the symbol map of
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
is
Q\mapsto R_{\lambda,t}+e^{tG}Ae^{tG^{*}}
.
Taking
G=-{\mathds{1}}
, we obtain the maximally symmetric Fermionic Mehler semigroups. as another special case of Theorem
5.1
,
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
has an extension to all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. The results of the next section provide, among other things, another proof of the extension property that provides additional information in this special case.

5.2.
The cone of GIG semigroup generators

5.18 THEOREM
.

The set of GIG semigroup generators is a convex cone. That is, if
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}
and
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}
are GIG semigroup generators, then for all
a,b\geq 0
,

a{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}+b{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}
is a GIG semigroup generator.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the Trotter Product Formula.

e^{t(a{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}+b{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2})}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(e^{(ta/n)t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}}e^{(tb/n)t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}}\right)^{n}\ .

Then since for each
t
and
n
,
e^{(ta/n)t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}}
and
e^{(tb/n)t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}}
map
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself, so does
e^{t(a{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}+b{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2})}
.
∎

The Trotter Product Formula argument used to prove Theorem
5.18
allows us to to compute the symbol of
e^{t({\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}+{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2})}\rho_{R}
in a significant class of examples.

5.19 THEOREM
.

Let
T\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
, and let
H\geq 0
be an operator on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that
[H,T]=0
and
{\rm ker}(H)\subseteq{\rm ker}(T)
.
Let
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}^{\dagger}
be the generator of the Fermionic Mehler semigroup
\{\Phi^{\dagger}_{e^{-tH},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2tH})T}\}_{t\geq 0}
.
The explicit form of
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}^{\dagger}
, and hence
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}
, the generator of the dual GIG semigroup, is given by Theorem
5.1
. Let

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}={\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}^{\dagger}
be the coherent generator
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}(X)=i[\widehat{K},X]
for some self-adjoint
K
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.

Then the symbol map of the GIG semigroup
\{e^{t({\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}+{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2})}\}_{t\geq 0}
is given by

e^{-t(H+iK)}Qe^{-t(H-iK)}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-s(H+iK)}(HT+TH)e^{-s(H-iK)}{\rm d}s\

Proof.

For any
Q\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,

\displaystyle Q_{e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}}(\rho_{Q})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-tH}Qe^{-tH}+({\mathds{1}}-e^{-t2H})T

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-tH}(Qt(HT+TH))e^{-tH}+\mathcal{O}(t^{2})\ .

and

Q_{e^{t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}}(\rho_{R})}=e^{-itK}Qe^{itK}\ .

It follows that the symbol of

{\displaystyle\left(e^{(t/n)t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}}e^{(t/n)t{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}}\right)^{n}\rho_{Q}}

is

(e^{-i(t/n)K}e^{-(t/n)H})^{n}Q(e^{-(t/n)H}e^{i(t/n)K})^{n}\\
+\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\frac{t}{n}\left(e^{-i(t/n)K}e^{-(t/n)H}\right)^{m}(HT+TH)\left(e^{-(t/n)H}e^{i(t/n)K}\right)^{m}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)^{2}\ .

Taking the limit
n\to\infty
yields

Q_{e^{t({\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}+{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2})}\rho_{Q}}=e^{-t(H+iK)}Qe^{-t(H-iK)}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-s(H+iK)}Ae^{-s(H-iK)}{\rm d}s\ .

∎

5.20 THEOREM
.

Let

\left(G,A\right)
be a pair consisting of
a contraction semigroup generator
G
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
together with an operator
A
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
satisfying
0\leq A\leq-G-G^{*}
.
Define

(5.48)

H:=-\frac{1}{2}(G+G^{*})\quad{\rm and}\quad K:=\frac{1}{2i}(G-G^{*})\ ,

and define
R_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}e^{sG}Ae^{sG^{*}}{\rm d}S
, as in Theorem
4.3
. Let
T\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
be the unique solution of
HT+TH=A
with
{\rm ker}(H)\subseteq{\rm ker}(T)
.

By Theorem
4.3
there is a unique semigroup
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}
of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
that maps
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself such that for each
t\geq 0
, the symbol map of
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}
is
Q\mapsto R_{t}+e^{tG}Qe^{tG^{*}}
.

Under the condition that

(5.49)

[H,A]=0\quad{\rm or\ equivalently}\ ,\quad[H,T]=0\ .

\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
has an extension to all of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
which is given by
\{e^{t({\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}+{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2})}\}_{t\geq 0}

where
{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}
is the generator of the Fermonic Mehler semigroup
\{\Phi_{e^{-tH},({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2tH}T)}\}_{t\geq 0}
, and where

{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2}(X)=i[\widehat{K},X]
,
K=\frac{1}{2i}(G-G^{*})
.

Proof.

Define
T=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-tH}Ae^{-tH}{\rm d}t
. Then
HT+TH=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-tH}(HA+AH)e^{-tH}{\rm d}t=A
, and evidently this is the unique solution with
{\rm ker}(H)\subseteq{\rm ker}(T)
.
When
H
and
A
commute,
T=({\mathds{1}}-e^{-2tH})A
, and so
T\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
.

For
t\geq 0
, define
S_{t}:=e^{tG}=e^{-t(H+iK)}
so that
\{S_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a contraction semigroup on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and define

R_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}S_{s}(HT+TH)S^{*}_{s}{\rm d}s\ .

Note that
{\displaystyle S_{s}R_{t}S_{s}^{*}=\int_{0}^{t}S_{s+t}(HT+TH)S^{*}_{s+t}{\rm d}s=\int_{s}^{t+s}S_{r}(HT+TH)S_{r}^{*}{\rm d}r}
, and hence

(5.50)

R_{t+s}=S_{s}R_{t}S_{s}^{*}+R_{s}\quad{\rm and}\quad R_{t}\leq{\mathds{1}}-S_{t}S_{t}^{*}\ .

Then

A=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}R_{t}=HT+TH\ .

Then
[H,A]=H[H,T]+[H,T]H
and hence under the condition
{\rm ker}(H)\subseteq{\rm ker}(T)
,
[H,A]=0
if and only if
[H,T]=0
.

By Theorem
5.19
,
e^{t({\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}+{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2})}
has the symbol map
Q\mapsto R_{t}+S_{t}QS_{t}^{*}
, and therefore
the restriction of
e^{t({\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{1}+{\mathord{\mathscr{L}}}_{2})}
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
is the same as the restriction of
\Phi_{S_{t},R_{t}}
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.
∎

This result extends a result of Evans proved in the appendix to
[
26
]
. He proved the semigroup property for
\Phi_{S_{t},R_{t}}
when each
S_{t}
commutes with
T
, though the result is expressed somewhat differently and the argument is completely different. Of course, his condition amounts to
[G,T]=0
whereas we require only
[G+G^{*},T]=0
. However, it is not entirely clear that his extension is the same as ours when
T
commutes with
K
as well as
H
.

5.3.
Embedding in semigroups

Let
(S,R)
be any admissible pair with
S
invertible, which is a necessary condition for
\Phi_{S,R}
to be invertible. Every invertible square matrix has a logarithm,
and hence infinitely many logarithms. Thus it is always possible to write an invertible contraction
S
in the form
S=e^{G}
. However, it may be that no choice of
G

generates a contraction semigroup. For example, consider

L:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}-\log(2)&2\\
0&-\log(2)\end{array}\right]
. Then
e^{tL}=2^{-t}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&2t\\
0&1\end{array}\right]
.
Simple computation show that
S=e^{3L}
is a contraction. All solutions
G
of
e^{G}=S
are of the form
3L+k2\pi i
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, For such
G
,

\|e^{\frac{1}{3}G}\|=\|e^{L}\|=1+\sqrt{2}
, so that
\{e^{tG}\}_{t\geq 0}
is not a contraction semigroup for any choice of
G
.

The question that we take up now is this:

\bullet

Suppose that
S
is a contraction on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
that can be written as
S=e^{G}
where
G+G^{*}\leq 0
so that
G
is a contraction semigroup generator.
Then given
R
such that
0\leq R\leq{\mathds{1}}-SS^{*}
, when does there exist
a semigroup
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
such that
\Phi_{S,R}={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{1}
?

If we can find an operator
A
satisfying
0\leq A\leq-(G+G^{*})
such that

(5.51)

R=\int_{0}^{1}e^{tG}Ae^{tG^{*}}{\rm d}t\ ,

then defining
R_{t}
by (
4.13
) and then
{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}=\Phi_{e^{tG},R_{t}}
yields a semigroup
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of GIG quantum operations such that
\Phi_{S,R}:={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{1}
.

5.21 THEOREM
.

Let
(S,R)
be any admissible pair with
S=e^{G}
and
G+G^{*}\leq 0
. Then there is a semigroup
\{{\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of GIG quantum operations such that
\Phi_{S,R}:={\mathord{\mathscr{P}}}_{1}

if and only if

(5.52)

0\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}S^{n}(-GR-RG^{*})S^{*n}\leq{\mathds{1}}-SS^{*}\ .

If
G
, or equivalently
S
, commutes with
R
and is normal, then both inequalities in (
5.52
) are satisfied, but there exist compatible pairs
(S,R)
such that
S
is invertible, but
(
5.52
) is not satisfied, and thus such that
\Phi_{S,R}
cannot be embedded in a semigroup of GIG quantum operations.

Proof.

The equation (
5.51
) can be solved for
A
: Since
S=e^{G}
,

S^{n}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{tG}Ae^{tG^{*}}{\rm d}t\right)S^{*n}=\int_{n}^{n+1}e^{tG}Ae^{tG^{*}}{\rm d}t\ ,

and therefore

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}S^{n}RS^{*n}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{tG}Ae^{tG^{*}}{\rm d}t=(-\Lambda_{G})^{-1}(A)\ .

Therefore,

A=-\Lambda_{G}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}S^{n}RS^{*n}\right)=-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}S^{n}\Lambda_{G}(R)S^{*n}\ .

To embed
\Phi_{S,R}
in a GIG semigroup, we require
0\leq A\leq{\mathds{1}}-SS^{*}
.
Because
(S,R)
is compatible,
0\leq R\leq{\mathds{1}}-SS^{*}
. While
(-\Lambda_{G})^{-1}
is positivity preserving, it is not in general the case that
-\Lambda_{G}
is positivity preserving. However, if

R
commutes with
G
, or what is the same thing,
R
commutes with
S
and
S^{*}
,
-\Lambda_{G}(R)=R^{1/2}(-G-G^{*})R^{1/2}\geq 0
, so that in this case,
A\geq 0
.
Also in this case,

-\Lambda_{G}(R)=(-G-G^{*})^{1/2}R(-G-G^{*})^{1/2}\leq(-G-G^{*})^{1/2}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})(-G-G^{*})^{1/2}\ .

Define
B:=(-G-G^{*})^{1/2}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})(-G-G^{*})^{1/2}
. If furthermore
S
is normal,

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}S^{n}S^{*n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(SS^{*})^{n}=({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{-1}\ .

In this case also
G
is normal and
B
commutes with
G
and
G^{*}
. Therefore,

A\leq({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{-1}B=-G-G^{*}\ .

However, simple examples show that if
G
does not commute with
R
,
A
may not be positive. For example, take

G=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&\phantom{-}2\\
\phantom{-}0&-1\end{array}\right]
.
Since
G+G^{*}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}-2&\phantom{-}2\\
\phantom{-}2&-2\end{array}\right]
,
G
is a contraction semigroup generator. Then
S:=e^{G}=e^{-1}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&2\\
0&1\end{array}\right]
and

SS^{*}=e^{-2}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}5&2\\
2&1\end{array}\right]
. Choose
R:=\frac{1}{15}({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}8&7\\
7&8\end{array}\right]({\mathds{1}}-SS^{*})^{1/2}

so that
0\leq R\leq{\mathds{1}}-SS^{*}
is satisfied. The matrix powers are simple to compute;
S^{n}=e^{-n}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&2n\\
0&1\end{array}\right]
, and hence one can compute
A
and see that is has one strictly negative eigenvalue. This gives and example of a compatible pair
(S,R)
with
S=e^{G}
,
G
a contraction semigroup generator, such that
\Phi_{S,R}
cannot be embedded in GIG quantum semigroup.
∎

6.
Invariant states of quantum operations on
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}

We have already states left invariant by semigroups of quantum operations that map
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
into itself. While the main focus of this work is on semigroups, It is also of interest to
treat individual quantum operations.

6.1 THEOREM
.

Let
(S,R)
be a compatible pair. Suppose that
\lim_{n\to\infty}S^{n}=P
exists. Then
P
is a projection, but not necessarily orthogonal and
PRP^{*}=0
. Moreover:

(1) The sum

(6.1)

R_{\infty}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}S^{n}RS^{*n}

converges and satisfies
0\leq R_{\infty}\leq{\mathds{1}}-PQP^{*}
.

(2) A density matrix
\rho\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
satisfies
\Phi_{S,R}\rho=\rho
if and only if
\rho\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
and has a symbol of the form

(6.2)

Q=R_{\infty}+PQ^{\prime}P\quad{\rm for\ some}\quad Q^{\prime}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}\ .

Proof.

By definition, for any
Q^{\prime}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
, the symbol of
\Phi_{R,S}(\rho_{Q}^{\prime})
is
R+SQ^{\prime}S^{*}
. Iterating, the symbol of
\Phi^{n}_{R,S}(\rho_{Q}^{\prime})
is

(6.3)

\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}S^{m}RS^{*m}+S^{n}Q^{\prime}S^{*n}\ .

Because
\lim_{n\to\infty}S^{n}=P
(where
P
may be zero),
P^{2}=\lim_{n\to\infty}S^{2n}=P
.
Likewise,
SP=\lim_{n\to\infty}S^{n+1}=PS=P
. Taking adjoints we have altogether,

PS=P=SP\quad{\rm and}\quad P^{*}S^{*}=P^{*}=S^{*}P^{*}\ .

Since
0\leq R\leq{\mathds{1}}-SS^{*}
,
0\leq PRP^{*}\leq PP^{*}-PSS^{*}P^{*}=0
, so that
PRP^{*}=0
.
By (
6.3
),
R_{n}:=\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}S^{m}RS^{*m}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
for all
n
, and in particular, this sum of non-negative terms is bounded above by
{\mathds{1}}
for all
n
. It follows that
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}S^{m}RS^{*m}
converges to the an element
R_{\infty}
of
{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
. Since
(R_{n},S^{n})
is compatibles for all
n
, so is
(R_{\infty},P)=\lim_{n\to\infty}(R_{n},S^{n})
.

It follows that for any
Q^{\prime}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
, the symbol of
\Phi^{n}_{R,S}(\rho_{Q})
converges in the limit
n\to\infty
to
R_{\infty}+PQ^{\prime}P
. Therefore

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Phi_{S,R}^{n}(\rho_{Q^{\prime}})=\rho_{(R_{\infty}+PQ^{\prime}P)}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}\ .

Now suppose that the range of
P
is at least two dimensional. Let
\psi
and
\phi
be unit vectors such that
P\psi=\eta
and
P\phi=\xi
where
\{\eta,\xi\}
is linearly independent.
Define
Q_{0}=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
and
Q_{1}=|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|
.
Then

\rho_{R_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|}\quad{\rm and}\quad\rho_{R_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}|\xi\rangle\langle\xi|}

are GIG invariant states for
\Phi_{S,R}
. But then so any non trivial convex combination of these two states,
and by Wolfe’s Lemma, it does not belong to
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
.

However, if
P=0
, then for all
Q^{\prime}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\Phi^{n}_{S,R}(\rho_{Q^{\prime}})=\rho_{R_{\infty}}
, and then since
{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
spans
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Phi^{n}_{S,R}(\rho)=\rho_{R_{\infty}}
for all density matrices
rho\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{GIG}
. Thus,
\rho_{R_{\infty}}
is the unique invariant state of
\Phi_{S,R}
in
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.
suppose that
\eta
spans the range of
P
.

Finally suppose
P
is rank one, and that
\eta
spans the range of
P
. Define

t_{0}:=\min\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}\ :\ R_{\infty}+t|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}\}\quad{\rm and}\quad t_{1}:=\max\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}\ :\ R_{\infty}+t|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}\}\ .

Then define
Q_{0}:=R_{\infty}+t_{0}|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|
and
Q_{1}:=R_{\infty}+t_{1}|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|
.
For any
Q^{\prime}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Phi_{S,R}^{n}(\rho_{Q^{\prime}})=\rho_{R_{\infty}+s_{Q^{\prime}}|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|}

for some
s_{Q^{\prime}}
, and evidently
t_{0}\leq s_{Q^{\prime}}\leq t_{0}
so there is a
\lambda_{Q^{\prime}}\in[0,1]
such that

R_{\infty}+s_{Q^{\prime}}=(1-\lambda_{Q^{\prime}})Q_{0}+\lambda_{Q^{\prime}}\ .

Now let
\rho
be any density matrix in
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. By the Araki-Wyss Theorem, Theorem
1.8
, we may write

\rho=\sum_{n}s_{n}\rho_{Q^{\prime}_{n}}

where the
s_{n}
are real and
\sum_{n}s_{n}=1
. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Phi_{S,R}^{n}(\rho)=(1-s)\rho_{Q_{0}}+s\rho_{Q_{1}}

for some
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We claim
s\in[0,1]
. Evidently
(1-s)\rho_{Q_{0}}+s\rho_{Q_{1}}
is a density matrix, and its symbol is
(1-s)Q_{0}+sQ_{1}=R_{\infty}+s(t_{1}-t_{0})|\eta\rangle\langle\eta|
.
Since
t_{0}\leq 0
, if
s>1
,
s(t_{1}-t_{0})\geq t_{1}
which would meant that
1-s)Q_{0}+sQ_{1}\notin{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
, which is impossible. Since
t_{1}>t_{0}
, if
s<0
,
sQ_{1}\geq R_{\infty}
would not be satisfied, so this too is impossible. Therefore,
s\in[0,1]
and hence
(1-s)Q_{0}+sQ_{1}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
by Wolfe’s Lemma.

Q:=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}S^{m}RS^{*m}+PQ^{\prime}P^{*}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}

This proves
(1)
.
Note that for any choice of
Q^{\prime}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{Q}}}
,

R+SQS^{*}=R+\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}S^{m+1}RS^{*(m+1)}+SPQ^{\prime}S^{*}P^{*}=Q\ .

This shows that the set of symbols specified in (
6.2
) is precisely the set of fixed points of the symbol map of
\Phi_{S,R}
. Evidently, any state
\rho\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
with such a symbol is invariant under
\Phi_{S,R}
.
∎

Appendix A
Conditional expectations in the CAR

A.1.
Conditional expectations in general

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a
C^{*}
algebra, and let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a
C^{*}
sub-algebra of
\mathcal{M}
. Let
{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}
be any norm-contractive projection
from
\mathcal{M}
onto
{\mathcal{N}}
. By a theorem of Tomiyama
[
64
]
,
{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}
preserves positivity,
{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}{\mathds{1}}={\mathds{1}}
,
and

(A.1)

{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(AXB)=A{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(X)B\qquad{\rm for\ all}\quad A,B\in{\mathcal{N}},\ X\in\mathcal{M}\ .

Moreover, as Tomiyama noted, it follows from (
A.1
) and the positivity preserving
property of
{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}
that

(A.2)

{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(X)^{*}{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(X)\leq{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(X^{*}X)\qquad{\rm for\ all}\ X\in\mathcal{M}\ ,

In fact, more is true: every norm contractive projection is
completely positive
.

A
conditional expectation
from
\mathcal{M}
onto
{\mathcal{N}}
, in the sense of Umegaki
[
65
,
66
,
67
,
68
]
, is a unital projection
{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}
from
\mathcal{M}
onto
{\mathcal{N}}
that is order preserving and such that
(
A.1
) and (
A.2
) are satisfied. Notice that this definition does not make any reference to any state.

A.1 DEFINITION
.

A
conditional expectation
from
\mathcal{M}
onto
{\mathcal{N}}
is
compatible
with a state
\rho
on
\mathcal{M}
in case

(A.3)

\rho({\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(A))=\rho(A)

for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
.

We are interested in the nature of conditional expectations in t
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
for subalgebras
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
where
{\mathcal{H}}
is a subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.

Given a state
\rho
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and a non-trivial subspace
{\mathcal{H}}
of
{\mathord{\mathscr{H}}}_{1}
, define
\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}
to be the restriction of
\rho
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
. This state has the density matrix

(A.4)

\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho)

where
\rho
is the density matrix of the state
\rho
. The question arises as to whether there is any conditional expectation
{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}
that is compatible with
\rho
. The next lemma says that if a compatible conditional expectation exists, it is unique.

A.2 LEMMA
.

Let
\rho
be a state on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, and let
{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}
be a linear map from
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
such that for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,
\rho({\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(A))=\rho(A)
and

{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(XA)=X{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(A)
. Then
{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}
is the orthogonal projection of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
with respect to the GNS inner product induced by
\rho
.

Proof.

For
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
we compute, using the given properties of
{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}
,

\langle X,A\rangle_{GNS,\rho}=\rho(X^{*}A)=\rho({\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(X^{*}A))=\rho(X^{*}{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(A))=\langle X,{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(A)\rangle_{GNS,\rho}\ .

Therefore,
A-{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(A)
lies in the orthogonal complement of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
with respect to the GNS inner product induced by
\rho
, while
{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}(A)
lies in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

By a theorem of Takesaki, (proved in a general von Neumann algebra setting) a
\rho
-compatible conditional expectation exists
if and only if the modular automorphism group for
\rho
leaves
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
invariant. Leaving aside domain considerations that are anyway irrelevant in finite dimensions, for faithful
rho
, this is the same as

(A.5)

\Delta_{\rho}(X)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}\quad{\rm for\ all}\quad X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}\

where
\Delta_{\rho}
is the modular operator associated to
\rho
; i.e., in this finite dimensional setting,

(A.6)

\Delta_{\rho}(X)=\rho X\rho^{-1}\

where on the right side,
\rho
is the density matrix of the state
\rho
.

When a conditional expectation
{\mathord{\mathscr{E}}}
compatible with a state
\rho
does not exist, there is a substitute that is useful for many purposes; this is the
the
Accardi-Cecchini coarse-graining operator

[
2
,
3
]
.

Before introducing this operator, we introduce another inner product on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
associated to any state
\rho
regarded as a density matrix with respect to
\tau
. This is the
Kubo-Martin-Schwinger
(KMS) inner product

(A.7)

\langle A,B\rangle_{KMS,\rho}=\tau(\rho^{1/2}A^{*}\rho^{1/2}B)\ .

Though we formulate the following lemma in the context of the CAR, the proof is readily generalized.

A.3 LEMMA
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a non-trivial subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and let
\rho
be a state on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. Let
\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}
denote its restriction to
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
. Then for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, and all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,

(A.8)

|\langle A,X\rangle_{KMS,\rho}|\leq\|A\|_{KMS,\rho}\|X\|_{KMS,\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}}\ .

Proof.

This is a consequence of the Lieb Concavity Theorem
[
42
]
and the fact that
\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}
can be written as an average over unitary conjugations of
\rho
as in (
A.18
).
For all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
, and any unitary
G\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\langle X,X\rangle_{KMS,\rho}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(\rho^{1/2}X^{*}\rho^{1/2}X)=\tau(\rho^{1/2}(GXG^{*})^{*}\rho^{1/2}GXG^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau((G^{*}\rho G)^{1/2}X^{*}(G^{*}\rho G)^{1/2}X)\ .

By the Lieb Concavity Theorem and
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho):=\frac{1}{|{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}|}\sum_{G\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}G\rho G^{*}
,

\displaystyle\langle X,X\rangle_{KMS,\rho}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}|}\sum_{G\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}\tau(\rho^{1/2}(GXG^{*})^{*}\rho^{1/2}GXG^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{1/2}X^{*}\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{1/2}X)=\langle X,X\rangle_{KMS,\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}}\ .

Therefore,

\|X\|_{KMS,\rho}\leq\|X\|_{KMS,\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}}
. Then (
A.8
) follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
∎

By Lemma
A.3
, the map
X\mapsto\langle A,X\rangle_{KMS,\rho}
is a bounded linear functional on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
equipped with the KMS inner product induced by
\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}
.
Therefore, there is a uniquely determined
{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}(A)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
such
that for all
X\in\mathcal{M}
and all
Y\in{\mathcal{N}}
,

(A.9)

\langle A,X\rangle_{KMS,\rho}=\langle{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}(A),X\rangle_{KMS,\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}}\ .

A.4 DEFINITION
.

Let
\rho
be a state on
\mathcal{M}
. The

Accardi-Cecchini coarse graining operator

{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}
is the linear map from
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
is defined by (
A.9
).

The map
{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}
was introduced by Accardi and Cecchini
[
3
]
, building on previous work by Accardi
[
2
]
.

Since
{\mathds{1}}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,
for all
A\in\mathcal{M}
,

\langle{\mathds{1}},A\rangle_{KMS,\rho}=\langle{\mathds{1}},{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}(A)\rangle_{KMS,\rho}
, and for all

A\in\mathcal{M}
,
\langle{\mathds{1}},A\rangle_{KMS,\rho}={\rm Tr}[\rho^{1/2}{\mathds{1}}\rho^{1/2}A]=\rho(A)
. Therefore

(A.10)

\rho({\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}(A))=\rho(A)\ .

Thus, the Accardi-Cecchini coarse-graining operator preserves expectations
with respect to
\rho
.

In the matricial setting, it is a particularly simple matter to derive an explicit expression for
{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}
when
\rho_{\mathcal{H}}
is faithful.
By definition, for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and all
Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,

(A.11)

\tau(\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{1/2}Y\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{1/2}{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}(A))=\tau(\rho^{1/2}Y\rho^{1/2}A)\ .

Make the change of variables
Z=\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{1/2}Y\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{1/2}
.
Since
\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{1/2}
is invertible and
Y
ranges over
{\mathcal{N}}
,
Z
ranges over
{\mathcal{N}}
. Hence

(A.12)

{\rm Tr}[Z{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}(A)]={\rm Tr}[(\rho^{1/2}\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1/2}Z\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1/2}\rho^{1/2})A]={\rm Tr}[Z(\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1/2}\rho^{1/2}A\rho^{1/2}\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1/2})]\ .

Since the above holds for all
Z\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, it follows that

(A.13)

{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}(A)=\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1/2}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho^{1/2}A\rho^{1/2})\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1/2}\ .

It is evident from this formula that
{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}
is a completely positive
unital map from
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
to
\\
scA_{\mathcal{H}}
, and therefore it is actually a contraction from
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
to
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,
and hence a contraction.
By Tomiyama’s Theorem, it cannot in general be a projection of
{\mathord{\mathscr{H}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, so that if

X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, it is not necessarily the case that
{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}(X)=X
. The set of

X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
for which this is true turns out to be a sub-algebra of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, as was shown by
Accardi and Cecchini
[
3
]
.

A.5 DEFINITION
.

The
Petz recovery map

{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}
is the
Hilbert-Schmidt adjoint of
{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}
That is,
{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}={\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}^{\dagger}
, or equivalently, for all density matrices
\gamma\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}

{\rm Tr}[\gamma{\mathord{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rho}(X)]={\rm Tr}[{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}(\gamma)X]\ .

As the dual of a unital completely positive map,
{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}
is a CPTP map. Moreover,
it follows immediately from the definition and (
A.13
) that for all density matrices
\gamma\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,

(A.14)

{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}(\gamma)=\rho^{1/2}(\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1/2}\gamma\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1/2})\rho^{1/2}\ .

It is evident form this formula not only that
{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}
is a CPTP map, but that
{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}(\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}})=\rho
; i.e.,

{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}
recovers
\rho
from
\rho_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}
. Now suppose that
\sigma
is another density matrix in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and that

(A.15)

{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}(\sigma_{{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}})=\sigma\ .

Then by the Data Processing Inequality and (
A.15
),

S(\rho||\sigma)\leq S({\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}(\rho_{\mathcal{H}})||{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}(\sigma_{\mathcal{H}}))=S(\rho||\sigma)
.
Hence when
{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}(\sigma_{\mathcal{H}})=\sigma
, there is equality in the Data Processing Inequality. The deeper result of Petz
[
52
,
53
]
is that there is equality in Data Processing Inequality
only
in this case.

A.2.
Tracial conditional expectations

Define the
Majorana fields
as follows:

(A.16)

Q(\psi)=Z(\psi)+Z^{*}(\psi)\quad{\rm and}\quad P(\psi)=\frac{1}{i}(Z(\psi)-Z^{*}(\psi))

Simple computations using the CAR show that for all
\psi,\phi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

(A.17)

\{Q(\psi),P(\phi)\}=0\quad{\rm and}\quad\{Q(\psi),Q(\phi)\}=\{P(\psi),P(\phi)\}=2\Re(\langle\psi,\phi\rangle)\ .

It follows that if
\{\psi_{n+1},\dots,\psi_{N}\}
is any orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}
, then with
W=e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}{\mathcal{N}}}
,

\{W_{\mathcal{H}}Q(\psi_{1}),\dots,W_{\mathcal{H}}Q(\psi_{N}),W_{\mathcal{H}}P(\psi_{1}),\dots,W_{\mathcal{H}}P(\psi_{N})\}

is a set of
2N
anti-commuting self adjoint unitaries in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
. Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{H}}
be the smallest subgroup of the unitary group on
{\mathord{\mathscr{H}}}
that contains this set, which is a finite group with
2^{2N+1}
elements, and the complex linear span of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{H}}
is
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
.

Evidently, for any
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{H_{1}}
,
{\displaystyle\frac{1}{|{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{H}}|}\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{H}}}GAG^{*}}
commutes with every
G\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and hence with every
B\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\prime}
.
By von Neumann’s Double Commutant Theorem, this average then belongs to
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
.

A.6 DEFINITION
.

The
tracial conditional expectation
onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}
, is given by

(A.18)

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)=\frac{1}{|{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{H}}|}\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{H}}}GAG^{*}\ .

A.7 THEOREM
.

The super-operator
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}
has the following properties:

(1) For all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{H}}})
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and
\|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)\|\leq\|A\|
.

(2) For all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{H}}})
,

\tau({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A))=\tau(A)

(3) For all
A,B\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(AXB)=A{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(X)B

(4) For all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
and for all
B\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)=A\quad{\rm and}\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(B)=\tau(B){\mathds{1}}\ .

(5) For all
X,Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{H}}})
,

\tau(Y^{*}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(X))=\tau({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(Y)^{*}X)\ .

Finally,
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}
is the unique operator from
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
into
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
with properties (2) and (3). Moreover, for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, the conditional expectation
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)
is characterized as the unique element of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
such that

\tau(B{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A))=\tau(BA)

for all
B\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
, which means that
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}
is the orthongonal projection in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
with respect to the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{GNS,\tau}
.

Proof.

By construction, for any
G\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{H}}})
,
[G,{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)]=0
. Since
{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
spans
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime\prime}={\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
. Again by construction,

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)
is an average over unitary conjugations of
A
, and hence
\|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)\|\leq\|A\|
follows by Minkowski’s inequality. This proves
(1)
, and

(2)
is an immediate consequence of cyclicity of the trace.

For
(3)
, let
A,B\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
. Then for all
G\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,
G(AXB)G^{*}=A(GXG^{*})B
an therefore
(3)
follows from (
A.18
).

For
(4)
, if
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, then
GAG^{*}=A
for all
G\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and hence
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)=A
. Next, let
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
. If
A
is even; i.e.,
\Gamma(A)=A
, then
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
and hence

GAG^{*}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
for all
G\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
. Therefore,
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
. By part
(1)
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)\in\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
is spanned by
{\mathds{1}}
.
Thus for even
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)=c{\mathds{1}}
for some constant
c
, and then by
(2)
,
c=\tau(A)
. If
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
is odd,
W_{\mathcal{H}}A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
and
\tau(A)=0
. Arguing as before,

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(W_{\mathcal{H}}A)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\cap{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
. Therefore,
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(W_{\mathcal{H}}A)=c{\mathds{1}}
where
c=\tau(W_{\mathcal{H}}A)
. Since
W_{\mathcal{H}}
is even,
W_{\mathcal{H}}A
is odd, and hence
\tau(W_{\mathcal{H}}A)=0
.
Altogether, since
W_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}={\mathds{1}}
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)=W_{\mathcal{H}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(W_{\mathcal{H}}A)=0=\tau(A){\mathds{1}}\ .

Since every element of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
is the sum of its even and odd parts, this proves
(4)
.

By the cyclicity of the trace,
\tau(Y^{*}GXG^{*})=\tau(G^{*}Y^{*}GX)
and
\sum_{G\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}G^{*}Y^{*}G=\sum_{G\in{\mathord{\mathscr{G}}}_{\mathcal{H}}}GY^{*}G^{*}
so that
(5)
follows from (
A.18
).

Finally, let
\Phi:{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\to{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
be such that for all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,
\tau(\Phi(X))=\tau(X)
and such that for all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and all
X,Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
,
\Phi(AXB)=A\Phi(X)B
.
Since for all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(A.19)

X=\Phi(X)+(X-\Phi(X))\ .

By hypothesis, for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
,
\tau(A^{*}(X-\Phi(X))=\tau(A^{*}X)-\tau(\Phi(A^{*}X))=0\ .

Therefore, (
A.19
) is the decomposition of
A
into its components in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
({\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}})^{\perp}
with respect to the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{GNS,\tau}
. Hence any linear map
\Phi
with these properties must be the orthongonal projection onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
with respect to the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{GNS,\tau}
. In particular, there is only one such map.
∎

A.8 COROLLARY
.

For all subspaces
{\mathcal{H}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
and all
B\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
,

(A.20)

\tau(AB)=\tau(A)\tau(B)\ .

Proof.

by part
(5)
followed by part
(3)
and then part
(2)
of Theorem
A.7
,

\tau(AB)=\tau({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(AB))=\tau(A{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(B))=\tau(A\tau(B){\mathds{1}})=\tau(A)\tau(B)\ .

∎

The following simple extension of Corollary
A.8
will be useful here.

A.9 THEOREM
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Let
{\mathcal{K}}
be a subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}
, and let
\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}
be a subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}
. Let
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
and let

B\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
. Then

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}}\oplus\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}}(AB)={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(A){\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}}(B)\ .

Proof.

By the final part of Theorem
A.7
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}}\oplus\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}}(AB)
is the unique element of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{K}}\oplus\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}}
such that

\tau(Z{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}}\oplus\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}}(AB))=\tau(ZAB)

for all
Z\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{K}}\oplus\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}}
.
Since
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{K}}\oplus\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}}
is spanned by elements of the form
YX
,
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}
and
Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\widetilde{K}}
, it suffices to show that for all such
X,Y
,

\tau(YXAB)=\tau(YX{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(A){\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}}(B))\ .

By cyclicity of the trace and and Corollary
A.8
,

\displaystyle\tau(YX{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(A){\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}}(B))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(X{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(A){\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}}(B)Y)=\tau(X{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}(A))\tau(Y{\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}}(B))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(XA)\tau(YB)=\tau(YXAB)\ .

∎

A.10 LEMMA
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
be non-trivial subspaces of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Then

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\tau}={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\tau}\circ{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\tau}\circ{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\tau}\ .

Proof.

For all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\tau}(A)
is characterized by
\tau(B{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\tau})=\tau(BA)
for all
B\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}
, while

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)
is characterized by
\tau(C{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A))=\tau(CA)
for all
C\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
. Therefore, for all

B\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}
,

\tau(B{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\tau}(A))=\tau(BA)=\tau(B{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A))=\tau(B{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\tau}({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)))\ .

∎

A.3.
Conditional expectation with respect to
\rho\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}

Let
\rho_{Q}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{GIG}
have the symbol
Q
on the single particle space
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}
be non-trivial subspaces of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, that are orthogonal complements of one another, and such that both are invariant under
Q
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}={\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{K}}
where
{\mathcal{H}}
is invariant under
Q
(and hence so is
{\mathcal{K}}
).

Let
n
denote the dimension of
{\mathcal{H}}
.
when these conditions are satisfied, there is an orthonormal basis
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{N}\}
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}

consisting of eigenvectors of
Q
,
Q\varphi_{j}=\mu_{j}\varphi_{j}
, such that
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis for
{\mathcal{H}}
and
\{\varphi_{n+1},\dots,\varphi_{N}\}
is an orthonormal basis for
{\mathcal{K}}
.

Then with
Q_{\mathcal{H}}
and
Q_{\mathcal{K}}
denoting the restrictions of
Q
to
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}
respectively,

(A.21)

\rho_{Q}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}((1-\mu_{j})2Z(\varphi_{j})Z^{*}(\varphi_{j})+\mu_{j}2Z^{*}(\varphi_{j})Z(\varphi_{j}))

factors as the product of two commuting gauge invariant Gaussian density matrices

(A.22)

\rho_{Q}=\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{H}}}\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}

where
\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{H}}}
is the product of the first
n
factors in (
A.21
) and
\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{K}}}
is the product of the final
N-n
factors in (
A.21
).
Not only do
\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{H}}}
and
\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}
commute,
\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{H}}}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
and
\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
.

To apply Takesaki’s Theorem, we need an expression for the modular operator of a GIG state. Consider first any density matrix
\rho
on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
.
Let
\Psi
and
\Phi
be eigenvectors of
\rho
with eigenvalues
\kappa
and
\lambda
respectively. Then

\Delta_{\rho}(|\Psi\rangle\langle\Phi|)=\kappa\lambda^{-1}|\Psi\rangle\langle\Phi|\ .

Thus,
|\Psi\rangle\langle\Phi|
is an eigenvector of
\Delta_{\rho}
with eigenvalue
\lambda\mu
. It follows that
\Delta_{\rho}
is positive semidefinite on
{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{H}}})
, and that diagonalizing the modular operator
\Delta_{\rho}
is the same as diagonalizing
\rho
.

Let
Q
be the symbol of a gauge invariant Gaussian state
\rho
, amd suppose that
0<Q<{\mathds{1}}
. Let
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
consisting of eigenvectors of
Q
;
Q\psi_{j}=\mu_{j}\psi_{j}
.
Define
Z_{j}:=Z(\psi_{j})
. Then

(A.23)

\rho=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left((1-\mu_{j})2Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}Z_{j}^{*}+\mu_{j}2Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}\right)\ ,

and hence

(A.24)

\rho^{-1}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(\frac{1}{2}(1-\mu_{j})^{-1}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}Z_{j}^{*}+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{j}^{-1}Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}\right)\ ,

By the CAR,

(A.25)

\Delta_{\rho}(Z_{k})=\frac{1-\mu_{k}}{\mu_{k}}Z_{k}\quad{\rm and}\quad\Delta_{\rho}(Z^{*}_{k})=\frac{\mu_{k}}{1-\mu_{k}}Z^{*}_{k}\ .

Evidently,

(A.26)

\Delta_{\rho}(Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}Z_{j}^{*})=Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}Z_{j}^{*}\quad{\rm and}\quad\Delta_{\rho}(Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}})=Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}\ .

Return now to the case in which
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}={\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{K}}
where
{\mathcal{H}}
is invariant under
Q
so that
\rho_{Q}
has the factorization (
A.22
).
In this case, the modular operator
\Delta_{\rho_{Q}}
has a very simple action on
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
: For all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,

\Delta_{\rho_{Q}}(X)=\rho_{Q}X\rho_{Q}^{-1}=\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{H}}}}\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{K}}}X\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1}\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{K}}}^{-1}=\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{H}}}X\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\ .

Therefore, by Takesaki’s Theorem, there exists a conditional expectation from
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
that is compatible with
\rho_{Q}
.

A.11 DEFINITION
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a non-trivial subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
that is invariant under
Q
. Then the unique conditional expectation from
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
that is compatible with
\rho_{Q}
is denoted by

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\rho_{Q}}
.

It is easy to give an explicit formula for
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\rho_{Q}}
.

A.12 LEMMA
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a non-trivial subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
that is invariant under
Q
, and let
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}
. Let
\rho_{Q}=\rho_{\mathcal{H}}\rho_{\mathcal{K}}
be the factorization of
\rho_{Q}
given in (
A.22
). Then for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(A.27)

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\rho_{Q}}=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}A)=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}^{1/2}A\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}^{1/2})\ .

Proof.

Evidently, for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,
\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}A)\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and if
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,

\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}A)=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}})\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(A)=A\ .

Therefore,
A\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}A)
is a projection onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

The second equality in (
A.27
) is a consequence of the partial cyclicity of the partial trace, and since

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}
is completely positive, this shows that
A\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}A)
is completely positive.

Since
\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{K}}}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\prime}
, for any
X,Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
and any
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(A.28)

\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}XAY)=X\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}A)Y\ .

This completes the proof that
A\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}A)
is a conditional expectation.

To see that is is compatible with
\rho_{Q}
, note that for any
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

\rho_{Q}(A)=\tau(\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{H}}}\rho_{{\mathcal{K}}}A)=\tau(\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{H}}}\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{K}}}A))=\tau((\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{H}}}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{\mathcal{K}}}A))=\rho_{Q}(\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}A))\ .

∎

Since
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}={\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{K}}
, we may regard
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
as the graded tensor product of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
and
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}
, and then
thinking of
\rho_{Q\_{\mathcal{K}}}
now as a state instead of as a density matrix, we can also write the operator
A\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}},\tau}(\rho_{Q_{{\mathcal{K}}}}A)
as

A\mapsto{\mathds{1}}\otimes\rho_{Q\_{\mathcal{K}}}
.

The construction of
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\rho_{Q}}
depended on the compatibility of
{\mathcal{H}}
and
Q
. If the subspace
{\mathcal{H}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
were not invariant under
Q
, such a conditional expectation would not exist because then
\Delta_{\rho_{Q}}
would not leave
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
invariant. Of course, if
Q
is a multiple of the identity, then every subspace
{\mathcal{H}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
is invariant under
Q
, and we always have a compatible conditional expectation. The symbol
Q
of the tracial state
\tau
is an example; in this case
Q=\frac{1}{2}{\mathds{1}}
. The vacuum state
\omega_{0}
is another example; in this case
Q=0
. We now derive an alternate formula for

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\omega_{0}}
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a non-trivial subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and let

E_{\mathcal{H}}
denote the orthogonal projection onto
{\mathcal{H}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Let

E^{\perp}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
denote the complementary projection, which is
E_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
. Then

E_{\mathcal{H}}=\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-tE_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}\ .

Define

(A.29)

{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}:=\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-t\widehat{E_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}}\ .

Since
E_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
is self adjoint, so is
\widehat{E_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}
. Therefore,
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}
is self adjoint. Moreover, for all
t
,
\left(e^{-t\widehat{E_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}}\right)^{2}=e^{-2t\widehat{E_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}}
and hence
\left({\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{2}={\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}
. This proves that
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}
is an orthogonal projection on
{\mathord{\mathscr{H}}}
.

A.13 THEOREM
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a non-trivial subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and let
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}
be the orthogonal projection on
{\mathord{\mathscr{H}}}
defined by (
A.29
). Then
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\omega_{0}}
is given by

(A.30)

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\omega_{0}}(A)={\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}A{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}\ .

Proof.

Define
\Psi_{\mathcal{H}}:{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\to{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
by

(A.31)

\Psi_{\mathcal{H}}(A)={\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}A{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}\ .

Then
\Psi
is a manifestly completely positive, and to prove that is a conditional expectation, we need only show that for all
X,Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
and all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
,

(A.32)

\Psi(XAY)=X\Psi(A)Y\ .

Let
n:={\rm dim}({\mathcal{H}})
, and then since
{\mathcal{H}}
is a non-trivial subspace,
1\leq n\leq N-1
. Let
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that the first
n
vectors span
{\mathcal{H}}
. Let
Z:{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\to{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
be a CAR field over
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, so that with
Z_{j}:=Z(\varphi_{j})
,
j=1,\dots,N
,
\{Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N}\}
satisfy the CAR.
For
K\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
, define
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{K}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
by
({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{K})_{j}=1
if
j\in K
, and
({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{K})_{j}=0
if
j\notin K
. Let
\Phi_{K}
denote
\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{K}}
where
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is given by (
1.6
). Then
e^{-t\widehat{E_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}}\Phi_{K}=e^{-t|K\cap\{1,\dots,n\}|}\Phi_{K}
. Therefore,

{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}=\sum_{K\subset\{1,\dots,n\}}|\Phi_{K}\rangle\langle\Phi_{K}|\ .

Recall that for
L\subset\{1,\dots,N\}
we have defined

Z_{L}:=(Z_{1}^{*})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{L}_{1}}\cdots(Z_{N}^{*})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{L}_{N}}\ .

Then

{\displaystyle Z_{L}\Phi_{K}=\begin{cases}0&L\cap K^{c}\neq\emptyset\\
\pm\Phi_{K\cap L^{c}}&L\subset K\end{cases}}
and hence

{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}Z^{*}_{L^{\prime}}Z_{L}^{\phantom{*}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}=\begin{cases}Z^{*}_{L^{\prime}}Z_{L}^{\phantom{*}}&L,L^{\prime}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}\ .

Therefore,
\Psi_{\mathcal{H}}(A)={\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}A{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
for all
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
, and in case
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}A{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}=A
. Therefore,
\Psi_{\mathcal{H}}
is a projection in
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
. Moreover since
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}
itself is an orthogonal projection on
{\mathord{\mathscr{H}}}
, for all
X\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}X={\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}X{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}})={\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}X{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}=({\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}X{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}){\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}=X{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}\ .

In other words,
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
. Then for any
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
and any
X,Y\in{\mathord{\mathscr{B}}}({\mathord{\mathscr{H}}})
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}XAY{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}=X{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}A{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}}Y\ ,

so that (
A.32
) is satisfied, and
\Psi
is a conditional expectation.

To see that
\Psi
is compatible with
\omega_{0}
, note that
every
A\in{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
can be written as

A=\sum_{L,L^{\prime}\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}}a_{L,L^{\prime}}Z^{*}_{L^{\prime}}Z_{L}^{\phantom{*}}\ ,

and
\omega_{0}(A)=a_{\emptyset}
. By

\Psi_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=\sum_{L,L^{\prime}\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}}a_{L,L^{\prime}}Z^{*}_{L^{\prime}}Z_{L}^{\phantom{*}}\ ,

and hence
\omega_{0}(\Psi_{\mathcal{H}}(A))=a_{0}=\omega_{0}(A)
so that
\Psi
is a conditional expectation that is compatible with
\omega_{0}
. By the uniqueness of compatible conditional expectations,

\Psi={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\omega_{0}}
.
∎

A.14 COROLLARY
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}
be subspaces of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that
[E_{\mathcal{H}},E_{\mathcal{K}}]=0
where
E_{\mathcal{H}}
and
E_{\mathcal{K}}
are the orthogonal projections in
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
onto
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}
respectively.
Then
[{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\omega_{0}},{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}},\omega_{0}}]=0
, and hence

(A.33)

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\omega_{0}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}},\omega_{0}}={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}},\omega_{0}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\omega_{0}}={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{K}},\omega_{0}}\ .

Proof.

Since
E_{\mathcal{H}}
and
E_{\mathcal{K}}
commute on
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, so do
E_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}
and
E_{{\mathcal{K}}^{\perp}}
. Then by (
1.13
)

\widehat{E_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\perp}}}
and
\widehat{E_{{\mathcal{K}}^{\perp}}}
commute on
{\mathord{\mathscr{K}}}
. Then
[{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\omega_{0}},{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}},\omega_{0}}]=0
follows from (
A.29
) and Theorem
A.13
, and the
identity (
A.33
) follows directly from this.
∎

The vacuum state
\omega_{0}
and the tracial state
\tau
are very special gauge invariant Gaussian states, and to deal with conditional expectation with respect to general gauge invariant Gaussian states
\rho
, an orthonormal basis for
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
with respect to the
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{GNS,\rho}
inner product is useful on account of the connection between conditional expectations and GNS orthogonal projections established in Lemma
A.2
.

A.15 LEMMA
.

Let
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
consisting of eigenvectors of
Q
so that

Q=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mu_{j}|\varphi_{j}\rangle\langle\varphi_{j}|
. Suppose that
0<\mu_{j}<1
for each
j
. Then with
Z_{j}=Z(\varphi_{j})
,
define
\{K_{j,0,0},K_{j,1,0},K_{j,0,1},K_{j,1,1}\}\subset{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\rm span}(\varphi_{j})}
by

(A.34)

K_{j,0,0}:={\mathds{1}}\ ,\quad K_{j,1,0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\mu_{j}}}Z_{j}^{*}\quad{\rm and}\quad K_{j,0,1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu_{j}}}Z_{j}

and

(A.35)

K_{j,1,1}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu_{j}(1-\mu_{j})}}(Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}-\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}})\ .

Let
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{N})
denote a generic element of the index set
\{\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\}^{N}
,
and define the operator

(A.36)

K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:=K_{1,\alpha_{1}}K_{2,\alpha_{2}}\cdots K_{N,\alpha_{N}}

Then

(A.37)

\{K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ {\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\}^{N}\ \}

is an orthonormal basis for
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
with respect to the GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{Q}
. Moreover, each
K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is an eigenvector of
\Delta_{\rho_{Q}}
:

(A.38)

\Delta_{\rho_{Q}}(K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\left(\prod_{j\ :\ \alpha_{j}=(1,0)}\frac{\mu_{j}}{1-\mu_{j}}\right)\left(\prod_{j\ :\ \alpha_{j}=(0,1)}\frac{1-\mu_{j}}{\mu_{j}}\right)K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ .

Finally, let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
that is invariant under
Q
. We choose the orthonormal basis
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{N}\}
so that for some
J_{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}

\{\varphi_{j}\ :\ j\in J_{\mathcal{H}}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}
. Then
defining
|K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle\langle K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|
using the GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{Q}
,

(A.39)

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\rho_{Q}}=\sum_{\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ \alpha_{j}=(0,0)\ {\rm for}\ j\notin J_{\mathcal{H}}\}}|K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle\langle K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\ .

Proof.

The density matrix for
\rho_{Q}
is given by

\rho_{Q}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}((1-\mu_{j})2Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}Z_{j}^{*}+\mu_{j}2Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}})\ .

Since even and odd elements of of
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
are orthogonal in the GNS inner product for any even state
\rho
, and since for any state
\rho
,

\langle Z_{j}^{*},Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}\rangle_{\rho,GNS}=\tau(Z_{j}^{2}\rho)=0\ ,

\{{\mathds{1}},Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}},Z_{j}^{*}\}
is mutually orthogonal for the
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{Q}}
inner product.
One computes

\langle Z_{j}^{*},Z_{j}^{*}\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{Q}}=\tau(Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}Z_{j}^{*}\rho_{Q})=(1-\mu_{j})\quad{\rm and}\quad\langle Z_{j},Z_{j}\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{Q}}=\tau(Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}\rho_{Q})=\mu_{j}\ .

Therefore,
\{K_{j,0,0},K_{j,1,0},K_{j,0,1}\}
is an orthonormal with respect to the GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{Q}
.

Evidently,
\{K_{j,0,0},K_{j,1,0},K_{j,0,1},Z^{*}_{j}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}\}
spans
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\rm span}(\varphi_{j})}
, and we obtain an orthonormal basis by applying the Gram-Schmidt procedure, which is particularly simple since

Z^{*}_{j}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}
being even is orthogonal to
K_{j,1,0}
and
K_{j,0,1}
.
Evidently
Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}-\rho_{Q}(Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}})=Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}-\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}}
is orthogonal to
{\mathds{1}}
in the GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{Q}
, and

\rho_{Q}((Z_{j}^{*}Z_{j}^{\phantom{*}}-\mu_{j}{\mathds{1}})^{2})=\mu_{j}(1-\mu_{j})
.
This proves that
\{K_{j,0,0},K_{j,1,0},K_{j,0,1},K_{j,1,1}\}
is an orthonormal basis for
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\rm span}(\varphi_{j})}
with respect to the inner product induced by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{Q}}
.

Next, one readily checks that

\langle K_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{Q}}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\langle K_{\beta_{j}},K_{\alpha_{j}}\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{Q}}=\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\ .

Then (
A.38
) follows directly from (
A.25
) and (
A.26
).

Finally, its is evident that
\{K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ \alpha_{j}=0\quad{\rm for\ all}\ j>n\}
spans
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
, and hence is an orthonormal basis for it with respect to the
GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{Q}
. Then
(
A.39
) is true because
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\rho_{Q}}
is the orthogonal projection onto
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
in the GNS inner product induced by
\rho_{Q}
.
∎

A.16 COROLLARY
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}
be subspaces of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
that are both invariant under
Q
, and are such that

[E_{\mathcal{H}},E_{\mathcal{K}}]=0
where
E_{\mathcal{H}}
and
E_{\mathcal{K}}
are the orthogonal projections in
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
onto
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}
respectively.
Then
[{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\omega_{0}},{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}},\omega_{0}}]=0
, and hence

(A.40)

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\rho_{Q}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}},\rho_{Q}}={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}},\rho_{Q}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\rho_{Q}}={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{K}},\rho_{Q}}\ .

Proof.

We choose the orthonormal basis
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{N}\}
figuring in Thoerem
A.15
so that
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis for
{\mathcal{H}}
, and

\{\varphi_{m+1},\dots,\varphi_{N}\}
is an orthonormal basis for
{\mathcal{K}}
which is possible since
E_{\mathcal{H}}
and
E_{\mathcal{K}}
commute. (In this case
\{\varphi_{n},\dots,\varphi_{n}\}
is an
orthonormal basis for
{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}
which, to avoid trivialities, we assume to be non-zero.) Then by (
A.39
),

(A.41)

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\rho_{Q}}=\sum_{\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ \alpha_{j}=(0,0)\ {\rm for}\ j\notin J_{\mathcal{H}}\}}|K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle\langle K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\ ,

(A.42)

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}},\rho_{Q}}=\sum_{\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ \alpha_{j}=(0,0)\ {\rm for}\ j\notin J_{\mathcal{K}}\}}|K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle\langle K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|

and

(A.43)

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{K}},\rho_{Q}}=\sum_{\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ \alpha_{j}=(0,0)\ {\rm for}\ j\notin J_{{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}}\}}|K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle\langle K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\ .

The fact that
[{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}},\omega_{0}},{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{K}},\omega_{0}}]=0
is evident from (
A.41
) and (
A.42
), and (
A.40
)
is evident from (
A.41
), (
A.42
) and (
A.43
).
∎

It is worth remarking that the orthonormal basis
\{K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ {\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\}^{N}\ \}
is also orthogonal for the KMS inner product associated to
\rho_{Q}
:

\langle K_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle_{KMS,\rho_{Q}}=\tau(\rho_{Q}^{1/2}K_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\rho_{Q}^{1/2}K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\tau(\rho_{Q}K_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\rho_{Q}^{1/2}K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rho_{Q}^{-1/2})=\langle K_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\Delta^{1/2}_{\rho_{Q}}(K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\rangle_{GNS,\rho_{Q}}\ .

Therefore, by (
A.38
),
\{K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ :\ {\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\}^{N}\ \}
is a mutually orthogonal, but not necessarily normalized, basis for
{\mathord{\mathscr{A}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}
with respect to the KMS inner product induced by
\rho_{Q}
.
Again by (
A.38
),

\|K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\|^{2}_{KMS,\rho_{Q}}=\left(\prod_{j\ :\ \alpha_{j}=(1,0)}\frac{\mu_{j}}{1-\mu_{j}}\right)^{1/2}\left(\prod_{j\ :\ \alpha_{j}=(0,1)}\frac{1-\mu_{j}}{\mu_{j}}\right)^{1/2}\|K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\|^{2}_{GNS,\rho_{Q}}\ .

A preliminary version of this work was presented at GOA q-Day, March 20, 2026. I thank Magdalena Musat, Mikael Rørdam, and Dima Shlyakhtenko for stimulating discussions.
